is readily verified to be a functor. The functor
f^{-1}
has at the same time a left adjoint
f_{\exists}
and a right adjoint
f_{\forall}
. The left adjoint is given by

f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y,\quad f_{\exists}(\phi)=\phi\circ f^{*};

the right adjoint is given by

f_{\forall}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y,\quad f_{\forall}(\phi)=\phi\swarrow f_{*}.

In the vocabulary of category theory,
f_{\exists}
and
f_{\forall}
are called the left and the right Kan extension of
f
, respectively.

Dually, the functor

f^{-1}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X,\quad f^{-1}(\mu)=f^{*}\circ\mu

also has a left adjoint
f^{\dagger}_{\forall}
and a right adjoint
f^{\dagger}_{\exists}
. Before spelling out the left adjoint and the right adjoint, we would like to remind the reader that the underlying order of
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is opposite to the pointwise order of coweights. The left adjoint of
f^{-1}
is

f^{\dagger}_{\forall}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}Y,\quad f^{\dagger}_{\forall}(\psi)=f^{*}\searrow\psi;

the right adjoint is

f^{\dagger}_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}Y,\quad f^{\dagger}_{\exists}(\psi)=f_{*}\circ\psi.

Proposition 5.9
.

For each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
, we have

f_{\exists}\dashv f^{-1}\dashv f_{\forall}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y

and

f^{\dagger}_{\forall}\dashv f^{-1}\dashv f^{\dagger}_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}Y.

Proposition 5.10
.

Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a fully faithful functor.

(i)

For all
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
,
f^{-1}\circ f_{\exists}(\phi)=\phi=f^{-1}\circ f_{\forall}(\phi)
.

(ii)

For all
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
,
f^{-1}\circ f^{\dagger}_{\exists}(\psi)=\psi=f^{-1}\circ f^{\dagger}_{\forall}(\psi)
.

Proof.

(i) Since
f
is fully faithful,
f^{*}\circ f_{*}=X
, then for all
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
,

f^{-1}\circ f_{\exists}(\phi)=\phi\circ f^{*}\circ f_{*}=\phi

and

\displaystyle f^{-1}\circ f_{\forall}(\phi)

\displaystyle=(\phi\swarrow f_{*})\circ f_{*}

\displaystyle=(\phi\swarrow f_{*})\swarrow f^{*}

\displaystyle(f_{*}\dashv f^{*})

\displaystyle=\phi\swarrow(f^{*}\circ f_{*})

\displaystyle=\phi.

(ii) Similar. ∎

Assigning to each
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
the functor

\mathcal{P}f\coloneqq f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y

defines a functor

\mathcal{P}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},

called the
presheaf functor
.
1
1

1

The terminology comes from the fact that, in category theory, a contravariant functor from a category to the category of sets is called a presheaf of that category.
This functor plays a central role in the theory of real-enriched categories.
Dually, we have a
copresheaf functor

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}

that maps
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
to

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}f\coloneqq f_{\exists}^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}Y,\quad\psi\mapsto f_{*}\circ\psi.

For an element
a
of a real-enriched category
X
, write
{\sf y}(a)
for the weight

X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star,\quad x\mapsto X(x,a);

write
{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)
for the coweight

\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X,\quad x\mapsto X(a,x).

Weights of the form
{\sf y}(a)
and coweights of the form
{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)
are said to be
representable
.
As distributors, the representable coweight
{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
is left adjoint to the representable weight
{\sf y}(a)\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
.

The following lemma, a special case of the Yoneda lemma in the theory of enriched categories, implies that for each real-enriched category
X
, both of

{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X,\quad a\mapsto{\sf y}(a)

and

{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X,\quad a\mapsto{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)

are fully faithful functors.

Lemma 5.11

(Yoneda lemma)
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
a\in X
.

(i)

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
,
\mathcal{P}X({\sf y}(a),\phi)=\phi(a)
.

(ii)

For each
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
,
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\psi,{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a))=\psi(a)
.

Proof.

(i) Since

\phi(a)=\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with X(a,x)=\phi\circ{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)

and
{\sf y}(a)
is right adjoint to
{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)
, by Lemma
5.6
we have

\phi(a)=\phi\circ{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)=\phi\swarrow{\sf y}(a)=\mathcal{P}X({\sf y}(a),\phi).

(ii) Since
\psi(a)={\sf y}(a)\circ\psi
and
{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)
is left adjoint to
{\sf y}(a)
, by Lemma
5.6
we have

\displaystyle\psi(a)

\displaystyle={\sf y}(a)\circ\psi={\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)\searrow\psi=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\psi,{\sf y}^{\dagger}(a)).\qed

From the Yoneda lemma one infers that for each real-enriched category
X
, the functor
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is fully faithful, so it is called the
Yoneda embedding
.
Dually, the fully faithful functor
{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is called the
coYoneda embedding
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category.

(i)

({\sf y}_{X})_{\forall}={\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}
, i.e.
({\sf y}_{X})_{\forall}(\phi)=\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi)
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
.

(ii)

({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{\dagger}_{\forall}={\sf y}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
, i.e.
({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{\dagger}_{\forall}(\psi)=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\psi,-)
for all
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
.

Proof.

We prove (i) for example. For all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}X
,

\displaystyle{\sf y}_{\forall}(\phi)(\gamma)

\displaystyle=(\phi\swarrow{\sf y}_{*})(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\mathcal{P}X({\sf y}(x),\gamma)\rightarrow\phi(x))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\gamma(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\gamma,\phi).\qed

Yoneda embeddings constitute a natural transformation

{\sf y}\colon{\rm id}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}

from the identity functor to the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}
. Dually, coYoneda embeddings constitute a natural transformation

{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon{\rm id}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}

from the identity functor to the copresheaf functor
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}
.

There exist natural connections between
\mathcal{P}X
and
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
. We present two examples here. The first is the Isbell adjunction.
For
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
and
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
, let

\ub\phi=X\swarrow\phi,\quad\lb\psi=\psi\searrow X.

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{X}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{X\swarrow\phi}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\psi\searrow X}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{X}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}

Proposition 5.13

(Isbell adjunction)
.

The functor
\ub\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is left adjoint to the functor
\lb\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
.

Proof.

Because

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\ub\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\psi\searrow(X\swarrow\phi)=(\psi\searrow X)\swarrow\phi=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,\lb\psi).\qed

The second is the distributor

\circ\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{P}X

that assigns to each pair
(\psi,\phi)\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\times\mathcal{P}X
the real number
\phi\circ\psi
.
A close look at this distributor in a special case may help us understand its role in the theory of real-enriched categories. Let
X
be a set, viewed as a discrete real-enriched category. Then,
\mathcal{P}X
is
([0,1]^{X},\sub_{X})
and
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is
([0,1]^{X},\sub_{X}^{\rm op})
. For each
\phi\in[0,1]^{X}
, the functor

\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}

obtained by fixing the second argument of
\circ\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{P}X
turns out to be the functor

\phi\circ-\colon([0,1]^{X},\sub_{X})\longrightarrow([0,1],\alpha_{L}),\quad\psi\mapsto\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with\psi(x).

Intuitively, the value
\phi\circ\psi=\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with\psi(x)

measures the truth degree that
\phi
and
\psi
have a common point.

Suppose
\phi
is a weight and
\psi
is a coweight of a real-enriched category
X
. For all
p\in[0,1]
, write
\psi\rightarrow p
for the weight
\star_{p}\swarrow\psi
and write
\phi\rightarrow p
for the coweight
\phi\searrow\star_{p}
of
X
, where
\star_{p}
denotes the distributor
\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
with value
p
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category.

(i)

For all
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
,
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
and
p\in[0,1]
,

\mathcal{P}X(\phi,\psi\rightarrow p)=\phi\circ\psi\rightarrow p
.

(ii)

For all
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
and
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
,
\phi\circ\psi=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}(\mathcal{P}X(\phi,\psi\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p)
.

(iii)

For all
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}X
,
\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}((\phi_{1}\circ(\phi_{2}\rightarrow p))\rightarrow p)
.

6.
Colimit and limit

A
colimit
of a weight
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
is an element
\colim\phi
of
X
such that for all
x\in X
,

X(\colim\phi,x)=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,{\sf y}(x)).

It is clear that each weight has, up to isomorphism, at most one colimit. So we shall speak of
the
colimit of a weight.

Since

\mathcal{P}X(\phi,{\sf y}(x))={\sf y}_{X}(x)\swarrow\phi=(X\swarrow\phi)(x)

for all
x\in X
, it follows that
\phi
has a colimit if and only if the coweight
X\swarrow\phi

is representable. A colimit of
\phi
is then a representation of the coweight
X\swarrow\phi
, i.e.
X(\colim\phi,-)=X\swarrow\phi
.

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{X}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{X\swarrow\phi}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\geq}

For each functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
and each weight
\phi
of
K
, the colimit of
\phi\circ f^{*}
, when exists, is called the
colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
. In particular,
\colim\phi
is the colimit of the identity functor
X\longrightarrow X
weighted by
\phi
.

We write
\colim_{\phi}f
for the colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
. By definition,
b
is a colimit of
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
weighted by
\phi\colon K{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
if and only if for all
x\in X
,

X(b,x)=\mathcal{P}X(\phi\circ f^{*},X(-,x))=\mathcal{P}K(\phi,X(f(-),x)).

Example 6.1
.

Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
is a functor,
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
. Then for each weight
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
, the colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
exists and is equal to the composite
\phi\circ f^{\sharp}
, i.e.
\colim_{\phi}f=\phi\circ f^{\sharp}
, where
f^{\sharp}\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
is the distributor corresponding to
f
. To see this we calculate: for all
r\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}{\sf V}(\phi\circ f^{*},{\sf V}(-,r))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,{\sf V}(f(-),r))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\rightarrow(f(x)\rightarrow r))

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with f(x)\Big)\rightarrow r

\displaystyle={\sf V}(\phi\circ f^{\sharp},r),

hence
\phi\circ f^{\sharp}
is a colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
.

Definition 6.2
.

A real-enriched category
X
is cocomplete if every weight of
X
has a colimit.

It is readily seen that
X
is cocomplete if and only if all weighted colimits of functors to
X
exist; that means, for each functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
and each weight
\phi
of
K
, the colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
exists.

Proposition 6.3
.

A real-enriched category
X
is cocomplete if and only if the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
has a left adjoint.

Proof.

If
X
is cocomplete, the correspondence
\phi\mapsto\colim\phi
defines a left adjoint of
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
. Conversely, any left adjoint of
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
must send a weight
\phi
of
X
to a colimit of
\phi
. ∎

Example 6.4
.

Example
6.1
shows that
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is cocomplete. The left adjoint of
{\sf y}\colon{\sf V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}{\sf V}
is given by

\mathcal{P}{\sf V}\longrightarrow{\sf V},\quad\phi\mapsto\phi(1).

Or equivalently, for each weight
\phi
of
{\sf V}
,
\colim\phi=\phi(1)
.

In fact, since for all
z\in[0,1]
,
z=1\rightarrow z\leq\phi(z)\rightarrow\phi(1)
, then

\phi(1)=\sup_{z\in[0,1]}\phi(z)\with z=\colim\phi,

the second equality holds by Example
6.1
.

Example 6.5
.

For each real-enriched category
X
,
\mathcal{P}X
is cocomplete. For this consider the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
. Since

({\sf y}_{X})_{\exists}\dashv{\sf y}_{X}^{-1}\dashv({\sf y}_{X})_{\forall}

and
({\sf y}_{X})_{\forall}={\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}
(Proposition
5.9
and Proposition
5.12
), it follows that
{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}
has a left adjoint, so
\mathcal{P}X
is cocomplete.
For each weight
\Phi\colon\mathcal{P}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
of
\mathcal{P}X
, the colimit of
\Phi
is
{\sf y}_{X}^{-1}(\Phi)
, i.e.
\colim\Phi=\Phi\circ({\sf y}_{X})_{*}
.

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{({\sf y}_{X})_{*}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\colim\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\textstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}

Since
\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}=({\sf y}_{X})_{\exists}
,
{\sf y}_{X}^{-1}=\colim_{\mathcal{P}X}
and
({\sf y}_{X})_{\forall}={\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}
, we obtain a string of adjunctions

\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}\dashv{\colim}_{\mathcal{P}X}\dashv{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X.

Example 6.6
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the real-enriched category
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is cocomplete. We check that for each weight
\Phi
of
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
, the coweight
({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\swarrow\Phi
of
X
is a colimit of
\Phi
.

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
\textstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{\star}}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\colim\Phi}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\geq}

First we note that for all
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
,

\psi\searrow({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}=\inf_{x\in X}(\psi(x)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(-,{\sf y}^{\dagger}(x)))=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(-,\psi).

\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
\scriptstyle{\psi\searrow({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
\scriptstyle{({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\leq}

Thus for all
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\swarrow\Phi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\psi\searrow(({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\swarrow\Phi)

\displaystyle=(\psi\searrow({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*})\swarrow\Phi

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)(\Phi,\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(-,\psi)),

showing that
({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\swarrow\Phi
is a colimit of
\Phi
.

In particular, for each weight
\phi
of
X
, the colimit of the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
weighted by
\phi
is
X\swarrow\phi
, because

\displaystyle{\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace{\sf y}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\colim\phi\circ({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}

\displaystyle=({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\swarrow(\phi\circ({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*})

\displaystyle=(({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\swarrow({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*})\swarrow\phi

\displaystyle=(({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*}\circ({\sf y}^{\dagger})_{*})\swarrow\phi

\displaystyle(({\sf y}^{\dagger})_{*}\dashv({\sf y}^{\dagger})^{*})

\displaystyle=X\swarrow\phi.

\displaystyle(\text{${\sf y}^{\dagger}$ is fully faithful})

The dual notion of colimit is limit. Precisely, a
limit
of a coweight
\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
is an element
\lim\psi
in
X
such that for all
x\in X
,

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X({\sf y}^{\dagger}(x),\psi)=X(x,\lim\psi).

It is obvious that each coweight has at most one limit up to isomorphism.

Since

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X({\sf y}^{\dagger}(x),\psi)=\psi\searrow{\sf y}^{\dagger}(x)=(\psi\searrow X)(x),

it follows that
\psi
has a limit if and only if the weight
\psi\searrow X

is representable.

\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\psi\searrow X}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{X}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\leq}

Remark 6.7
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category and let
\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
be a coweight of
X
. Then,
a\in X
is a colimit of the weight
\psi^{\rm op}
of
X^{\rm op}
if and only if for all
x\in X
,

X^{\rm op}(a,x)=\mathcal{P}(X^{\rm op})(\psi^{\rm op},X^{\rm op}(-,x)).

Since

\mathcal{P}(X^{\rm op})(\psi^{\rm op},X^{\rm op}(-,x))=\inf_{z\in X}(\psi(z)\rightarrow X(x,z)),

it follows that
a
is a colimit of the weight
\psi^{\rm op}
of
X^{\rm op}
if and only if

X(x,a)=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X({\sf y}^{\dagger}(x),\psi).

Therefore, a limit of a coweight
\psi
of
X
is precisely a colimit of the weight
\psi^{\rm op}
of
X^{\rm op}
.

For each functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
and each coweight
\psi
of
K
, the limit of
f^{\dagger}_{\exists}(\psi)
, when exists, is called the
limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
. In particular,
\lim\psi
is the limit of the identity functor
X\longrightarrow X
weighted by
\psi
. We write
\lim_{\psi}f
for the limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
.

Example 6.8
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
f\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
is a functor. Then, for each coweight
\psi
of
X
, the limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
exists and is given by
{\lim}_{\psi}f=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(f^{\sharp},\psi)
, where
f^{\sharp}\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
is the distributor corresponding to
f
. In fact, for all
r\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf V}({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{\sf V}(r),f_{*}\circ\psi)

\displaystyle=\psi\searrow(f_{*}\searrow{\sf V}(r,-))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\psi(x)\rightarrow(r\rightarrow f(x)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(r\rightarrow(\psi(x)\rightarrow f(x)))

\displaystyle={\sf V}(r,\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(f^{\sharp},\psi)),

which shows
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(f^{\sharp},\psi)
is a limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
.

Definition 6.9
.

A real-enriched category
X
is complete if each coweight of
X
has a limit.

It is readily seen that
X
is complete if and only if for any functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
and any coweight
\psi
of
K
, the limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
exists.
Dual to Proposition
6.3
, we have the following:

Proposition 6.10
.

A real-enriched category
X
is complete if and only if the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
has a right adjoint.

Example 6.11
.

The real-enriched category
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is complete. For each coweight
\psi
of
{\sf V}
, by Example
6.8
,
\inf_{z\in[0,1]}(\psi(z)\rightarrow z)
is a limit of
\psi
.

Example 6.12
.

For each real-enriched category
X
,
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is complete. For this consider the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
. Since

({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{\dagger}_{\forall}\dashv({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{-1}\dashv({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{\dagger}_{\exists}

and
({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{\dagger}_{\forall}={\sf y}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
(Proposition
5.9
and Proposition
5.12
), it follows that
{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
has a right adjoint, so
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is complete.
For each coweight
\Psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
, the limit of
\Psi
is
({\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X})^{-1}(\Psi)
, i.e.
\lim\Psi=({\sf y}_{X}^{\dagger})^{*}\circ\Psi
.

\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\lim\Psi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{({\sf y}_{X}^{\dagger})^{*}}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}

Thus, we have a string of adjunctions

{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}\dashv{\lim}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}\dashv\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X.

Example 6.13
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the real-enriched category
\mathcal{P}X
is complete. For each coweight
\Psi
of
\mathcal{P}X
, the weight
\Psi\searrow{\sf y}_{*}
of
X
is a limit of
\Psi
.

\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\star}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\lim\Psi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf y}_{*}}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\leq}

In particular, for each coweight
\psi
of
X
, the limit of the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
\psi
is
\psi\searrow X
, i.e.
\lim_{\psi}{\sf y}=\psi\searrow X
.

Theorem 6.14
.

(Stubbe
[
76
]
)
A real-enriched category is complete if and only if it is cocomplete.

Proof.

Let
X
be a complete real-enriched category. We show that each weight
\phi
of
X
has a colimit, so
X
is cocomplete. Consider the coweight
X\swarrow\phi
of
X
. Since
X
is complete, there is some
a\in X
such that

X(-,a)=(X\swarrow\phi)\searrow X.

Then

X(a,-)=X\swarrow X(-,a)=X\swarrow((X\swarrow\phi)\searrow X)=X\swarrow\phi,

showing that
\phi
has a colimit.
Likewise for the converse implication. ∎

In the following we examine the relation between functors, colimits of weights and limits of coweights.
First of all, for any functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
and any weight
\phi
of
X
, if both
\colim\phi
and
\colim_{\phi}f
exist, then
{\colim}_{\phi}f\sqsubseteq f({\colim}\phi)
, because

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=X({\colim}\phi,{\colim}\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,X(-,{\colim}\phi))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{P}X(\phi,Y(f(-),f({\colim}\phi)))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,Y(-,f({\colim}\phi))\circ f_{*})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}Y(\phi\circ f^{*},Y(-,f({\colim}\phi))

\displaystyle(-\circ f^{*}\dashv-\circ f_{*})

\displaystyle=Y({\colim}_{\phi}f,f({\colim}\phi)).

Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a functor. We say that
f

preserves the colimit of a weight

\phi
of
X
, if
f(\colim\phi)
is a colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
, i.e.
f(\colim\phi)=\colim_{\phi}f
, whenever
\colim\phi
exists. Likewise,
f
preserves the limit of a coweight
\psi
of
X
if
f(\lim\psi)
is a limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
whenever
\lim\psi
exists.

We say that a functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y

preserves colimits
if it preserves all colimits that exist. Likewise,
f

preserves limits
if it preserves all limits that exist.

Theorem 6.15
.

Each left adjoint preserves colimits. Dually, each right adjoint preserves limits.

Proof.

Suppose that
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is left adjoint to
g\colon Y\longrightarrow X
. We show that
f
preserves colimits and
g
preserves limits.

Assume that
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
has a colimit. Since for all
y\in Y
,

\displaystyle Y(f(\colim\phi),y)

\displaystyle=X(\colim\phi,g(y))

\displaystyle(f\dashv g)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,X(-,g(y)))

\displaystyle(\text{definition of $\colim\phi$})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,Y(f(-),y))

\displaystyle(f\dashv g)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,Y(-,y)\circ f_{*})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}Y(\phi\circ f^{*},Y(-,y)),

\displaystyle(-\circ f^{*}\dashv-\circ f_{*})

it follows that
f(\colim\phi)
is a colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
.

By duality,
g
preserves limits. ∎

Theorem 6.16
.

If
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
preserves colimits and
X
is cocomplete, then
f
is a left adjoint.

Proof.

Since
X
is cocomplete, the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
has a left adjoint
\colim_{X}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow X
. Let
g
be the composite

Y\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.47423pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\sf y}_{Y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.09335pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.1867pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{P}Y\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.46886pt\raise 6.80057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.94289pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.88577pt\raise 6.975pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96945pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\colim}_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.98737pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.97473pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>X;

that is,
g(y)=\colim_{X}Y(f(-),y)
. We claim that
g
is a right adjoint of
f
.

By Theorem
4.5
, we need to check that for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
f(x)\sqsubseteq y
if and only if
x\sqsubseteq g(y)
.
If
f(x)\sqsubseteq y
, then

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq Y(f(x),y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(X(-,x),Y(f(-),y)))

\displaystyle(\text{Yoneda lemma})

\displaystyle\leq X({\colim}_{X}X(-,x),{\colim}_{X}Y(f(-),y)))

\displaystyle(\text{${\colim}_{X}$ is a functor})

\displaystyle=X(x,g(y)),

\displaystyle(\text{definition of $g$})

showing that
x\sqsubseteq g(y)
.

For the converse implication, first we show that
1\leq Y(f(g(y)),y)
for all
y\in Y
. Since
f
preserves colimits and
g(y)={\colim}_{X}(Y(-,y)\circ f_{*})
, then

f(g(y))={\colim}_{Y}(Y(-,y)\circ f_{*}\circ f^{*})\leq{\colim}_{Y}Y(-,y)\cong y,

so
1\leq Y(f(g(y)),y)
.

Now, suppose that
x\sqsubseteq g(y)
. Then

1\leq X(x,g(y))\leq Y(f(g(y)),y)\with Y(f(x),f(g(y)))\leq Y(f(x),y),

hence
f(x)\sqsubseteq y
.
∎

Proposition 6.17
.

The Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
preserves limits; the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
preserves colimits.

Proof.

For the first conclusion, we need to show that for each coweight
\psi
of
X
, if
\lim\psi
exists, then
{\sf y}(\lim\psi)
is the limit of
{\sf y}_{*}\circ\psi
in
\mathcal{P}X
, i.e.
{\sf y}(\lim\psi)={\lim}_{\mathcal{P}X}({\sf y}_{*}\circ\psi)
. To see this, we calculate:

\displaystyle\lim({\sf y}_{*}\circ\psi)

\displaystyle=({\sf y}_{*}\circ\psi)\searrow{\sf y}_{*}

\displaystyle(\text{Example \ref{inf in PX}})

\displaystyle=\psi\searrow({\sf y}_{*}\searrow{\sf y}_{*})

\displaystyle=\psi\searrow({\sf y}^{*}\circ{\sf y}_{*})

\displaystyle({\sf y}_{*}\dashv{\sf y}^{*})

\displaystyle=\psi\searrow X

\displaystyle(\text{${\sf y}$ is fully faithful})

\displaystyle=X(-,\lim\psi)

\displaystyle(\text{definition of $\lim\psi$})

\displaystyle={\sf y}(\lim\psi).

The second conclusion follows by duality. ∎

A functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is
colimit-dense
if for each
y\in Y
, there is a weight
\phi
of
X
such that
y
is a colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
. Likewise,
f
is
limit-dense
if for each
y\in Y
, there is a coweight
\psi
of
X
such that
y
is a limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
.

There is a nice characterization of colimit-dense and limit-dense functors.

Proposition 6.18
.

(Lai and Shen
[
46
]
)
Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a functor.

(i)

f
is colimit-dense if and only if
f_{*}\swarrow f_{*}=Y
.

(ii)

f
is limit-dense if and only if
f^{*}\searrow f^{*}=Y
.

Proof.

We prove (i), leaving (ii) to the reader. Assume that
f
is colimit-dense. We wish to show
f_{*}\swarrow f_{*}=Y
. It suffices to show that for each
y
of
Y
,
(f_{*}\swarrow f_{*})(y,-)\leq Y(y,-)
. Since
f
is colimit-dense,
y=\colim_{\phi}f
for some weight
\phi
of
X
. So,

Y(y,-)=Y\swarrow(\phi\circ f^{*})=(Y\swarrow f^{*})\swarrow\phi=f_{*}\swarrow\phi,

hence
\phi\leq f_{*}(-,y)
. Therefore,

(f_{*}\swarrow f_{*})(y,-)=f_{*}\swarrow f_{*}(-,y)\leq f_{*}\swarrow\phi=Y(y,-).

Conversely, suppose that
f_{*}\swarrow f_{*}=Y
. Then for each
y
of
Y
,

Y(y,-)=(f_{*}\swarrow f_{*})(y,-)=(Y\swarrow f^{*})\swarrow f_{*}(-,y)=Y\swarrow(f_{*}(-,y)\circ f^{*}),

showing that
y
is a colimit of
f
weighted by
f_{*}(-,y)
, hence
f
is colimit-dense. ∎

Proposition 6.19
.

For each real-enriched category, the Yoneda embedding is colimit-dense and the coYoneda embedding is limit-dense.

Proof.

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
, by Example
6.5
we have

{\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace{\sf y}=\colim\phi\circ{\sf y}^{*}=\phi\circ{\sf y}^{*}\circ{\sf y}_{*}=\phi,

the last equality holds by full faithfulness of
{\sf y}
, hence
{\sf y}
is colimit-dense. ∎

Theorem 6.20
.

(Kelly
[
39
]
)
For each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
Y
separated and cocomplete, there is a unique left adjoint
\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow Y
such that
f=\overline{f}\circ{\sf y}_{X}
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{f}
be the composite of left adjoints

\mathcal{P}X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.4874pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{\exists}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.09335pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.1867pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{P}Y\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49193pt\raise 7.22223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.72223pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\colim_{Y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.78748pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.57495pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Y,

where
\colim_{Y}\colon\mathcal{P}Y\longrightarrow Y
is the left adjoint of
{\sf y}_{Y}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y
. Then
\overline{f}
satisfies the requirement. This proves the existence.
For uniqueness, suppose
\overline{f}
satisfies the conditions. For each
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
, since
\phi
is the colimit of the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
\phi
and
\overline{f}
preserves colimits, then

\overline{f}(\phi)=\overline{f}({\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace{\sf y}_{X})={\colim}_{\phi}(\overline{f}\circ{\sf y}_{X})={\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace f,

showing that
\overline{f}
is unique.
∎

7.
Tensor and cotensor

Cocompleteness of a real-enriched category
X
requires that every weight of
X
has a colimit. The aim of this section is to show that in order that all weights of a real-enriched category
X
have colimits, it suffices to require certain subclass of weights of
X
have colimits.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category. For each
x\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
, the
tensor of
r
with
x
, denoted by
r\otimes x
, is an element of
X
such that for all
y\in X
,

X(r\otimes x,y)=r\rightarrow X(x,y).

Tensors are a special kind of colimits. Actually,
r\otimes x
is the colimit of the functor
s_{x}\colon\star\longrightarrow X
weighted by
r_{\star}
, where,
s_{x}
is the functor sending the unique object of
\star
to
x
, and
r_{\star}
is the weight of
\star
with value
r
. Said differently,

r\otimes x=\colim(r\with{\sf y}(x)).

For each
y\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
, the
cotensor of
r
with

y
, denoted by
r\multimap y
, is an element of
X
such that for all
x\in X
,

X(x,r\multimap y)=r\rightarrow X(x,y).

The cotensor
r\multimap y
is the limit of the functor
s_{y}\colon\star\longrightarrow X
weighted by
r_{\star}
. Said differently,

r\multimap y=\lim(r\with{\sf y}^{\dagger}(y)).

Definition 7.1
.

A real-enriched category
X
is tensored if the tensor
r\otimes x
exists for all
x\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
;
X
is cotensored if the cotensor
r\multimap y
exists for all
y\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
.

It is clear that
X
is tensored if and only if its opposite
X^{\rm op}
is cotensored. Every complete (hence cocomplete) real-enriched category is both tensored and cotensored.

If
X
is both tensored and cotensored, then for all
r\in[0,1]
and
x,y\in X
,

\displaystyle r\otimes x\sqsubseteq y~~\text{in}~~X_{0}

\displaystyle\iff r\leq X(x,y)

\displaystyle\iff x\sqsubseteq r\multimap y~~\text{in}~~X_{0}.

Therefore,

r\otimes-\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow X_{0}

is left adjoint to

r\multimap-\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow X_{0}.

This shows that in a both tensored and cotensored real-enriched category, tensors and cotensors are determined by each other.

Example 7.2
.

The real-enriched category
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is tensored and cotensored. For each
r\in[0,1]
and each
x\in V
,

r\otimes x=r\with x,\quad r\multimap x=r\rightarrow x.

The real-enriched category
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
is also tensored and cotensored. For each
r\in[0,1]
and each
y\in{\sf V}^{\rm op}
,

r\otimes y=r\rightarrow y,\quad r\multimap y=y\with r.

Example 7.3
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category. In
\mathcal{P}X
, the tensor and cotensor of
r\in[0,1]
with
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
are, respectively,
r\with\phi
and
r\rightarrow\phi
. In
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
, the tensor and cotensor of
r\in[0,1]
with
\psi\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
are
r\rightarrow\psi
and
r\with\psi
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{B}X
be the subset of
\mathcal{P}X
consisting of weights of the form
r\with{\sf y}(x)
; that is,

\mathcal{B}X=\{r\with{\sf y}(x)\mid x\in X,r\in[0,1]\}.

Since for each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
, each
x\in X
and each
r\in[0,1]
,
f_{\exists}(r\with{\sf y}_{X}(x))=r\with{\sf y}_{Y}(f(x))
, it follows that the assignment
X\mapsto\mathcal{B}X
defines a subfunctor of the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}
, through which the natural transformation
{\sf y}\colon{\rm id}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
factors.

Write
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{B}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
for the inclusion functor and
{\sf t}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}X
for the Yoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{B}X
. It is clear that
{\sf y}=\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf t}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is tensored.

(2)

The functor
{\sf t}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}X
has a left adjoint.

(3)

For each
x\in X
, the functor
X(x,-)\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
is a right adjoint, where
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
.

In this case,
X(x,-)\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
is right adjoint to
-\otimes x\colon{\sf V}\longrightarrow X
.

Proof.

(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
This follows from the fact that the tensor
r\otimes x
of
r
with
x
, if exists, is precisely the colimit of the weight
r\with{\sf y}(x)
.

(1)\Leftrightarrow(3)
A restatement of the equality
X(r\otimes x,y)=r\rightarrow X(x,y)
. ∎

Dual conclusions hold for cotensored real-enriched categories. We spell them out for later use. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}X
be the subset of
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
consisting of coweights of the form
r\with{\sf y}^{\dagger}(y)
,
y\in X,r\in[0,1]
.

Write
\mathfrak{i}^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
for the inclusion functor and
{\sf t}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}X
for the coYoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}X
.

Proposition 7.5
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is cotensored.

(2)

The map
{\sf t}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}X
has a right adjoint.

(3)

For each
y\in X
, the map
X(-,y)\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is a left adjoint, where
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
.

In this case,
X(-,y)\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is left adjoint to
-\multimap y\colon{\sf V}^{\rm op}\longrightarrow X
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a map between real-enriched categories.

(i)

If both
X
and
Y
are tensored, then
f
is a functor if and only if
f\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow Y_{0}
preserves order and
r\otimes f(x)\sqsubseteq f(r\otimes x)
for all
x\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
.

(ii)

If both
X
and
Y
are cotensored, then
f
is a functor if and only if
f\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow Y_{0}
is order-preserving and
f(r\multimap y)\sqsubseteq r\multimap f(y)
for all
y\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

We prove (i) for example.
Suppose
f
is a functor. First, it is trivial that
f\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow Y_{0}
preserves order. Next, let
r\in[0,1]
and
x\in X
. Since
X
is tensored and
1\leq X(r\otimes x,r\otimes x)
, it follows that
r\leq X(x,r\otimes x)
, then
r\leq Y(f(x),f(r\otimes x))
and consequently,
r\otimes f(x)\leq f(r\otimes x)
. This proves the necessity.

Now we prove the sufficiency. Let
x,y\in X
. Since
X(x,y)\leq X(x,y)
, it follows that
X(x,y)\otimes x\leq y
, then

X(x,y)\otimes f(x)\leq f(X(x,y)\otimes x)\leq f(y),

and consequently
X(x,y)\leq Y(f(x),f(y))
, so
f
is a functor. ∎

A weight
\phi
of
X
is
conical
if there is a functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
such that
\phi=\thinspace!_{*}\circ f^{*}
, where
!_{*}
is the graph of the unique functor
!\colon K\longrightarrow\star
. Dually, a coweight
\psi
of
X
is
conical
if there is a functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
such that
\psi=f_{*}\circ\thinspace!^{*}
, where
!^{*}
is the cograph the unique functor
!\colon K\longrightarrow\star
. It is easily verified that a weight
\phi
is conical if and only if

\phi=\sup_{a\in A}{\sf y}(a)

for a subset
A
of
X
; a coweight
\psi
is conical if and only if

\psi=\sup_{b\in B}{\sf y}^{\dagger}(b)

for a subset
B
of
X
.

Definition 7.7
.

(Kelly
[
39
]
, Stubbe
[
77
]
) Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category. We say that

(i)

X
is conically cocomplete if every conical weight of
X
has a colimit.

(ii)

X
is conically complete if every conical coweight of
X
has a limit.

(iii)

X
is order-complete if the underlying ordered set
X_{0}
is complete.

Theorem 7.8
.

(Kelly
[
39
]
, Stubbe
[
77
]
)
For each real-enriched category
X
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

X
is cocomplete.

(2)

X
is complete.

(3)

X
is tensored and conically cocomplete.

(4)

X
is cotensored and conically complete.

(5)

X
is order-complete, tensored and cotensored.

Proof.

By duality and Theorem
6.14
we only need to check that (1), (3) and (5) are equivalent.

(1)\Rightarrow(3)
Obvious.

(3)\Rightarrow(5)
First we show that
X
is order-complete. Suppose
A
is a subset of
X
. We show that each colimit of the conical weight
\phi=\sup_{x\in A}{\sf y}(x)
is a join of
A
in
X_{0}
. For all
x\in A
, we have

\displaystyle X(x,\colim\phi)

\displaystyle=X(\colim\phi,-)\searrow X(x,-)

\displaystyle(\text{Yoneda lemma})

\displaystyle=(X\swarrow\phi)\searrow X(x,-)

\displaystyle(\text{definition of $\colim\phi$})

\displaystyle\geq(X\swarrow X(-,x))\searrow X(x,-)

\displaystyle(x\in A)

\displaystyle=X(x,-)\searrow X(x,-)

\displaystyle=1,

which shows that
\colim\phi
is an upper bound of
A
in
X_{0}
. Suppose that
y
is another upper bound of
A
in
X_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle X(\colim\phi,y)

\displaystyle=X(-,y)\swarrow\phi

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in A}(X(-,y)\swarrow X(-,x))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in A}X(x,y)

\displaystyle=1,

hence
\colim\phi\sqsubseteq y
in
X_{0}
. Therefore,
\colim\phi
is a join of
A
in
X_{0}
.

Next we show that
X
is cotensored. For each
r\in[0,1]
and
y\in X
, let

A=\{a\in X\mid r\leq X(a,y)\}.

We show that the colimit of the conical weight
\phi=\sup_{a\in A}{\sf y}(a)
is the cotensor of
r
with
y
; that is,
X(x,\colim\phi)=r\rightarrow X(x,y)
for all
x\in X
. For all
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle s\leq r\rightarrow X(x,y)

\displaystyle\iff r\leq s\rightarrow X(x,y)

\displaystyle\iff r\leq X(s\otimes x,y)

\displaystyle\iff s\otimes x\sqsubseteq\colim\phi

\displaystyle(\text{$\colim\phi$ is a join of $A$ in $X_{0}$})

\displaystyle\iff s\leq X(x,\colim\phi),

hence
\colim\phi
is the cotensor of
r
with
y
.

(5)\Rightarrow(1)

For a weight
\phi
of
X
, let
a
be the join in
X_{0}
of the set
A=\{\phi(x)\otimes x\mid x\in X\}
. We show that
a
is a colimit of
\phi
, hence
X
is cocomplete.
For all
y\in X
, since
a
is a join of
A
in
X_{0}
, then

\displaystyle X(a,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{z\in A}X(z,y)

\displaystyle(\text{Proposition \ref{chara of cotensored}\thinspace(3)})

\displaystyle=\inf_{z\in A}(X(-,y)\swarrow X(-,z))

\displaystyle(\text{Yoneda lemma})

\displaystyle=X(-,y)\swarrow\sup_{z\in A}X(-,z)

\displaystyle=X(-,y)\swarrow\sup_{x\in X}X(-,\phi(x)\otimes x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(X(-,y)\swarrow X(-,\phi(x)\otimes x))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}X(\phi(x)\otimes x,y)

\displaystyle(\text{Yoneda lemma})

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\rightarrow X(x,y))

\displaystyle(\text{definition of tensor})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,{\sf y}(y)),

hence
a
is a colimit of
\phi
.
∎

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
X,Y
be cocomplete real-enriched categories. Then a functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a left adjoint if and only if

(i)

f\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow Y_{0}
preserves joins;

(ii)

f
preserves tensors in the sense that
f(r\otimes x)=r\otimes f(x)
for all
r\in[0,1]
and
x\in X
.

Proof.

Necessity is trivial since tensors are a special kind of colimits. For sufficiency, let
g\colon Y\longrightarrow X
be the map such that
g\colon Y_{0}\longrightarrow X_{0}
is right adjoint to
f\colon X_{0}\longrightarrow Y_{0}
. We claim that
g\colon Y\longrightarrow X
is a right adjoint of
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
. By Theorem
4.5
and Proposition
7.6
, we only need to check that
r\otimes g(y)\leq g(r\otimes y)
for all
r\in[0,1]
and
y\in Y
. Since

f(r\otimes g(y))=r\otimes f(g(y))\leq r\otimes y,

it follows that
r\otimes g(y)\leq g(r\otimes y)
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
X
be a complete real-enriched category. Then for each functor
f\colon K\longrightarrow X
and each weight
\phi
of
K
, the colimit of
f
weighted by
\phi
is given by the join of
\{\phi(z)\otimes f(z)\mid z\in K\}
in
X_{0}
. Dually, for each coweight
\psi
of
K
, the limit of
f
weighted by
\psi
is given by the meet of
\{\psi(z)\multimap f(z)\mid z\in K\}
in
X_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
\phi\circ f^{*}
is the join in
(\mathcal{P}X)_{0}
of the subset
\{\phi(z)\with X(-,f(z))\mid z\in K\},
the functor
\colim\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow X
preserves colimits, it follows that

{\colim}_{\phi}f=\colim\phi\circ f^{*}=\sup_{z\in K}\colim(\phi(z)\with X(-,f(z)))=\sup_{z\in K}\phi(z)\otimes f(z).

The second conclusion follows by duality. ∎

Corollary 7.11
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
f\colon K\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is a functor.

(i)

For each weight
\phi
of
K
,
\colim_{\phi}f=\sup_{z\in K}\phi(z)\with f(z)
.

(ii)

For each coweight
\psi
of
K
,
\lim_{\psi}f=\inf_{z\in K}(\psi(z)\rightarrow f(z))
.

Since
\mathcal{P}\star={\sf V}
, Example
6.1
and Example
6.8
are then special case of the above corollary.
The following conclusion, proved in Lai and Zhang
[
49
]
, extends the Tarski fixed point theorem, which says that every order-preserving map from a complete lattice to itself has a fixed point, to the enriched context.

Theorem 7.12
.

If
X
is a complete real-enriched category and
f\colon X\longrightarrow X
is a functor, then the subcategory

{\rm Fix}(f)=\{x\in X\mid f(x)\cong x\}

of
X
composed of fixed points of
f
is complete.

Proof.

First we show that the subcategory

Y=\{x\in X\mid x\sqsubseteq f(x)\}

of
X
is complete. Let
i\colon Y\longrightarrow X
denote the inclusion. Then for each weight
\phi
of
Y
, the join

a=\sup\{\phi(x)\otimes x\mid x\in Y\}

in
X_{0}
is a colimit of the functor
i
weighted by
\phi
. Since
f
is a functor,

f(a)\sqsupseteq\sup\{f(\phi(x)\otimes x)\mid x\in Y\}\sqsupseteq\sup\{\phi(x)\otimes f(x)\mid x\in Y\}\sqsupseteq a,

which shows that
a\in Y
. Therefore,
Y
is closed in
X
under formation of colimits. In particular,
Y
is cocomplete, hence complete.

Next, restricting the domain and codomain of
f
to
Y
we obtain a functor
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Y
. A similar argument shows that

Z=\{y\in Y\mid f(y)\sqsubseteq y\}

is closed in
Y
under formation of limits, hence a complete real-enriched category.
It is clear that
{\rm Fix}(f)=Z
, the conclusion thus follows. ∎

Definition 7.13
.

Suppose
X
is a partially ordered set. A (left) action of
[0,1]
on
X
is a map
\otimes\colon[0,1]\times X\longrightarrow X
subject to the following conditions: for all
x\in X
and
r,s,\in[0,1]
,

(i)

1\otimes x=x
;

(ii)

s\otimes(r\otimes x)=(s\with r)\otimes x
;

(iii)

r\otimes-\colon X\longrightarrow X
is a left adjoint;

(iv)

-\otimes x\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow X
is a left adjoint.

It is clear that
X
has a bottom element
\bot
and that
0\otimes x=\bot=x\otimes 0
for all
x\in X
. By (iii), the map
0\otimes-\colon X\longrightarrow X
has a right adjoint, which is denoted by
0\multimap-\colon X\longrightarrow X
. Then, for each
x\in X
,
0\multimap x
is a top element of
X
. So, the partially ordered set
X
is bounded, i.e., it has both a bottom element and a top element.

A
lax morphism

f\colon(X,\otimes)\longrightarrow(Y,\otimes)
between
[0,1]
-actions is an order-preserving map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
such that

r\otimes f(x)\leq f(r\otimes x)

for all
r\in[0,1]
and
x\in X
.

\textstyle{{[0,1]}\times Y}
\textstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{{[0,1]}\times X}
\textstyle{{[0,1]}\times Y}
\scriptstyle{1\times f}
\textstyle{{[0,1]}\times X}
\textstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\leq}

Let
X
be a separated, tensored and cotensored real-enriched category
X
. Assigning to each pair
(r,x)
the tensor
r\otimes x
defines a
[0,1]
-action on the partially ordered set
X_{0}
. Proposition
7.6
says that
X\mapsto(X_{0},\otimes)
is a functor from the full subcategory of
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
consisting of separated, tensored and cotensored real-enriched categories to the category of
[0,1]
-actions and lax morphisms. This functor is indeed an isomorphism of categories.

Proposition 7.14
.

Suppose
\otimes
is an action of
[0,1]
on a partially ordered set
(X,\leq)
. For all
x,y\in X
, let

\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y)=\sup\{r\in[0,1]\mid r\otimes x\leq y\}.

Then
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is a separated, tensored and cotensored real-enriched category with
\leq
being its underlying order.

Proof.

First of all, it is clear that for all
r\in[0,1]
and
x,y\in X
,

r\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y)\iff r\otimes x\leq y.

Since
1\otimes x=x
, then
\alpha_{\otimes}(x,x)\geq 1
for all
x\in X
. If
r\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y)
and
s\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(y,z)
, then

(s\with r)\otimes x=s\otimes(r\otimes x)\leq s\otimes y\leq z,

which shows that
s\with r\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(x,z)
, hence
\alpha_{\otimes}(y,z)\with\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y)\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(x,z)
and consequently,
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is a real-enriched category. It remains to show that
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is tensored, cotensored, separated, and has
\leq
as underlying order

For all
r,s\in[0,1]
and
x,y\in X
, since

\displaystyle s\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(r\otimes x,y)

\displaystyle\iff s\otimes(r\otimes x)\leq y

\displaystyle\iff s\with r\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y)

\displaystyle\iff s\leq r\rightarrow\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y),

then
r\otimes x
is a tensor of
r
with
x
in
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
, hence
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is tensored.

For each
r\in[0,1]
, let
r\multimap-\colon X\longrightarrow X
be the right adjoint of
r\otimes-\colon X\longrightarrow X
.
For all
x,y\in X
and all
s\in[0,1]
, since

\displaystyle s\leq X(x,r\multimap y)

\displaystyle\iff s\otimes x\leq r\multimap y

\displaystyle\iff r\otimes(s\otimes x)\leq y

\displaystyle\iff(r\with s)\otimes x\leq y

\displaystyle\iff r\with s\leq X(x,y)

\displaystyle\iff s\leq r\rightarrow X(x,y),

then

r\multimap y
is a cotensor of
r
with
y
in
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
, hence
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is cotensored.

Finally, since

1\leq\alpha_{\otimes}(x,y)\iff 1\otimes x\leq y\iff x\leq y,

it follows that
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is separated with
\leq
being its underlying order. ∎

Proposition 7.15
.

The category of
[0,1]
-actions and lax morphisms is isomorphic to the full subcategory of
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
consisting of separated, tensored and cotensored real-enriched categories.

8.
Cauchy completeness

A weight
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
of a real-enriched category
X
is
Cauchy
if it is a right adjoint, as a distributor. That means, there is a distributor
\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
such that
\phi\circ\psi\geq 1
and
\psi(y)\with\phi(x)\leq X(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a functor between real-enriched categories and let
\phi
be a weight of
X
. If
\phi
is a Cauchy weight, as a composite of right adjoints,
\phi\circ f^{*}
is a right adjoint, hence a Cauchy weight of
Y
. So, assigning to each real-enriched category
X
the subcategory
\mathcal{C}X
of
\mathcal{P}X
composed of Cauchy weights defines a subfunctor

\mathcal{C}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}

of the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}
. Since each representable weight is Cauchy, the natural transformation from the identity functor to
\mathcal{P}
with Yoneda embeddings as components factors through the functor
\mathcal{C}
.

The following conclusion is a special case of the characterization of Cauchy weights of enriched categories in Street
[
75
]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
\phi
is a weight of
X
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\phi
is Cauchy.

(2)

The colimit
\colim_{\phi}f
of any functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
weighted by
\phi
, when exists, is preserved by every functor
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
, i.e.
g(\colim_{\phi}f)=\colim_{\phi}(g\circ f)
.

In particular, every Cauchy weight with a colimit is representable.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Since
\colim_{\phi}f
, when exists, is the colimit of the Cauchy weight
\phi\circ f^{*}
of
Y
, it suffices to show that if
b
is a colimit of a Cauchy weight
\xi
of
Y
, then for every functor
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
,
g(b)
is a colimit of
g
weighted by
\xi
, i.e.
g(b)=\colim_{\xi}g
. By definition of colimit we have
Y\swarrow\xi=Y(b,-)
. Since
Y\swarrow\xi
is left adjoint to
\xi
by Lemma
5.6
, then

\xi=Y(b,-)\searrow Y=Y(-,b).

This shows in particular that
\xi
is representable. Since

\xi\circ g^{*}=Y(-,b)\circ g^{*}=Z(-,g(b)),

it follows that
g(b)
is a colimit of
\xi\circ g^{*}
, i.e.
g(b)=\colim_{\xi}g
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
We show that the coweight
\psi\coloneqq X\swarrow\phi
is left adjoint to
\phi
. Consider the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
and the functor

g\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow{\sf V},\quad\xi\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\psi,\xi),

where
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
. On the one hand, by Example
6.6
we have

{\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace{\sf y}^{\dagger}=X\swarrow\phi=\psi,

so
g(\colim_{\phi}\thinspace{\sf y}^{\dagger})=1
. On the other hand, since for each
x\in X
,

g\circ{\sf y}^{\dagger}(x)=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\psi,X(x,-))=\psi(x),

i.e.
g\circ{\sf y}^{\dagger}=\psi^{\sharp}\colon X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
, then by Example
6.1
we have

{\colim}_{\phi}(g\circ{\sf y}^{\dagger})={\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace\psi^{\sharp}=\phi\circ\psi.

Therefore,
\phi\circ\psi=1
. The inequality
\psi\circ\phi\leq X
is trivial, so
\psi
is left adjoint to
\phi
, as desired. ∎

The next characterization of Cauchy weights is a special case of that in Kelly and Schmitt
[
40
]
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
\phi
is a weight of
X
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\phi
is Cauchy.

(2)

The functor
\sub_{X}(\phi,-)\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves colimits.

(3)

The functor
\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves limits.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
If
\psi
is a left adjoint of
\phi
, then for all
\xi\in\mathcal{P}X
,

\sub_{X}(\phi,\xi)=\xi\swarrow\phi=\xi\circ\psi,

from which one deduces that
\sub_{X}(\phi,-)\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves colimits.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Assume that the functor
\sub_{X}(\phi,-)
preserves colimits. We show that
\psi\coloneqq X\swarrow\phi
is left adjoint to
\phi
, hence
\phi
is Cauchy. It suffices to check that
\phi\circ\psi\geq 1
. Since
\sub_{X}(\phi,-)
preserves colimits and

\phi={\colim}_{\phi}\thinspace{\sf y}=\sup_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\otimes{\sf y}(x)),

it follows that

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\with\sub_{X}(\phi,{\sf y}(x))

\displaystyle=\sub_{X}(\phi,\colim_{\phi}{\sf y})\quad\quad~(\text{$\sub_{X}(\phi,-)$ preserves colimits})

\displaystyle=\sub_{X}(\phi,\phi)

\displaystyle=1.

(1)\Rightarrow(3)
If
\psi
is a left adjoint of
\phi
, then for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
,

\phi\circ\lambda=\psi\searrow\lambda=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X(\lambda,\psi),

from which one infers that
\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves limits.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
Assume that the functor
\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves limits. We show that
\psi\coloneqq X\swarrow\phi
is left adjoint to
\phi
. It suffices to check that
\phi\circ\psi\geq 1
. Since

X\swarrow\phi=\inf_{y\in X}(\phi(y)\rightarrow X(y,-))

and
\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves limits, it follows that

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi

\displaystyle=\inf_{y\in X}(\phi(y)\rightarrow\phi\circ X(y,-))=\inf_{y\in X}(\phi(y)\rightarrow\phi(y))=1.

The proof is completed. ∎

Definition 8.3
.

A real-enriched category
X
is Cauchy complete if it is separated and all of its Cauchy weights are representable.

In other words,
X
is Cauchy complete if each of its Cauchy weight has a unique colimit. Suppose
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
is a Cauchy weight of
X
, and
\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
is a left adjoint of
\phi
. Then
\psi^{\rm op}\colon X^{\rm op}{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
is a Cauchy weight of
X^{\rm op}
. Since all Cauchy weights of
X^{\rm op}
arise in this way, it follows that the notion of Cauchy completeness is self-dual; that is, if
X
is Cauchy complete, then so is its opposite
X^{\rm op}
.

Each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
generates an adjunction of distributors
f_{*}\dashv f^{*}
. The following proposition shows that when
Y
is Cauchy complete, every such adjunction arises in this way.

Proposition 8.4
.

(Lawvere
[
54
]
)
Suppose
Y
is a real-enriched category. The following are equivalent:

(1)

Y
is Cauchy complete.

(2)

For any pair of distributors
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
and
\psi\colon Y{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
, if
\phi
is left adjoint to
\psi
, then there is a unique functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
such that
\phi=f_{*}
and
\psi=f^{*}
.

Proof.

It suffices to observe that if
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
is left adjoint to
\psi\colon Y{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
, then for each
x\in X
, the distributor
\phi(x,-)\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
is left adjoint to
\psi(-,x)\colon Y{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
. ∎

For a real-enriched category
X
, let
\mathcal{C}X
be the subcategory of
\mathcal{P}X
composed of Cauchy weights. Let
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{C}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
be the inclusion functor, and let
{\sf c}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}X
be the Yoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{C}X
. The composite
\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf c}
is then the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
.

Lemma 8.5
.

(Stubbe
[
76
]
)
Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category. Then

{\sf c}_{*}\circ{\sf c}^{*}=\mathcal{C}X\quad\text{and}\quad{\sf c}^{*}\circ{\sf c}_{*}=X.

Therefore,
X
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}X
in the category of distributors.

Proof.

The equality
{\sf c}^{*}\circ{\sf c}_{*}=X
follows from that
{\sf c}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}X
is fully faithful. For the equality
{\sf c}_{*}\circ{\sf c}^{*}=\mathcal{C}X
, let
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}X
. Since
X\swarrow\phi
is left adjoint to
\phi
by Lemma
5.6
, then

\displaystyle{\sf c}_{*}\circ{\sf c}^{*}(\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}{\sf c}_{*}(x,\psi)\with{\sf c}^{*}(\phi,x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\mathcal{C}X({\sf y}(x),\psi)\with\mathcal{C}X(\phi,{\sf y}(x))

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\psi(x)\with(X(-,x)\swarrow\phi)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\psi(x)\with(X\swarrow\phi)(x)

\displaystyle=\psi\circ(X\swarrow\phi)

\displaystyle=\psi\swarrow\phi

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}X(\phi,\psi).\qed

Theorem 8.6
.

(Kelly
[
39
]
)
Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category.

(i)

\mathcal{C}X
is Cauchy complete.

(ii)

For each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
Y
Cauchy complete, there is a unique functor
\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{C}X\longrightarrow Y
that extends
f
. Furthermore, if
f
is fully faithful, then so is
\overline{f}
.

Proof.

(i) We show that for each Cauchy weight
\Phi
of
\mathcal{C}X
, the colimit
\phi
of the inclusion functor
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{C}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
\Phi
is a Cauchy weight of
X
, which implies that
X
is Cauchy complete. In other words, we show that
\mathcal{C}X
is closed in
\mathcal{P}X
under formation of colimits of Cauchy weights. Since the composite
\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf c}
is the Yoneda embbeding, from Example
6.5
it follows that

\phi={\colim}_{\Phi}\mathfrak{i}=\colim\Phi\circ\mathfrak{i}^{*}=\Phi\circ\mathfrak{i}^{*}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{*}\circ{\sf c}_{*}=\Phi\circ{\sf c}_{*}.

Let
\psi
be the coweight

{\sf c}^{*}\circ\Psi\colon*{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{C}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X,

where

\Psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{C}X
is the left adjoint of
\Phi\colon\mathcal{C}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
. We claim that
\psi
is a left adjoint of
\phi
, hence
\phi
is Cauchy.

On the one hand, since
{\sf c}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}X
is fully faithful, we have

\psi\circ\phi={\sf c}^{*}\circ\Psi\circ\Phi\circ{\sf c}_{*}\leq{\sf c}^{*}\circ{\sf c}_{*}=X.

On the other hand, by Lemma
8.5
, we have

\phi\circ\psi=\Phi\circ{\sf c}_{*}\circ{\sf c}^{*}\circ\Psi=\Phi\circ\Psi\geq 1.

Therefore,
\psi
is left adjoint to
\phi
, as claimed.

(ii) It is readily verified that the functor

\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{C}X\longrightarrow Y,\quad\phi\mapsto{\colim}_{\phi}f

extends
f
. Uniqueness follows from that each Cauchy weight
\phi
is the colimit of
{\sf c}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}X
weighted by
\phi
and that colimits of Cauchy weights are preserved by all functors.

Finally, we check that if
f
is fully faithful, then so is
\overline{f}
; that is,
\mathcal{C}X(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=Y(\overline{f}(\phi_{1}),\overline{f}(\phi_{2}))
for all Cauchy weights
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
of
X
. Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{P}Y(\phi_{1}\circ f^{*},\phi_{2}\circ f^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{1}\circ f^{*}\circ f_{*},\phi_{2}\circ f^{*}\circ f_{*})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{1},\phi_{2}),

it follows that

\mathcal{C}X(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\mathcal{P}Y(\phi_{1}\circ f^{*},\phi_{2}\circ f^{*})=Y(\overline{f}(\phi_{1}),\overline{f}(\phi_{2})),

where the last equality holds because for
i=1,2
, the weight
\phi_{i}\circ f^{*}
is Cauchy, hence represented by
\overline{f}(\phi_{i})
.
∎

An immediate corollary of the above theorem is that the full subcategory of
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
composed of Cauchy complete real-enriched categories is reflective, with reflection given by
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}X
.
The real-enriched category
\mathcal{C}X
is called the
Cauchy completion
of
X
.

Corollary 8.7
.

Cauchy completion is idempotent in the sense that
\mathcal{C}X=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{C}X)
for every real-enriched category
X
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}X
is Cauchy complete, all of its Cauchy weights are representable, then the conclusion follows. ∎

The following characterization of Cauchy completion is taken from Hofmann
[
31
]
, Hofmann and Tholen
[
34
]
.

Proposition 8.8
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the Cauchy completion of
X
is the equalizer in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
of the parallel pair

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}}

Proof.

We show that a weight
\phi
of
X
is Cauchy if and only if
\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}(\phi)={\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}(\phi)
.

For sufficiency consider the coweight
\psi=X\swarrow\phi={\sf y}_{X}^{*}\swarrow\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi)
. We wish to show that
\phi
is right adjoint to
\psi
, hence a Cauchy weight. Since
\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}(\phi)={\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}(\phi)=\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi)
, then

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi

\displaystyle=\phi\circ(X\swarrow\phi)

\displaystyle=\phi\circ({\sf y}_{X}^{*}\swarrow\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi))

\displaystyle=\phi\circ{\sf y}_{X}^{*}\circ\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-)\dashv\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}(\phi)\circ\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi)\circ\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-)

\displaystyle=1.

The inequality
\psi\circ\phi\leq X
is trivial. Thus,
\phi
is right adjoint to
\psi
.

For necessity suppose
\phi
is a Cauchy weight. Then
\psi\coloneqq X\swarrow\phi
is left adjoint to
\phi
, hence

1=\phi\circ\psi=\phi\circ{\sf y}_{X}^{*}\circ\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-)=\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}(\phi)\circ\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-),

therefore

\displaystyle{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}(\phi)\circ\mathcal{P}X(\phi,-)\circ\mathcal{P}X(-,\phi)=\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}(\phi).\qed

In the following we characterize Cauchy completeness in terms of topological property.
Let
X
be a real-enriched category. For each
x\in X
and
r<1
, the set

B(x,r)\coloneqq\{y\in X\mid X(x,y)>r\}

is called the
open ball
of
X
with center
x
and radius
r
. The collection
\{B(x,r)\mid x\in X,r<1\}
is a base for a topology on
X
, the resulting topology is called the
open ball topology
of
X
.

For each real-enriched category
(X,\alpha)
, the
symmetrization
of
(X,\alpha)
refers to the symmetric (in an evident sense) real-enriched category
(X,S(\alpha))
, where
S(\alpha)(x,y)=\min\{\alpha(x,y),\alpha(y,x)\}
.

It is clear that the open ball topology of the symmetrization of
X
is the least common refinement of the open ball topology of
X
and that of
X^{\rm op}
.

Proposition 8.9
.

A net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of a real-enriched category
X
converges to
x
w.r.t the open ball topology if and only if
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})=1
.

Assigning open ball topology to real-enriched categories yields a functor from real-enriched categories to topological spaces.

Definition 8.10
.

Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a net and
a
is an element of a real-enriched category
X
. We say that

(i)

\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is Cauchy (also called biCauchy) if

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j,k\geq i}X(x_{j},x_{k})=1.

(ii)

a
is a bilimit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
if for all
x\in X
,

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})=X(x,a),\quad\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)=X(a,x).

It is clear that a net has at most one bilimit up to isomorphism.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
be a Cauchy net and
a
be an element of a real-enriched category
X
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

a
is a bilimit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
.

(2)

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(a,x_{j})=1
and
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},a)=1
.

(3)

\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
converges to
a
in the open ball topology of the symmetrization of
X
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
trivial.

(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
This follows from Proposition
8.9
and the fact that the requirement in (2) is equivalent to that

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\min\{X(a,x_{j}),X(x_{j},a)\}=1.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
For all
x\in X
, we calculate:

\displaystyle X(x,a)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(a,x_{j})\Big)\with X(x,a)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(a,x_{j})\with X(x,a)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},a)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},a)\with X(x,x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq X(x,a),

which implies
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})=X(x,a)
. The other equality is verified likewise. So
a
is a bilimit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
. ∎

Proposition
8.9
implies that if a net of
X
converges in the open ball topology of the symmetrization of
X
, then it is a Cauchy net of
X
.

Example 8.12
.

Let
\with
be the product t-norm. Consider the real-enriched category
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
. Then, a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy if and only if it is eventually constant with value
0
or converges in the usual sense to some point other than
0
. Likewise for
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
.

Proposition 8.13
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

Every Cauchy weight of
X
has a colimit.

(2)

Every Cauchy net of
X
has a bilimit.

(3)

Every Cauchy sequence of
X
has a bilimit.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a Cauchy net of
X
. Then the weight

\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})

is Cauchy with a left adjoint given by the coweight

\psi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},-).

Actually, a slightly stronger conclusion will be proved in Proposition
9.8
. Let
a
be a colimit of the Cauchy weight
\phi
. Then
\phi(x)=X(x,a)
and
\psi(x)=X(a,x)
for all
x\in X
, which implies that
a
is a bilimit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
Trivial.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)

Suppose
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
is a Cauchy weight, with a left adjoint
\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
. Then,
\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with\psi(x)\geq 1
and
\psi(y)\with\phi(x)\leq X(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
.

For each
n\geq 1
, pick some
x_{n}
such that

\phi(x_{n})\with\psi(x_{n})\geq 1-1/n.

Since for all
n,m\geq 1
we have

X(x_{n},x_{m})\geq\psi(x_{m})\with\phi(x_{n})\geq(1-1/m)\with(1-1/n),

then
\{x_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence, hence has a bilimit, say
a
. This means that for all
x\in X
,

\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}X(x,x_{m})=X(x,a),\quad\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}X(x_{m},x)=X(a,x).

We claim that
a
is a colimit of
\phi
. For this it suffices to show that

\phi=\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}X(-,x_{m}).

For all
x\in X
and
k\geq 1
, since

(1-1/k)\with\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}X(x,x_{m})\leq\sup_{n\geq k}\inf_{m\geq n}\phi(x_{m})\with X(x,x_{m})\leq\phi(x),

it follows that

\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}X(x,x_{m})\leq\phi(x)

by arbitrariness of
k
. On the other hand, since

X(x,x_{m})\geq\psi(x_{m})\with\phi(x)\geq(1-1/m)\with\phi(x)

for all
m\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}X(x,x_{m})

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{n\geq 1}\inf_{m\geq n}(1-1/m)\with\phi(x)\geq\phi(x).\qed

The following conclusion was first observed by Lawvere
[
54
]
for metric spaces.

Theorem 8.14
.

A real-enriched category
X
is Cauchy complete if and only if each of its Cauchy sequences converges uniquely in the open ball topology of its symmetrization.

9.
Yoneda completeness

This section deals with an important notion in the theory of real-enriched categories, that of Yoneda completeness. Yoneda complete enriched categories may be viewed as analogue of directed partially ordered sets in the enriched context.

The following definition is taken from Bonsangue, van Breugel and Rutten
[
9
]
, and Wagner
[
83
]
.

Definition 9.1
.

Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a net and
b
is an element of a real-enriched category
X
. We say that

(i)

\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy if

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq j\geq i}X(x_{j},x_{k})=1.

(ii)

b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
if for all
y\in X
,

X(b,y)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j}X(x_{j},y).

Every Cauchy net is forward Cauchy; every bilimit is a Yoneda limit; and every net has at most one Yoneda limit up to isomorphism.
The following proposition says that for Cauchy nets, Yoneda limits coincides with bilimits.

Proposition 9.2
.

Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a Cauchy net and
b
is an element of a real-enriched category
X
. Then
b
of
X
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
if and only if it is a bilimit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
.

Proof.

Sufficiency is trivial, for necessity we show
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(b,x_{j})\geq 1.
Since
b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(b,x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}X(x_{k},x_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k,j\geq i}X(x_{k},x_{j})

\displaystyle\geq 1,

which completes the proof.
∎

Definition 9.3
.

A real-enriched category is Yoneda complete if each of its forward Cauchy nets has a unique Yoneda limit. A functor between real-enriched categories (not necessarily Yoneda complete) is Yoneda continuous if it preserves Yoneda limits of all forward Cauchy nets.

It is clear that
X
is Yoneda complete if it is separated and every forward Cauchy net of
X
has a Yoneda limit.

Suppose
(X,\leq)
is an ordered set,
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a net and
b
is an element of
X
. It is readily verified that

(i)

\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy in the real-enriched category
\omega(X,\leq)
if and only if it is eventually monotone.

(ii)

b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
if and only if it is the least eventual upper bound of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
.

Therefore,
\omega(X,\leq)
is Yoneda complete whenever
(X,\leq)
is a dcpo. Furthermore, an order-preserving map
f\colon(X,\leq)\longrightarrow(Y,\leq)
is Scott continuous if and only if
f\colon\omega(X,\leq)\longrightarrow\omega(Y,\leq)
is Yoneda continuous.

Lemma 9.4
.

Suppose
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
.

(i)

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}
is a Yoneda limit of
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, so
{\sf V}
is Yoneda complete.

(ii)

\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is order convergent; that is,
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}
.

Proof.

The first conclusion is contained in Wagner
[
83
]
, the second in Lai, D & G Zhang
[
52
]
.

(i) We wish to show that for each
x\in[0,1]
,

\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)\rightarrow x=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(a_{j}\rightarrow x).

Since
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{k\geq j}(a_{j}\rightarrow a_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\sup_{l\in D}\inf_{k\geq l}(a_{j}\rightarrow a_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\Big[a_{j}\rightarrow\Big(\sup_{l\in D}\inf_{k\geq l}a_{k}\Big)\Big],

then

\displaystyle\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)\rightarrow x

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\Big(\sup_{l\in D}\inf_{k\geq l}a_{k}\Big)\rightarrow x\Big]\with\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\Big[a_{j}\rightarrow\Big(\sup_{l\in D}\inf_{k\geq l}a_{k}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(a_{j}\rightarrow x).

For the converse inequality, since the index set
D
is directed, then

\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(a_{j}\rightarrow x)\Big)\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\with(a_{j}\rightarrow x)\leq x,

hence

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(a_{j}\rightarrow x)\leq\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)\rightarrow x.

(ii) Since
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}
is a Yoneda limit of
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, it follows that for all
x\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)\rightarrow x

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(a_{j}\rightarrow x)\leq\Big(\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)\rightarrow x.

Putting
x=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}
we obtain that

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}\geq\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}.

The converse inequality is trivial.
∎

The following lemma, from Lai, D & G Zhang
[
52
]
, concerns Yoneda limits in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
. We note that it is not the dual of Lemma
9.4
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Suppose
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
.

(i)

\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}
is a Yoneda limit of
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, so
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is Yoneda complete.

(ii)

\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is order convergent; that is,
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}
.

Proof.

(i) We wish to show that for all
x\in[0,1]
,

x\rightarrow\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(x\rightarrow a_{j}).

Since
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
,

1\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq j\geq i}\alpha_{R}(a_{j},a_{k})=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq j\geq i}(a_{k}\rightarrow a_{j}),

then

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\Big[\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}(a_{k}\rightarrow a_{j})\Big]\geq 1,

hence

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\Big[\Big(\inf_{h\in D}\sup_{k\geq h}a_{k}\Big)\rightarrow a_{j}\Big]\geq 1,

and consequently,

\displaystyle x\rightarrow\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\Big[x\rightarrow\inf_{h\in D}\sup_{k\geq h}a_{k}\Big]\with\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\Big[\Big(\inf_{h\in D}\sup_{k\geq h}a_{k}\Big)\rightarrow a_{j}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(x\rightarrow a_{j}).

On the other hand, since for each
i\in D
we always have

\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}(x\rightarrow a_{k})\leq x\rightarrow\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j},

then

\displaystyle x\rightarrow\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}

\displaystyle=\inf_{i\in D}\Big(x\rightarrow\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}(x\rightarrow a_{k})

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(x\rightarrow a_{j}).

Therefore,
\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}
is a Yoneda limit of
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in D}
in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
.

(ii) Since
\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}
is a Yoneda limit of
\{a_{i}\}
in
([0,1],\alpha_{R})
, it follows that for all
x\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle x\rightarrow\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(x\rightarrow a_{j})\leq x\rightarrow\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}.

Putting
x=1
we obtain that

\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}a_{j}\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}a_{j}.

The converse inequality is trivial. ∎

Remark 9.6
.

Lemma
9.4
implies that every forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is order convergent;
Lemma
9.5
implies that every forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
is order convergent too. But,
{\sf V}
and
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
may have different forward Cauchy nets. For example, in the case that
\with
is the product t-norm, the sequence
\{1/n\}_{n\geq 1}
is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
but not in
{\sf V}
.

Lemma 9.7
.

(Wagner
[
83
]
)
Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
\mathcal{P}X
. Then,
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\phi_{j}
is a Yoneda limit of
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in D}
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}X
is Yoneda complete.

Proof.

We wish to show that for each
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
,

\mathcal{P}X\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\phi_{j},\phi\Big)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{j},\phi).

For each
x\in X
, it is clear that
\{\phi_{i}(x)\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
([0,1],\alpha_{L})
, hence

\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\phi_{j}(x)\Big)\rightarrow\phi(x)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(\phi_{j}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x)).

Thus, we only need to check that

\inf_{x\in X}\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(\phi_{j}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{j},\phi).

Since
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy,

\displaystyle\quad\inf_{x\in X}\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(\phi_{j}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\inf_{x\in X}\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}(\phi_{k}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))\Big]\with\Big[\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{k\geq j}\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{j},\phi_{k})\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{x\in X}\Big[\Big(\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}(\phi_{k}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))\Big)\with\Big(\inf_{k\geq j}\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{j},\phi_{k})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{x\in X}\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h,j}(\phi_{k}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))\with(\phi_{j}(x)\rightarrow\phi_{k}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{x\in A}(\phi_{j}(x)\rightarrow\phi(x))

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\mathcal{P}X(\phi_{j},\phi).

The converse inequality is trivial. ∎

Proposition 9.8
.

A forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of a real-enriched category
X
is Cauchy if and only if the weight
\phi\coloneqq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})
is Cauchy.

Proof.

The conclusion is contained in Hofmann and Reis
[
32
]
, Li and Zhang
[
55
]
.
Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a Cauchy net. We show that
\phi
is right adjoint to

\psi\coloneqq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},-);

that is,
\phi\circ\psi\geq 1
and
\psi\circ\phi(x,y)\leq X(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
.
For this we calculate:

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\Big[\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\Big]
