\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\sup_{i\in D}\Big[\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\Big)\with\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\Big]

(
D
is directed)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{i\in D}\Big[\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{i},x_{j})\Big)\with\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x_{i})\Big)\Big]

(let
x=x_{i}
for each
i
)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{i},x_{j})\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x_{i})\Big)

(
D
is directed)

\displaystyle=1;

(
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is Cauchy)

and

\displaystyle\psi\circ\phi(x,y)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},y)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\Big[\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},y)\Big)\with\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\Big)\Big]

(
D
is directed)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}X(x_{i},y)\with X(x,x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq X(x,y).

Conversely, suppose
\phi
is a Cauchy weight. By Proposition
5.6
,
the left adjoint of
\phi
is
\psi\coloneqq X\swarrow\phi
. By Lemma
9.7
,
\phi
is a Yoneda limit of the forward Cauchy net
\{X(-,x_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
\mathcal{P}X
, then for all
x\in X
,

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,X(-,x))

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq i}\mathcal{P}X(X(-,x_{k}),X(-,x))

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq i}X(x_{k},x).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k,j\geq i}X(x_{k},x_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{x\in X}\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k,j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\with X(x_{k},x)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{x\in X}\Big[\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq i}X(x_{k},x)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\with\psi(x)

\displaystyle=1,

which shows that the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is Cauchy.
∎

In the following we show that Yoneda limits of forward Cauchy nets can be characterized as colimits of a special kind of weights — ideals of real-enriched categories.

We say that a functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
between real-enriched categories
preserves finite colimits
if for any finite real-enriched category
K
(that means,
K
has a finite number of objects), any functor
h\colon K\longrightarrow X
, and any weight
\phi
of
K
,
f(\colim_{\phi}h)
is a colimit of
f\circ h
weighted by
\phi
whenever
\colim_{\phi}h
exists; that is to say,
f(\colim_{\phi}h)=\colim_{\phi}(f\circ h)
.

Definition 9.9
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category. A weight
\phi
of
X
is called an ideal if the functor

\sub_{X}(\phi,-)\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow{\sf V}

preserves finite colimits, where
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
.

The term
ideal
is chosen because of the fact that an ideal of an ordered set
P
(i.e. a directed lower set of
P
) is exactly a non-empty join-irreducible element in the set of lower sets of
P
.

Every Cauchy weight is clearly an ideal; in particular, every representable weight is an ideal. Since
\mathcal{P}X
is complete, it follows from Corollary
7.10
that a weight
\phi
of
X
is an ideal if and only if it satisfies the following conditions:

(I1)

\sub_{X}(\phi,-)\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves tensors; that is,

\sub_{X}(\phi,r\with\lambda)=r\with\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)

for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}X
and all
r\in[0,1]
.

(I2)

\sub_{X}(\phi,-)\colon(\mathcal{P}X)_{0}\longrightarrow[0,1]
preserves finite joins; that is,

\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda\vee\mu)=\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)\vee\sub_{X}(\phi,\mu)

for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}X
.

Suppose
\phi
is an ideal of
X
. Since
\sub_{X}(\phi,1_{X})=1
, it follows that
\sub_{X}(\phi,p_{X})=p
for all
p\in[0,1]
and that
\phi
is
inhabited
in the sense that
\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)=1
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, let

\mathcal{I}X

be the subcategory of
\mathcal{P}X
composed of ideals of
X
. Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a functor. By the equality

\sub_{Y}(f_{\exists}(\phi),\mu)=\sub_{X}(\phi,f^{-1}(\mu))

for every weight
\phi
of
X
and every weight
\mu
of
Y
, one readily verifies that if
\phi
is an ideal of
X
, then
f_{\exists}(\phi)
is an ideal of
Y
. So the assignment
X\mapsto\mathcal{I}X
defines a functor

\mathcal{I}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},

which is a subfunctor of the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

Proposition
8.13
shows that for each real-enriched category
X
, every Cauchy weight of
X
has a colimit if, and only if, every Cauchy net of
X
has a bilimit. A parallel result holds for Yoneda limits and colimits of ideals.

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category. Then, every forward Cauchy net of
X
has a Yoneda limit if and only if every ideal of
X
has a colimit. Therefore,
X
is Yoneda complete if and only if each of its ideals has a unique colimit.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Lemma
9.11
and Proposition
9.13
below. ∎

The following lemma, from Flagg, Sünderhauf and Wagner
[
18
]
, relates Yoneda limits of forward Cauchy nets to colimits of weights.

Lemma 9.11
.

Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of a real-enriched category
X
. Then, an element
b
of
X
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
if and only if
b
is a colimit of the weight
\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})
.

Proof.

Since
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is fully faithful,
\{X(-,x_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of

\mathcal{P}X
. By Lemma
9.7
, the weight

\phi\coloneqq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})

is a Yoneda limit of
\{X(-,x_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, thus for each
x\in X
,

\mathcal{P}X(\phi,X(-,x))=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\mathcal{P}X(X(-,x_{j}),X(-,x))=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x).

Therefore,

b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}

\displaystyle\iff\forall x\in X,~X(b,x)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)

\displaystyle\iff\forall x\in X,~X(b,x)=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,X(-,x))

\displaystyle\iff\text{$b$ is a colimit of $\phi$}.\qed

For every dcpo
(X,\leq)
, the real-enriched category
\omega(X,\leq)
is Yoneda complete. Conversely, as a consequence of the above lemma we show that the underlying order of a Yoneda complete real-enriched category is directed complete.

Corollary 9.12
.

The underlying ordered set of each Yoneda complete real-enriched category is a dcpo.

Proof.

Suppose
X
is Yoneda complete,
D
is a directed subset of
X_{0}
. We view
D
as a forward Cauchy net of
X
and let
b
be its Yoneda limit. By Lemma
9.11
,
b
is a colimit of the weight
\sup_{x\in D}\inf_{y\geq x}X(-,y)
. Since
\inf_{y\geq x}X(-,y)=X(-,x)
for every
x\in D
, it follows that
b
is a colimit of the conical weight
\sup_{x\in D}X(-,x)
, hence a join of
D
in
X_{0}
by the argument of Theorem
7.8
. ∎

To state and prove Proposition
9.13
, we need the notion of formal balls of a real-enriched category. Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category. A
formal ball
of
X
is a pair
(x,r)
with
x\in X
and
r\in[0,1]
,
x
is called the center and
r
the radius. For formal balls
(x,r)
and
(y,s)
, let

(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,s)\quad\text{if}~r\leq s\with X(x,y).

The relation
\sqsubseteq
is reflexive and transitive, hence an order. We write
\mathrm{B}X
for the set of formal balls of
X
ordered by
\sqsubseteq
. It is obvious that
(x,0)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
for all
x,y\in X
and
s\in[0,1]
, so any formal ball with radius
0
is a bottom element of
\mathrm{B}X
.

The ordered set of formal balls is introduced by Weihrauch and Schreiber
[
85
]
for metric spaces. It has been extended to quasi-metric spaces and quantale-enriched categories in general, and has been investigated extensively, see e.g. Edalat and Heckmann
[
14
]
, Goubault-Larrecq and Ng
[
24
]
, Kostanek and Waszkiewicz
[
43
]
, Rutten
[
71
]
.

The ordered set
\mathrm{B}X
of formal balls is closely related to
\mathcal{P}X
. For all
x,y\in X
and
r,s\in[0,1]
, since

\mathcal{P}X(r\with{\sf y}(x),s\with{\sf y}(y))=r\rightarrow\mathcal{P}X({\sf y}(x),s\with{\sf y}(y))=r\rightarrow(s\with X(x,y)),

it follows that

(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,s)\iff 1\leq\mathcal{P}X(r\with{\sf y}(x),s\with{\sf y}(y)).

So, if we identify a formal ball
(x,r)
with the weight
r\with{\sf y}(x)
, then the order relation between formal balls is the order relation inherited from
(\mathcal{P}X)_{0}
.

The following proposition is an improvement of related results in Lai, D & G Zhang
[
52
]
. It shows in particular that ideals of a real-enriched category
X
are precisely those weights generated by forward Cauchy nets.

Proposition 9.13
.

For each weight
\phi
of a real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\phi
is an ideal of
X
.

(2)

\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)=1
and
\phi
is a coprime in
(\mathcal{P}X)_{0}
in the sense that

\phi\leq\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}\implies\phi\leq\psi_{1}\text{ or }\phi\leq\psi_{2}

for all weights
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of
X
.

(3)

\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)=1
and
\mathrm{B}\phi\coloneqq\{(x,r)\in\mathrm{B}X\mid\phi(x)>r\}
is a directed subset of
\mathrm{B}X
.

(4)

\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})
for some forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of
X
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Obvious.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
First we show that for all weights
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of
X
,

\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2})\leq\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi_{1})\vee\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi_{2}).

Let
r=\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2})
. Since cotensors in
\mathcal{P}X
are computed pointwise (Example
7.3
) and
[0,1]
is linearly ordered, it follows that

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\leq r\rightarrow(\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2})=(r\rightarrow\psi_{1})\vee(r\rightarrow\psi_{2}),

then either
\phi\leq r\rightarrow\psi_{1}
or
\phi\leq r\rightarrow\psi_{2}
, hence
r\leq\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi_{1})\vee\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi_{2})
.

Now we prove that
(\mathrm{B}\phi,\sqsubseteq)
is directed. For
(x,r)
and
(y,s)
of
\mathrm{B}\phi
, consider the weights

\phi_{1}=X(x,-)\rightarrow r\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{2}=X(y,-)\rightarrow s.

Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}X(\phi,\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{P}X(\phi,\phi_{1})\vee\mathcal{P}X(\phi,\phi_{2})=(\phi(x)\rightarrow r)\vee(\phi(y)\rightarrow s)<1,

there exists
z\in X
such that

\phi(z)>(X(x,z)\rightarrow r)\vee(X(y,z)\rightarrow s)

because
\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)=1
. Pick
t\in[0,1]
such that

\phi(z)>t>(X(x,z)\rightarrow r)\vee(X(y,z)\rightarrow s).

Then
(z,t)\in\mathrm{B}\phi
. We assert that
(z,t)
is an upper bound of
(x,r)
and
(y,s)
, hence
(\mathrm{B}\phi,\sqsubseteq)
is directed. To see this, let
u=X(x,z)\rightarrow r
. Then
u
is by definition the largest element of
[0,1]
with
u\with X(x,z)\leq r
. Since
t>u
, it follows that
r<t\with X(x,z)
, then
(x,r)\sqsubseteq(z,t)
. Likewise,
(y,s)\sqsubseteq(z,t)
.

(3)\Rightarrow(4)
Index the directed set
(\mathrm{B}\phi,\sqsubseteq)
by itself as
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
; that means,
(x_{i},r_{i})\sqsubseteq(x_{j},r_{j})
if and only if
i\leq j
. We show in two steps that the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
satisfies the requirement.

Step 1
.
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy.

Let
r<1
. Pick
(x_{i},r_{i})\in\mathrm{B}\phi
with
r_{i}>r
. Then

r\leq r_{i}\leq r_{j}\leq r_{k}\with X(x_{j},x_{k})\leq X(x_{j},x_{k})

whenever
i\leq j\leq k
. Thus,
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy by arbitrariness of
r
.

Step 2
. For all
x\in X
,

\phi(x)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j}).

If
r<\phi(x)
, then
(x,r)\in\mathrm{B}\phi
, so
(x,r)=(x_{k},r_{k})
for some
k\in D
. Therefore,

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{j\geq k}X(x_{k},x_{j})\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{i},x_{j}),

and consequently,

\phi(x)\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})

by arbitrariness of
r
.

Conversely, if

r<\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j}),

then there exist
k\in D
and
s\in[0,1]
such that

r<s\leq\inf_{j\geq k}X(x,x_{j}).

Since
\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)=1
, there is
l\in D
such that
r\leq s\with r_{l}
. Take an upper bound
(x_{h},r_{h})
of
(x_{k},r_{k})
and
(x_{l},r_{l})
. Then

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\leq s\with r_{l}\leq s\with r_{h}\leq X(x,x_{h})\with\phi(x_{h})\leq\phi(x).

Therefore,

\phi(x)\geq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})

by arbitrariness of
r
.

(4)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X
. We wish to show that

\phi\coloneqq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})

is an ideal.

Step 1
. We show that for each weight
\lambda
of
X
,

\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}\lambda(x_{j}).

Since
\lambda
is a weight,
\{\lambda(x_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
,
then

\displaystyle\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sub_{X}\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j}),\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\mathcal{P}X(X(-,x_{j}),\lambda)

(Lemma
9.7
)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\lambda(x_{j})

(Yoneda lemma)

\displaystyle=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}\lambda(x_{j}).

(Lemma
9.5
(ii))

Step 2
. For all
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}X
and all
r\in[0,1]
,
\sub_{X}(\phi,r\with\lambda)=r\with\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)
.

By Step 1, we have

\displaystyle\sub_{X}(\phi,r\with\lambda)

\displaystyle=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}r\with\lambda(x_{j})

\displaystyle=r\with\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}\lambda(x_{j})

(
\with
is continuous)

\displaystyle=r\with\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda).

Step 3
. For all weights
\lambda,\mu
of
X
,
\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda\vee\mu)=\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)\vee\sub_{X}(\phi,\mu)
.

For this we calculate:

\displaystyle~\quad\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda)\vee\sub_{X}(\phi,\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}\lambda(x_{j})\vee\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}\mu(x_{j})

(Step 1)

\displaystyle=\inf_{i\in D}\sup_{j\geq i}(\lambda(x_{j})\vee\mu(x_{j}))

\displaystyle=\sub_{X}(\phi,\lambda\vee\mu).

(Step 1)

The proof is completed.
∎

Suppose
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a directed subset of the ordered set
\mathrm{B}X
of formal balls of a real-enriched category
X
. If
r_{i}
tends to
1
, the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is clearly forward Cauchy. Such nets are said to be
weightable
in Goubault-Larrecq
[
23
]
.

We list here some consequences of Lemma
9.11
and Proposition
9.13
.

(i)

A functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is Yoneda continuous if and only if it preserves colimits of all ideals.

(ii)

For each forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, there is a weightable forward Cauchy net
\{y_{j}\}_{j\in E}
such that
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
and
\{y_{j}\}_{j\in E}
have the same Yoneda limits. In particular, a real-enriched category is Yoneda complete if and only if each of its weightable forward Cauchy nets has a unique Yoneda limit.

Another consequence of Proposition
9.13
is that for real-enriched categories, ideals in the sense of Definition
9.9
coincide with those in the sense of Flagg, Sünderhauf and Wagner
[
18
, Definition 10]
.

Corollary 9.14
.

Suppose
\phi
is a weight of a real-enriched category
X
. Then
\phi
is an ideal of
X
if and only if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)=1
.

(ii)

If
r<1
,
s_{1}<\phi(x_{1})
and
s_{2}<\phi(x_{2})
, then there exists
x\in X
such that
r<\phi(x)
,
s_{1}<X(x_{1},x)
and
s_{2}<X(x_{2},x)
.

Corollary 9.15
.

(i)

A weight
\phi
of
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is an ideal if and only if either
\phi(x)=x\rightarrow a
for some
a\in[0,1]
or
\phi(x)=\sup_{b<a}(x\rightarrow b)
for some
a>0
.

(ii)

A weight
\psi
of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
is an ideal if and only if either
\psi(x)=a\rightarrow x
for some
a\in[0,1]
or
\psi(x)=\sup_{b>a}(b\rightarrow x)
for some
a<1
.

Proof.

We prove (i) for example. Sufficiency is easy since the weight
\sup_{b<a}(x\rightarrow b)
is generated by the forward Cauchy sequence
\{a-1/n\}_{n\geq 1}
. For necessity suppose
\phi
is an ideal of
([0,1],\alpha_{L})
. Then there is a forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of
([0,1],\alpha_{L})
such that

\phi(x)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(x\rightarrow x_{j}).

Let
a=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}x_{j}
. Then

\phi(x)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(x\rightarrow x_{j})=\sup_{i\in D}\Big(x\rightarrow\inf_{j\geq i}x_{j}\Big),

so, either
\phi(x)=x\rightarrow a
or
\phi(x)=\sup_{b<a}(x\rightarrow b)
. ∎

Corollary 9.16
.

Every functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
X
symmetric is Yoneda continuous.

Proof.

Let
b
be a Yoneda limit of a forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of
X
. From symmetry of
X
one infers that
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a Cauchy net, hence
\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j})
is a Cauchy weight by Proposition
9.8
and consequently,
f
preserves the colimit of
\phi
by Proposition
8.1
. ∎

Theorem 9.17
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category. Then

(i)

\mathcal{I}X
is Yoneda complete.

(ii)

For each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
Y
Yoneda complete, there is a unique Yoneda continuous functor
\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{I}X\longrightarrow Y
that extends
f
.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{I}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
for the inclusion functor and write
{\sf t}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}X
for the Yoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{I}X
. The composite
\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf t}
is then the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
.

(i) We show that
\mathcal{I}X
is closed in
\mathcal{P}X
under formation of colimits of ideals; or equivalently,
\mathcal{I}X
is closed in
\mathcal{P}X
under Yoneda limits of forward Cauchy nets. That means, for each ideal
\Lambda
of
\mathcal{I}X
, the colimit of the inclusion functor
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{I}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
\Lambda
is an ideal of
X
.
Since the composite
\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf t}
is the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
, from Example
6.5
it follows that

{\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i}=\colim\Lambda\circ\mathfrak{i}^{*}=\Lambda\circ\mathfrak{i}^{*}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{*}\circ{\sf t}_{*}=\Lambda\circ{\sf t}_{*},

hence for each weight
\phi
of
X
,

\displaystyle\sub_{X}({\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i},\phi)=\sub_{X}(\Lambda\circ{\sf t}_{*},\phi)=\sub_{\mathcal{I}X}(\Lambda,\sub_{X}(-,\phi)).

Then, for each weight
\phi
of
X
and each
r\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\sub_{X}({\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i},r\with\phi)

\displaystyle=\sub_{\mathcal{I}X}(\Lambda,\sub_{X}(-,r\with\phi))

\displaystyle=\sub_{\mathcal{I}X}(\Lambda,r\with\sub_{X}(-,\phi))

\displaystyle=r\with\sub_{\mathcal{I}X}(\Lambda,\sub_{X}(-,\phi))

\displaystyle=r\with\sub_{X}({\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i},\phi),

showing that
\sub_{X}({\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i},-)
preserves tensor.
In the same fashion one verifies that
\sub_{X}({\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i},-)
preserves finite join. So
{\colim}_{\Lambda}\mathfrak{i}
is an ideal of
X
, as desired.

(ii) The functor

\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{I}X\longrightarrow Y,\quad\phi\mapsto{\colim}_{\phi}

clearly extends
f
. It remains to check that
\overline{f}
preserves colimits of ideals. It is clear that
\overline{f}
is equal to the composite
\colim\circ f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{I}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}Y\longrightarrow Y
. Since
\colim\colon\mathcal{I}Y\longrightarrow Y
is left adjoint to
{\sf t}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}Y
, it preserves colimits. Since
\mathcal{I}X
and
\mathcal{I}Y
are, respectively, closed in
\mathcal{P}X
and
\mathcal{P}Y
under formation of colimits of ideals, and
f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y
preserves all colimits, it follows that the composite
\overline{f}
preserves colimits of all ideals.
Uniqueness of
\overline{f}
follows from that each ideal
\phi
of
X
is the colimit of
{\sf t}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}X
weighted by
\phi
.∎

Proposition
9.13
shows that forward Cauchy nets are closely related to directed subsets of the set of formal balls. Actually, there exist interesting interactions between Yoneda completeness of a real-enriched category and directed completeness of its set of formal balls.

Theorem 9.18
.

If the continuous t-norm
\&
is Archimedean, then for each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

Every forward Cauchy net of
X
has a Yoneda limit.

(2)

Every directed subset of the ordered set
\mathrm{B}X
of formal balls has a join.

Theorem
9.18
shows that, when
\&
is a continuous Archimedean t-norm, Yoneda completeness of a real-enriched category is equivalent to directed completeness of its set of formal balls. This is first proved in Edalat and Heckmann
[
14
, Theorem 6]
for metric spaces, then extended to categories enriched over a “value quantale” in Kostanek and Waszkiewicz
[
43
, Theorem 7.1]
. The form stated here appears in Yang and Zhang
[
87
]
.

To prove Theorem
9.18
we need several lemmas. In the following, we agree by convention that a directed subset
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
\mathrm{B}X
is always indexed by itself; that means,
(x_{i},r_{i})\sqsubseteq(x_{j},r_{j})
if and only if
i\leq j
.

Lemma 9.19
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a directed subset of
\mathrm{B}X
. If
x
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
and
r=\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}
, then
(x,r)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
in
\mathrm{B}X
.

Proof.

The conclusion is contained in Kostanek and Waszkiewicz
[
43
, Lemma 7.7]
, also in Ali-Akbari, Honari, Pourmahdian and Rezaii
[
2
, Theorem 3.3]
and Goubault-Larrecq
[
23
, Lemma 7.4.25]
. First we show that
(x,r)
is an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
; that is,
r_{i}\leq r\with X(x_{i},x)
for all
i\in D
.

Since
x
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, then

1=X(x,x)=\sup_{k\in D}\inf_{j\geq k}X(x_{j},x).

For each
i\in D
and each
\epsilon<r_{i}
, by continuity of
\&
there is some
k\in D
such that
\epsilon\leq r_{i}\with X(x_{j},x)
whenever
j\geq k
. Thus, for all
j\geq i,k
we have

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq r_{i}\with X(x_{j},x)\leq r_{j}\with X(x_{i},x_{j})\with X(x_{j},x)\leq r\with X(x_{i},x).

By arbitrariness of
\epsilon
we obtain that
r_{i}\leq r\with X(x_{i},x)
.

Next we show that
(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
whenever
(y,s)
is an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
.
Since
(y,s)
is an upper bound,
then
r_{i}\leq r_{j}\leq s\with X(x_{j},y)
whenever
i\leq j
, hence

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}s\with X(x_{j},y)=s\with X(x,y),

which shows that
(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
, as desired. ∎

Lemma 9.20
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a directed subset of
\mathrm{B}X
with
r_{i}>0
for some
i\in D
.

(i)

If
\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}=1
, then the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy.

(ii)

If
\&
is Archimedean, then the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy.

Proof.

(i) Since
r_{i}\leq r_{j}\with X(x_{i},x_{j})
whenever
i\leq j
, it follows that

1=\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j}r_{j}\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j\leq k}X(x_{j},x_{k}),

then
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy.

(ii) If
\&
is Archimedean, it is readily verified that for all
r,s,t\in[0,1]
,

0<r\leq s\with t\implies t\geq s\rightarrow r.

Without loss of generality we assume that
r_{i}>0
for all
i\in D
. Since
r_{i}\leq r_{j}\with X(x_{i},x_{j})
whenever
i\leq j
, then
X(x_{i},x_{j})\geq r_{j}\rightarrow r_{i}
whenever
i\leq j
. Since the net
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in D}
converges to its join and the implication operator of an Archimedean continuous t-norm is continuous except possibly at
(0,0)
, it follows that
X(x_{i},x_{j})
tends to
1
, so
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy.
∎

We say that a real-enriched category
X
has
property
(R)
if, for each pair
(s,t)
of elements of
[0,1]
with
0<s\leq t
, there exists
r<1
such that

(x,t\with r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,s)\iff(x,r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,t\rightarrow s)

for all
x,y\in X
and all
r^{\prime}\geq r
.

Lemma 9.21
.

Let
X
be a real-enriched category with property
(R)
. Then for all
x,y\in X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

(x,1)\sqsubseteq(y,1)
.

(2)

(x,s)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
for all
s\not=0
.

(3)

(x,s)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
for some
s\not=0
.

Proof.

It suffices to check
(3)\Rightarrow(1)
. Since
0<s\leq s
, there is some
r<1
such that

(x,s\with r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,s)\iff(x,r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,s\rightarrow s)

whenever
r^{\prime}\geq r
. Putting
r^{\prime}=1
gives that
(x,1)\sqsubseteq(y,1)
. ∎

Proposition 9.22
.

(Yang and Zhang
[
87
]
)
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The continuous t-norm
\with
is Archimedean.

(2)

Every real-enriched category has property
(R)
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Since
\with
is Archimedean, it is either isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm or to the product t-norm. In the following we check the conclusion for the case that
\with
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm, leaving the other case to the reader.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
\with
is, not only isomorphic to, the Łukasiewicz t-norm; that is,
r\with s=\max\{0,r+s-1\}
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category and
0<s\leq t
. If
t=1
, it is trivial that

(x,t\with r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,s)\iff(x,r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,t\rightarrow s)

for all
r^{\prime}>0
and
x,y\in X
, so each
r>0
satisfies the requirement. If
t<1
, pick
r\in(1-t,1)
. Then for all
r^{\prime}\geq r
and
x,y\in X
,

\displaystyle(x,t\with r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,s)

\displaystyle\iff r^{\prime}+t-1\leq s+X(x,y)-1

\displaystyle\iff r^{\prime}\leq s-t+X(x,y)

\displaystyle\iff(x,r^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(y,t\rightarrow s),

which shows that
r
satisfies the requirement.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
We wish to show that
\with
has no nontrivial idempotent element. It suffices to show that for all
s\not=0
and
q\in[0,1]
, if
s\leq s\with q
then
q=1
. Consider the real-enriched category
X=\{x,y\}
with
X(x,x)=X(y,y)=1
and
X(x,y)=q=X(y,x)
. Since
(x,s)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
and
X
has property (R), then
(x,1)\sqsubseteq(y,1)
, hence
1\leq 1\with X(x,y)=q
.
∎

Corollary 9.23
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
has property
(R)
.

(2)

For all
x,y\in X
, if
X(x,y)\geq p
for some idempotent element
p>0
, then
X(x,y)=1
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Suppose
X(x,y)\geq p>0
with
p
idempotent. Let
s=t=p
. Since
t=p=s\with X(x,y)
, it follows that
(x,p)\sqsubseteq(y,p)
. Then by property (R) one gets that
(x,1)\sqsubseteq(y,1)
, hence
X(x,y)=1
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
By Proposition
9.22
we may assume that
\&
is non-Archimedean with a nontrivial idempotent
q
. Let
X
be a real-enriched category such that
X(x,y)=1
whenever
X(x,y)\geq p
for some idempotent element
p>0
. We wish to show that
X
has property (R). For this we show that if
0<s\leq t
and
r>q
, then

(x,t\with r)\sqsubseteq(y,s)\iff(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,t\rightarrow s)

for all
x,y\in X
.
The direction
\Longleftarrow
is trivial. For the other direction,
suppose
(x,t\with r)\sqsubseteq(y,s)
. We proceed with two cases.
If
q\leq t
, then
q\leq t\with r\leq s\with X(x,y)
, so
X(x,y)=1
by assumption, hence
t\with r\leq s
. Therefore
r\leq t\rightarrow s
and consequently,
(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,t\rightarrow s)
.
If
q>t
, then
t=t\with r\leq s\with X(x,y)
, so
t=s
and
X(x,y)\geq p
, where
p
is the least idempotent element in
[t,1]
. By assumption we have
X(x,y)=1
, then
(x,r)\sqsubseteq(y,t\rightarrow s)
.
∎

Lemma 9.24
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category with property
(R)
,
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a directed subset of
\mathrm{B}X
. If

(i)

\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}=1
,

(ii)

(b,1)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, and

(iii)

for each
t>0
the directed set
\{(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
\mathrm{B}X
has a join,

then
b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
.

Proof.

We wish to show that for all
y\in X
,

X(b,y)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},y).

Fix
i\in D
. For each
j\geq i
, since
(x_{j},r_{j})\sqsubseteq(b,1)
, then

\displaystyle r_{i}\with X(b,y)

\displaystyle\leq r_{j}\with X(b,y)\leq X(x_{j},b)\with X(b,y)\leq X(x_{j},y),

hence

r_{i}\with X(b,y)\leq\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},y).

Therefore,

\displaystyle X(b,y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}(r_{i}\with X(b,y))\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},y).

For the converse inequality, let

t=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},y).

We show that
t\leq X(b,y)
. We assume
t>0
and finish the proof in two steps.

Step 1
.
(b,t)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
.

By assumption
\{(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
has a join, say
(z,s)
. We only need to show that
(b,t)
is equivalent to
(z,s)
. Since
(b,t)
is an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, then
(z,s)\sqsubseteq(b,t)
, hence
s\leq t
. Since
r_{i}
tends to
1
and
t\with r_{i}\leq s\with X(x_{i},z)
for all
i\in D
, it follows that
t\leq s
. Therefore
0<s=t
.
Since
r_{i}
tends to
1
, we may assume that all
r_{i}
are large enough. Since
(z,t)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
,
t\with r_{i}\leq t\with X(x_{i},z)
for all
i\in D
, then by property (R) of
X
,
r_{i}\leq(t\rightarrow t)\with X(x_{i},z)=X(x_{i},z)
for all
i\in D
. This shows that
(z,1)
is an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, so
(b,1)\sqsubseteq(z,1)
, and then
(b,t)\sqsubseteq(z,t)
. Therefore,
(b,t)
is equivalent to
(z,s)
.

Step 2
.
t\leq X(b,y)
.

For each
i\in D
,

\displaystyle r_{i}\with t

\displaystyle=r_{i}\with\sup_{k\in D}\inf_{j\geq k}X(x_{j},y)

\displaystyle=r_{i}\with\sup_{k\geq i}\inf_{j\geq k}X(x_{j},y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\geq i}\inf_{j\geq k}r_{i}\with X(x_{j},y)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{k\geq i}\inf_{j\geq k}r_{j}\with X(x_{i},x_{j})\with X(x_{j},y)

\displaystyle\leq X(x_{i},y),

then
(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\sqsubseteq(y,1)
. This shows that
(y,1)
is an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},t\with r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, then
(b,t)\sqsubseteq(y,1)
and
t\leq X(b,y)
, as desired. ∎

The condition (iii) in Lemma
9.24
cannot be dropped.

Example 9.25
.

(Yang and Zhang
[
87
]
) Suppose
\&
is the Łukasiewicz t-norm or the product t-norm.
Consider the real-enriched category
X
, where
X=\{1\}\cup\{1-1/n\mid n\geq 2\}
and

X(x,y)=\begin{cases}1/2&x=1,y\not=1,\\
x\rightarrow y&{\rm otherwise}.\end{cases}

We assert that
X
is not Yoneda complete, but every directed subset
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
\mathrm{B}X
with
\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}=1
has a join.

The sequence
\{1-1/n\}_{n\geq 2}
is forward Cauchy but has no Yoneda limit, so
X
is not Yoneda complete. Now we show that every directed subset
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
\mathrm{B}X
with
\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}=1
has a join. Since
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy, either
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is eventually constant or
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
converges to
1
(in the usual sense).

Case 1.
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
converges to
1
. Then we claim that
(1,1)
is the only upper bound, hence a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. Since
X(z,1)=1
for all
z\in X
, then
r_{i}\leq 1=1\with X(x_{i},1)
for all
i\in D
, hence
(1,1)
is an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. Let
(y,s)
be an upper bound of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. Then
s=1
and
r_{i}\leq X(x_{i},y)
for all
i\in D
. Since both
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in D}
and
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
converge to
1
, we must have
y=1
. Therefore,
(1,1)
is the only upper bound of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
.

Case 2.
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is eventually constant. Then there is some
b\in X
and some
i\in D
such that
x_{j}=b
whenever
j\geq i
. It is clear that
(b,1)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
.

Now we are able to prove Theorem
9.18
.

Proof of Theorem
9.18
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Let
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
be a directed subset of
\mathrm{B}X
. If
r_{i}=0
for all
i\in D
, then
(x,0)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
for any
x\in X
. If
r_{i}>0
for some
i\in D
, by Lemma
9.20
(ii) the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy, so it has a Yoneda limit, say
x
. By Lemma
9.19
,
(x,r)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, where
r=\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}
. This shows that
\mathrm{B}X
is directed complete.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
We show that every ideal
\phi
of
X
has a colimit. By Proposition
9.13
, there is a directed subset
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
\mathrm{B}X
such that
\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}=1
and

\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j}).

By assumption
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
has a join, say
(b,1)
. By Proposition
9.22
and Lemma
9.24
,
b
is a Yoneda limit of the forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, hence a colimit of
\phi
by Lemma
9.11
. ∎

The argument of Theorem
9.18
actually proves that for each continuous t-norm and each real-enriched category
X
, it holds that

(i)

If
X
is Yoneda complete, then each directed subset
(x_{i},r_{i})_{i\in D}
of
\mathbf{B}X
with
\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}=1
has a join.

(ii)

If
X
has property (R) and
\mathbf{B}X
is directed complete, then
X
is Yoneda complete.

The following examples show that in Theorem
9.18
the requirement that
\with
is Archimedean is indispensable.

Example 9.26
.

(Yang and Zhang
[
87
]
) This example shows that if
\with
is non-Archimedean, then there is a real-enriched category that is Yoneda complete, but its set of formal balls is not directed complete.
Since
\with
is non-Archimedean, there is some
b\in(0,1)
such that
b\with b=b
. Consider the real-enriched category
X
, where
X=(0,b)
and

X(x,y)=\begin{cases}1&x=y,\\
\min\{x\rightarrow y,y\rightarrow x\}&x\not=y.\end{cases}

Since
X(x,y)\leq b
whenever
x\not=y
, every forward Cauchy net of
X
is eventually constant, so
X
is Yoneda complete. In the following we show that
\mathrm{B}X
is not directed complete. Pick a strictly increasing sequence
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
(0,b)
that converges to
b
. For each
n
let
r_{n}=x_{n}
. We claim that the subset
(x_{n},r_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
\mathrm{B}X
is directed and has no join.

By continuity of
\with
, for all
n\leq m
, we have

\displaystyle r_{n}

\displaystyle=x_{n}=x_{m}\with(x_{m}\rightarrow x_{n})=r_{m}\with X(x_{n},x_{m}),

so
(x_{n},r_{n})\sqsubseteq(x_{m},r_{m})
and consequently,
(x_{n},r_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is directed.

Next we show that
(x_{n},r_{n})_{n\geq 1}
does not have a join. Suppose on the contrary that
(x,r)
is a join of
(x_{n},r_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. Since
x<b
, there is some
n_{0}
such that
x<x_{m}
whenever
m\geq n_{0}
. Since
(x,r)
is an upper bound of
(x_{n},r_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, for each
m\geq n_{0}
we have

r_{m}\leq r\with X(x_{m},x)\leq x_{m}\rightarrow x,

this is impossible since the left side tends to
b
, while the right side tends to
x
.

Example 9.27
.

(Yang and Zhang
[
87
]
) If
\with
is the Gödel t-norm, then there is a real-enriched category that is not Yoneda complete, but its set of formal balls is directed complete. Consider the real-enriched category
X
, where
X=\{1\}\cup\{1-1/n\mid n\geq 2\}
and

X(x,y)=\begin{cases}1&x=y,\\
1/3&x=1,y\not=1,\\
\min\{x,y\}&{\rm otherwise}.\end{cases}

We claim that
X
is not Yoneda complete, but
\mathrm{B}X
is directed complete.

For each
n\geq 2
, let
x_{n}=1-1/n
. It is readily verified that the sequence
(x_{n})_{n\geq 2}
is forward Cauchy and has no Yoneda limit, so
X
is not Yoneda complete. It remains to check that
\mathrm{B}X
is directed complete. Let
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
be a directed subset of
\mathrm{B}X
. Then
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a monotone net of real numbers. Let
r=\sup_{i\in D}r_{i}
. We distinguish two cases.

Case 1. The net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is eventually constant; that is, there exist
b\in X
and
i\in D
such that
x_{j}=b
whenever
j\geq i
. In this case
(b,r)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
.

Case 2. The net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is not eventually constant. Then for each
i
there is some
j\geq i
such that
x_{i}\not=x_{j}
, hence

r_{i}\leq r_{j}\with X(x_{i},x_{j})\leq\min\{r_{j},x_{i},x_{j}\}\leq x_{i}.

Let
b=\min\{x\in X\mid r\leq x\}.

Then
(b,r)
is a join of
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
.

10.
Flat completeness

Definition 10.1
.

Suppose
\phi
is a weight of a real-enriched category
X
.

(i)

\phi
is conically flat if the functor
\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves limits of finite conical coweights, where
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
.

(ii)

\phi
is flat if
\phi\circ-\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves all finite limits.

The definition of flat weights is based on the notion of
flat distributors
in Bénabou
[
7
, Section 6]
and the characterization of flat functors in Borceux
[
10
]
. When the continuous t-norm is isomorphic to the product t-norm, conically flat weights appear as
flat left modules of quasi-metric spaces
in Vickers
[
82
]
.

As we shall see below, in contrast to the characterization of Cauchy weights in Proposition
8.2
, flat weights and ideals are not the same things in general.

We remind the reader that the underlying order of
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
is opposite to the pointwise order of coweights, the underlying order of
(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}
agrees with the pointwise order, the cotensor of
r
with
\psi
in
(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X)^{\rm op}
is given by
r\rightarrow\psi
.

Consider the following conditions:

(a)

1=\phi\circ 1_{X}=\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)
; that is,
\phi
is inhabited.

(b)

For all coweights
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
X
,
\phi\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})=(\phi\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\phi\circ\psi_{2})
.

(c)

For all
r\in[0,1]
and all coweights
\psi
of
X
,
\phi\circ(r\rightarrow\psi)=r\rightarrow\phi\circ\psi
.

Then,
\phi
is conically flat if and only if it satisfies (a) and (b);
\phi
is flat if and only if it satisfies (a)–(c).

The following characterization of conically flat weights is due to Gutiérrez García, Höhle and Kubiak
[
26
]
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Suppose
\phi
is a weight of a real-enriched category
X
. Then,
\phi
is conically flat if and only if it is inhabited and

(p_{1}\with\phi(x_{1}))\wedge(p_{2}\with\phi(x_{2}))=\sup_{x\in X}((p_{1}\with X(x_{1},x))\wedge(p_{2}\with X(x_{2},x)))\with\phi(x)

for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
and all
p_{1},p_{2}\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Necessity is obvious, sufficiency follows from that

\psi=\sup_{x\in X}\psi(x)\with X(x,-)

for each coweight
\psi
of
X
. ∎

We leave it to the reader to check that when the continuous t-norm
\with
is the Gödel t-norm,
\phi
is conically flat if and only if it is inhabited and satisfies

\phi(x_{1})\wedge\phi(x_{2})=\sup_{x\in X}X(x_{1},x)\wedge X(x_{2},x)\wedge\phi(x).

Proposition
8.2
shows that every Cauchy weight is flat. The following conclusion, due to Lai, D & G Zhang
[
52
]
, says that every ideal is conically flat.

Proposition 10.3
.

Every ideal is conically flat.

Proof.

Suppose
\phi
is an ideal of a real-enriched category
X
. We wish to show that
\phi
is conically flat. Since every ideal is inhabited, we only need to check that for all coweights
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
X
,
\phi\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})=(\phi\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\phi\circ\psi_{2})
.

By Proposition
9.13
there is a forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
such that

\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j}).

We claim that for each coweight
\psi
of
X
,

\phi\circ\psi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\psi(x_{j}).

Since
\psi
is a coweight,
\{\psi(x_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
, then

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}(\sub_{X}(\phi,\psi\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p)

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{sub vs tensor}})

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}\Big(\sub_{X}\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j}),\psi\rightarrow p\Big)\rightarrow p\Big)

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}\Big(\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\sub_{X}\big(X(-,x_{j}),\psi\rightarrow p\big)\Big)\rightarrow p\Big)

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{Yoneda limits in PX}})

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}\Big(\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(\psi(x_{j})\rightarrow p)\Big)\rightarrow p\Big)

\displaystyle(\text{Yoneda lemma})

\displaystyle=\inf_{p\in[0,1]}\Big(\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\psi(x_{j})\rightarrow p\Big)\rightarrow p\Big)

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{yoneda limit in Q}})

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\psi(x_{j}).

Now for any coweights
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
X
, we have

\displaystyle(\phi\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\phi\circ\psi_{2})

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\psi_{1}(x_{j})\wedge\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\psi_{2}(x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(\psi_{1}(x_{j})\wedge\psi_{2}(x_{j}))

\displaystyle=\phi\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

In 2005, Vickers
[
82
]
proved that every flat left module of a generalized metric space is generated by a forward Cauchy net. Said differently, when the continuous t-norm is isomorphic to the product t-norm, a weight of a real-enriched category is conically flat if and only if it is an ideal. The following theorem says that a necessary and sufficient condition for ideals, flat weights, and conically flat weights to coincide with each other is that the continuous t-norm is Archimedean.

Theorem 10.4
.

(Lai, D & G Zhang
[
52
]
)
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The continuous t-norm
\&
is Archimedean.

(2)

Every ideal is a flat weight.

(3)

Every conically flat weight is an ideal.

In this case, ideals, flat weights, and conically flat weights coincide with each other.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
It suffices to show that if
\phi
is an ideal of a real-enriched category
X
, then
\phi\circ(r\rightarrow\psi)=r\rightarrow\phi\circ\psi
for all
r\in[0,1]
and all coweights
\psi
of
X
. First we note that when
\with
is continuous and Archimedean, for each
r\in[0,1]
the function
r\rightarrow-\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow[0,1]
is continuous. Pick a forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
such that

\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j}).

Then by the argument of Proposition
10.3
we have

\phi\circ(r\rightarrow\psi)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(r\rightarrow\psi(x_{j}))=r\rightarrow\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\psi(x_{j})=r\rightarrow\phi\circ\psi,

the second equality holds due to the continuity of
r\rightarrow-\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow[0,1]
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose on the contrary that
\&
is not Archimedean. Take an idempotent element
b
of
\with
other than
0
and
1
, consider the ideal

\phi=\sup_{x<b}\alpha_{L}(-,x)

and the coweight
\psi=\alpha_{L}(b,-)
of
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
. Then

\phi\circ(b\rightarrow\psi)=\sup_{x<b}(b\rightarrow(b\rightarrow x))=b<1=b\rightarrow\sup_{x<b}x=b\rightarrow\phi\circ\psi,

contradicting that
\phi
is flat.

(1)\Rightarrow(3)
Since a continuous Archimedean t-norm is either isomorphic to the product t-norm or isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm, one readily verifies

•

if
0<r\with s
, then
\with
is strictly monotone at
(r,s)
;

•

if
0<t\leq r\with s
, then
r\rightarrow t\leq s
.

Suppose
\phi
is a flat weight of a real-enriched category
X
. Let

\mathrm{B}\phi=\{(x,r)\in X\times(0,1]\mid\phi(x)>r\}.

We assert that
\mathrm{B}\phi
is a directed subset of the ordered set
\mathrm{B}X
of formal balls of
X
.

It is clear that
\mathrm{B}\phi
is not empty. For
(x,r)
and
(y,s)
of
\mathrm{B}\phi
, consider the coweights:

\psi_{1}=\max\{r\rightarrow 0,s\}\with X(x,-),\quad\psi_{2}=\max\{s\rightarrow 0,r\}\with X(y,-).

Since
\phi(x)>r
and
\phi(y)>s
, it follows that

\phi\circ\psi_{1}=\max\{r\rightarrow 0,s\}\with\phi(x)>r\with s

and

\phi\circ\psi_{2}=\max\{s\rightarrow 0,r\}\with\phi(y)>r\with s,

hence

\phi\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})>r\with s.

So there exists
z\in X
such that

\min\{\phi(z)\with\psi_{1}(z),\phi(z)\with\psi_{2}(z)\})>r\with s.

This implies

\phi(z)\with(\max\{r\rightarrow 0,s\}\with X(x,z))>r\with s

and

\phi(z)\with(\max\{s\rightarrow 0,r\}\with X(y,z))>r\with s,

in particular,
X(x,z)>r
and
X(y,z)>s
. Let
t
be the larger one of

(\max\{r\rightarrow 0,s\}\with X(x,z))\rightarrow(r\with s)

and

(\max\{s\rightarrow 0,r\}\with X(y,z))\rightarrow(r\with s).

Then it is routine to check that

\phi(z)>t,\quad r\leq t\with X(x,z),\quad s\leq t\with X(y,z),

which show that
(z,t)
is an upper bound of
(x,r)
and
(y,s)
in
\mathrm{B}\phi
.

Index the directed set
\mathrm{B}\phi
by itself as
\{(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. Then by the argument in Proposition
9.13
one sees that the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy and

\phi(x)=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})

for all
x\in X
, so
\phi
is an ideal.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose on the contrary that
b
is a non-trivial idempotent element of
\&
. Pick
a\in(0,b)
. Consider the weight
\phi
of
{\sf V}
given by
\phi(x)=b\vee(x\rightarrow a)
. Since neither
\phi(x)\leq b
for all
x
nor
\phi(x)\leq x\rightarrow a
for all
x
, then
\phi
is not an ideal. In the following we derive a contradiction by showing that
\phi
is conically flat.

It is clear that
\phi
is inhabited. It remains to check that for all coweights
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
of
{\sf V}
,
\phi\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})=(\phi\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\phi\circ\psi_{2})
.
Since
b
is idempotent and
\psi_{i}
(
i=1,2
) is monotone, then

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in[0,1]}((b\with\psi_{i}(x))\vee((x\rightarrow a)\with\psi_{i}(x)))

\displaystyle=(b\wedge\psi_{i}(1))\vee\psi_{i}(a)

\displaystyle=(b\vee\psi_{i}(a))\wedge\psi_{i}(1),

therefore

\displaystyle(\phi\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\phi\circ\psi_{2})

\displaystyle=(b\vee\psi_{1}(a))\wedge\psi_{1}(1)\wedge(b\vee\psi_{2}(a))\wedge\psi_{2}(1)

\displaystyle=(b\vee(\psi_{1}(a)\wedge\psi_{2}(a))\wedge(\psi_{1}(1)\wedge\psi_{2}(1))

\displaystyle=\phi\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}).\qed

Definition 10.5
.

A real-enriched category is (conically, resp.) flat complete if it is separated and each of its (conically, resp.) flat weights has a colimit.

For each real-enriched category
X
, let

\mathcal{F}X

be the subcategory of
\mathcal{P}X
composed of flat weights of
X
. The assignment
X\mapsto\mathcal{F}X
defines a functor
\mathcal{F}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, which is a subfunctor of the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

Theorem 10.6
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category. Then

(i)

\mathcal{F}X
is flat complete.

(ii)

For each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
Y
flat complete, there is a unique functor
\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{F}X\longrightarrow Y
that extends
f
and preserves colimits of flat weights.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{F}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
for the inclusion functor and
{\sf t}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}X
for the functor obtained by restricting the codomain of the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
to
\mathcal{F}X
. It is clear that the composite
\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf t}
is the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
.

(i) We show that
\mathcal{F}X
is closed in
\mathcal{P}X
under formation of colimits of flat weights. That means, for each flat weight
\Phi
of
\mathcal{F}X
, the colimit of the inclusion functor
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{F}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
\Phi
is a flat weight of
X
. Since the composite
\mathfrak{i}\circ{\sf t}
is the Yoneda embbeding, by Example
6.5
it holds that

{\colim}_{\Phi}\mathfrak{i}=\Phi\circ\mathfrak{i}^{*}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{*}\circ{\sf t}_{*}=\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}.

We wish to show that
\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}
is a flat weight of
X
.

Step 1
.
\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}
is inhabited. This is easy, since

\sup_{x\in X}\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}(x)=\sup_{x\in X}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{F}X}\Phi(\phi)\with\phi(x)=\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{F}X}\Big(\Phi(\phi)\with\sup_{x\in X}\phi(x)\Big)=1.

Step 2
. For any coweights
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
X
,

\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})=(\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ\psi_{2}).

To see this, for each coweight
\psi
of
X
consider the coweight

-\circ\psi\colon\mathcal{F}X\longrightarrow{\sf V}

of
\mathcal{F}X
.
Then

\displaystyle\Phi\circ(-\circ\psi)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{F}X}\Big(\Phi(\phi)\with\sup_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\with\psi(x))\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{F}X}(\Phi(\phi)\with\phi(x))\with\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in X}(\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*})(x)\with\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ\psi.

Since
\Phi
is flat and each element of
\mathcal{F}X
is flat, it follows that

\displaystyle\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})

\displaystyle=\Phi\circ(-\circ(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\Phi\circ((-\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(-\circ\psi_{2}))

\displaystyle=(\Phi\circ(-\circ\psi_{1}))\wedge(\Phi\circ(-\circ\psi_{2}))

\displaystyle=(\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ\psi_{1})\wedge(\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ\psi_{2}).

Step 3
. Similar to Step 2, one verifies that for all
r
of
[0,1]
and all coweight
\psi
of
X
, it holds that

\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ(r\rightarrow\psi)=r\rightarrow\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}\circ\psi.

Therefore,
\Phi\circ{\sf t}_{*}
is a flat weight of
X
.

(ii) The functor

\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{F}X\longrightarrow Y,\quad\phi\mapsto{\colim}_{\phi}f

clearly extends
f
. It remains to check that
\overline{f}
preserves colimits of flat weights. It is clear that
\overline{f}
is the composite
\colim\circ f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{F}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}Y\longrightarrow Y
. Since
\colim\colon\mathcal{F}Y\longrightarrow Y
is left adjoint to
{\sf t}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}Y
, it preserves colimits. Since
\mathcal{F}X
and
\mathcal{F}Y
are closed in
\mathcal{P}X
and
\mathcal{P}Y
under formation of colimits of flat weights, respectively, and
f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y
preserves all colimits, it follows that the composite
\overline{f}
preserves colimits of all flat weights.
Uniqueness of
\overline{f}
follows from that each flat weight
\phi
of
X
is the colimit of
{\sf t}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}X
weighted by
\phi
.∎

For conically flat weights, there is a similar result. For each real-enriched category
X
, let

\mathcal{F}_{\rm c}X

be the subcategory of
\mathcal{P}X
composed of conically flat weights of
X
. The assignment
X\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\rm c}X
defines a functor
\mathcal{F}_{\rm c}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, which is a subfunctor of the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

Theorem 10.7
.

Suppose
X
is a real-enriched category. Then

(i)

\mathcal{F}_{\rm c}X
is conically flat complete.

(ii)

For each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
Y
conically flat complete, there is a unique functor
\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{F}_{\rm c}X\longrightarrow Y
that extends
f
and preserves colimits of conically flat weights.

11.
Smyth completeness

Smyth completeness originated in the works of Smyth
[
73
,
74
]
on quasi-uniform spaces. The following postulation, which extends that of Smyth complete quasi-metric spaces in Goubault-Larrecq
[
23
]
, Künzi and Schellekens
[
44
]
, is based on the characterization of Smyth complete quasi-uniform spaces in Sünderhauf
[
81
]
.

Definition 11.1
.

A real-enriched category is Smyth complete if each of its forward Cauchy nets converges uniquely in the open ball topology of its symmetrization.

Proposition
8.9
implies that if a net of
X
converges in the open ball topology of its symmetrization, then it is a Cauchy net of
X
. Therefore,

Proposition 11.2
.

If a real-enriched category
X
is Smyth complete, then it is separated and all of its forward Cauchy nets are Cauchy.

Example 11.3
.

This example concerns Smyth completeness of the real-enriched categories
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
and
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
. We distinguish three cases.

Case 1. The continuous t-norm
\&
is not Archimedean. Pick some
a\in[0,1]
with
0<a\leq a^{+}<1
, where
a^{+}
is the least idempotent element in
[a,1]
. Since
\sup_{x<a}(a\rightarrow x)\leq a^{+}
, then
[a,1]
is open in
{\sf V}
and
[0,a]
is open in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
, hence the singleton set
\{a\}
is open in the symmetrization of
{\sf V}
. Since
\{a-1/n\}_{n}
is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}
and
\{a+1/n\}_{n}
is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
, it follows that neither
{\sf V}
nor
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is Smyth complete.

Case 2.
\&
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm. In this case, the open ball topology of
{\sf V}
is
\{\varnothing,[0,1]\}\cup\{(a,1]\mid a<1\}
, the open ball topology of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is
\{\varnothing,[0,1]\}\cup\{[0,a)\mid a>0\}
. It is readily verified that a net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}
if and only if it is Cauchy in the usual sense, if and only if it is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
. Thus, both
{\sf V}
and
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
are Smyth complete.

Case 3.
\&
is isomorphic to the product t-norm. In this case, the open ball topology of
{\sf V}
is
\{\varnothing,[0,1]\}\cup\{(a,1]\mid a<1\}
, the open ball topology of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is
\{\varnothing,\{0\},[0,1]\}\cup\{[0,a)\mid a>0\}
. Since the sequence
\{1/n\}_{n\geq 1}
is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
, but not convergent in its symmetrization,
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is not Smyth complete. Since a net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is forward Cauchy in
{\sf V}
if and only if it is eventually constant or converges (in the usual sense) to some element other than
0
, it follows that
{\sf V}
is Smyth complete.

The following theorem says that Smyth completeness of a real-enriched category can be characterized purely in terms of its categorical structure, without resort to its symmetrization and open ball topology.

Theorem 11.4
.

(Yu and Zhang
[
90
]
)
For each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is Smyth complete.

(2)

Every forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of
X
has a unique bilimit.

(3)

X
is separated and every ideal of
X
is representable.

(4)

X
is Cauchy complete and every ideal of
X
is a Cauchy weight.

In this case,
X
is Yoneda complete.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
By Proposition
11.2
, every forward Cauchy net of a Smyth complete real-enriched category is a Cauchy net, then the conclusion follows from Lemma
8.11
.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
It suffices to show that every ideal
\phi
of
X
is a Cauchy weight and has a colimit. By Proposition
9.13
there is a forward Cauchy net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
of
X
such that

\phi=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(-,x_{j}).

By assumption, there is some
a\in X
such that for all
x\in X
,

\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x,x_{j})=X(x,a),\quad\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)=X(a,x).

Putting
x=a
one sees that
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a Cauchy net of
X
, then
\phi
is a Cauchy weight by Lemma
9.8
, and consequently,
a
is a colimit of
\phi
by the argument of Proposition
8.13
.

(3)\Rightarrow(4)
Obvious.

(4)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X
. Since every ideal of
X
is a Cauchy weight, by Lemma
9.8
the net
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is Cauchy, so it has a unique bilimit by Cauchy completeness of
X
and the argument of Proposition
8.13
, then by Lemma
8.11
, it converges uniquely in the open ball topology of the symmetrization of
X
.
∎

Corollary 11.5
.

A Yoneda complete real-enriched category
X
is Smyth complete if and only if
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}X
is Yoneda continuous.

Corollary 11.6
.

For each real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is Cauchy complete.

(2)

The symmetrization of
X
is Smyth complete.

(3)

The symmetrization of
X
is Cauchy complete.

A separated real-enriched category
X
is
Smyth completable
if there is a fully faithful functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
with
Y
Smyth complete.

Proposition 11.7
.

(Yu and Zhang
[
90
]
)
For a separated real-enriched category
X
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is Smyth completable.

(2)

Every ideal of
X
is a Cauchy weight.

(3)

Every forward Cauchy net of
X
is a Cauchy net.

(4)

Every ideal of
\mathcal{I}X
is representable, hence
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{I}X)=\mathcal{I}X
.

Proof.

The equivalence
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
follows from Proposition
9.13
and Lemma
9.8
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Suppose
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is fully faithful with
Y
being Smyth complete. Let
\phi
be an ideal of
X
. Then
\phi\circ f^{*}
is an ideal, hence a Cauchy weight of
Y
. Let
\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
be a left adjoint of
\phi\circ f^{*}\colon Y{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
. We show that
f^{*}\circ\psi\colon\star{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
is a left adjoint of
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
. First, since
\psi\dashv\phi\circ f^{*}
, then
\phi\circ(f^{*}\circ\psi)=(\phi\circ f^{*})\circ\psi\geq 1
. Next,

\displaystyle\psi\dashv\phi\circ f^{*}

\displaystyle\implies\psi\circ\phi\circ f^{*}\leq Y

\displaystyle\implies\psi\circ\phi\leq f_{*}

\displaystyle(f^{*}\circ f_{*}=X)

\displaystyle\implies(f^{*}\circ\psi)\circ\phi\leq X.

\displaystyle(f^{*}\circ f_{*}=X)

Therefore,
f^{*}\circ\psi
is a left adjoint of
\phi
.

(2)\Rightarrow(4)
Since
\mathcal{I}X=\mathcal{C}X
by assumption, it suffices to check that every ideal of
\mathcal{C}X
is representable. Suppose
\phi\colon\mathcal{C}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
is an ideal of
\mathcal{C}X
. By Lemma
8.5
,
{\sf c}_{*}\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{C}X
is an isomorphism in the category of distributors, it follows that
\phi\circ{\sf c}_{*}\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\mathcal{C}X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}\star
is an ideal, hence a Cauchy weight of
X
. Again by that
{\sf c}_{*}
is an isomorphism,
\phi
is a Cauchy weight of
\mathcal{C}X
. Therefore,
\phi
is representable since
\mathcal{C}X
is Cauchy complete.

(4)\Rightarrow(1)
Since every ideal of
\mathcal{I}X
is representable, then
\mathcal{I}X
is Smyth complete and consequently,
X
is Smyth completable. ∎

Corollary 11.8
.

A separated real-enriched category is Smyth completable if and only if its Cauchy completion is Smyth complete.

Proof.

Sufficiency is clear, for necessity assume that
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a fully faithful functor with
Y
Smyth complete. Since
Y
is Cauchy complete, there is a unique functor
\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{C}X\longrightarrow Y
that extends
f
. It is not hard to check that
\overline{f}
is also fully faithful, hence
\mathcal{C}X
is Smyth completable. By Theorem
11.7
every ideal of
\mathcal{C}X
is Cauchy, hence representable by Cauchy completeness of
\mathcal{C}X
, and consequently,
\mathcal{C}X
is Smyth complete. ∎

12.
The presheaf monad

In this section we show that the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}
is a monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, the subfunctors
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{I}
are its submonads.

For each real-enriched category
X
, let
{\sf m}_{X}
be the functor

{\colim}\colon\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X

that maps each weight
\Phi
of
\mathcal{P}X
to its colimit. Then,
{\sf m}
is a natural transformation
\mathcal{P}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
and the triple

\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})

is a monad, where
{\sf y}
denotes the natural transformation from the identity functor to
\mathcal{P}
given by Yoneda embeddings. We call
\mathbb{P}
the presheaf monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. Instead of checking directly that
(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
satisfies the requirements for a monad, we show that it arises from an adjunction.

Let

[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Sup}

denote the category with separated and cocomplete real-enriched categories as objects and left adjoints as morphisms.

Theorem
6.20
implies that the assignment

f\colon X\longrightarrow Y~\mapsto~f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y

defines a functor

\mathcal{P}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Sup}

that is left adjoint to the forgetful functor

U\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Sup}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}.

The monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
defined by the adjunction
\mathcal{P}\dashv U
is precisely the presheaf monad
\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
.

The monad
\mathbb{P}
is a typical example of KZ-monads in a locally ordered category. A category
\mathscr{C}
is
locally ordered
if (i) the home-set
\mathscr{C}(A,B)
is an ordered set for any objects
A
and
B
; and (ii) the composition preserves order. A 2-functor between locally ordered categories is a functor that preserves order on hom-sets. The functor
\mathcal{P}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is clearly a 2-functor. Locally ordered categories are special cases of 2-categories, we refer to Borceux
[
10
]
or Lack
[
45
]
for 2-categories and 2-functors.

In a locally ordered category, we say that a morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
is left adjoint to a morphism
g\colon B\longrightarrow A
if
g\circ f\geq 1_{A}
and
f\circ g\leq 1_{B}
. In this case, we write
f\dashv g
. The following
proposition, extracted from Kock
[
42
]
and Zöberlein
[
92
]
, is taken from Hofmann
[
31
]
.

Proposition 12.1
.

Let
(T,m,e)
be a monad in a locally ordered category
\mathscr{C}
with
T
a
2
-functor.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

Te_{X}\leq e_{TX}
for all objects
X
.

(2)

Te_{X}\dashv m_{X}
for all objects
X
.

(3)

m_{X}\dashv e_{TX}
for all objects
X
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Since
m_{X}\circ Te_{X}=1_{TX}
, it remains to check that
Te_{X}\circ m_{X}\leq 1_{T^{2}X}
.
This is easy since

Te_{X}\circ m_{X}=m_{TX}\circ T^{2}e_{X}\leq m_{TX}\circ Te_{TX}=1_{T^{2}X}.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Since
Te_{X}\dashv m_{X}
, then

Te_{X}=Te_{X}\circ m_{X}\circ e_{TX}\leq 1_{T^{2}X}\circ e_{TX}=e_{TX}.

(1)\Leftrightarrow(3)
Similar to
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
.
∎

A monad in a locally-ordered category is of
Kock-Zöberlein type
, or a KZ-monad, if it satisfies one (hence all) of the equivalent conditions in Proposition
12.1
. A KZ-monad is also called a
KZ-doctrine
in the literature.

Proposition 12.2
.

Suppose
(T,m,e)
is a KZ-monad in a locally ordered category. Then, for each object
X
and each morphism
h\colon TX\longrightarrow X
, the pair
(X,h)
is a
T
-algebra if and only if
h\circ e_{X}=1_{X}
; in which case
h\dashv e_{X}
.

Proof.

If
(X,h)
is a
T
-algebra, then
h\circ e_{X}=1_{X}
by definition of
T
-algebra. Conversely, suppose that
h\circ e_{X}=1_{X}
. Then

h\circ Th\leq h\circ Th\circ e_{TX}\circ m_{X}=h\circ e_{X}\circ h\circ m_{X}=h\circ m_{X}.

Since
T
is a KZ-monad, it follows that
Te_{X}\circ m_{X}\leq 1_{TX}
, hence

h\circ Th\geq h\circ Th\circ Te_{X}\circ m_{X}=h\circ T(h\circ e_{X})\circ m_{X}=h\circ m_{X}.

Therefore,
h\circ Th=h\circ m_{X}
and consequently,
(X,h)
is a

T
-algebra.

It remains to check that
h\dashv e_{X}
for each
T
-algebra
(X,h)
. Since
T
is of Kock-Zöberlein type,

e_{X}\circ h=Th\circ e_{TX}\geq Th\circ Te_{X}=T(h\circ e_{X})=1_{TX}.

Therefore
h\dashv e_{X}
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 12.3
.

The presheaf monad
\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is a KZ-monad in the locally ordered category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

Proof.

By Example
6.5
, for each real-enriched category
X
we have

\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}\dashv{\sf m}_{X}\dashv{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X},

the conclusion thus follows.
∎

Since
(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is a KZ-monad, for each real-enriched category
A
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

There is a functor
h\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow A
such that
(A,h)
is a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra.

(2)

The Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
has a left inverse.

(3)

A
is separated and the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{A}
has a left adjoint.

In this case, the
\mathbb{P}
-algebra structure map
h\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow A
is a left adjoint of
{\sf y}_{A}
, hence
h
maps each weight of
A
to its colimit. Therefore, a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra is a separated and cocomplete real-enriched category; a
\mathbb{P}
-homomorphism is a left adjoint between separated cocomplete real-enriched categories. All told,

\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\sf Alg}=[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Sup}.

From this fact one immediately infers:

Proposition 12.4
.

The forgetful functor
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is monadic.

Dually, we have a copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger},{\sf m}^{\dagger},{\sf y}^{\dagger})
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. For each real-enriched category
X
, let

{\sf m}^{\dagger}_{X}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X

be the functor that sends each coweight of
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
to its limit. Then

{\sf m}^{\dagger}\colon(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger})^{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}

is a natural transformation and

\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger},{\sf m}^{\dagger},{\sf y}^{\dagger})

is a monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, called the copresheaf monad. The copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
is co-KZ in the sense that for each real-enriched category
X
,

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{X}\vdash{\sf m}^{\dagger}_{X}\vdash{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X.

Since
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
is co-KZ, a
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra is a separated real-enriched category
A
such that the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}A
has a right adjoint. Therefore, the category

\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}

of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebras and
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-homomorphisms is precisely the category of separated and complete real-enriched categories and right adjoint functors.
Mapping each
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
to its right adjoint
\overline{f}\colon B\longrightarrow A
defines an isomorphism of categories

(\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg})^{\rm op}\cong\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

Theorem
12.6
below characterizes
\mathbb{P}
-algebras as (left)
[0,1]
-modules.

Definition 12.5
.

(Joyal and Tierney
[
38
]
) A (left)
[0,1]
-module is a pair
(X,\otimes)
, where
X
is a complete lattice and
\otimes
is a
[0,1]
-action on
X
. A
[0,1]
-module homomorphism
f\colon(X,\otimes)\longrightarrow(Y,\otimes)
is a join-preserving map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
such that
r\otimes f(x)=f(r\otimes x)
for all
r\in[0,1]
and
x\in X
.

With
[0,1]
-modules and
[0,1]
-module homomorphisms we have a category

[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Mod}.

Theorem 12.6
.

(Stubbe
[
77
]
)

The category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Mod}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Proof.

If
X
is a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra, then the
[0,1]
-action
(X_{0},\otimes)
is a
[0,1]
-module. If
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a homomorphism between
\mathbb{P}
-algebras, then
f\colon(X_{0},\otimes)\longrightarrow(Y_{0},\otimes)
is a
[0,1]
-module homomorphism since any left adjoint preserves tensors.
Conversely, if
(X,\otimes)
is a
[0,1]
-module, then the real-enriched category
(X,\alpha_{\otimes})
is separated, order-complete, tensored and cotensored, hence a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra. If
f\colon(X,\otimes)\longrightarrow(Y,\otimes)
is a
[0,1]
-module homomorphism, then the map
f\colon(X,\alpha_{\otimes})\longrightarrow(Y,\alpha_{\otimes})
preserves tensors and joins (with respect to the underlying order), hence a left adjoint by Corollary
7.9
.
The conclusion thus follows. ∎

Every
\mathbb{P}
-algebra
A
is clearly a quotient of the free algebra
\mathcal{P}A
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. The following result shows that whether every
\mathbb{P}
-algebra can be written as a subalgebra of a free one depends on the structure of the truth-value set
([0,1],\&,1)
.

Proposition 12.7
.

(Lai and Zhang
[
49
]
)
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The continuous t-norm
\&
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm.

(2)

Each
\mathbb{P}
-algebra
A
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{P}X
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
for some real-enriched category
X
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Let
A
be a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra. Since the coYoneda embedding
{\sf y}^{\dagger}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}A
is a fully faithful left adjoint, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}A
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}A
. Since
\&
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm, it is readily verified that

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A,\quad\psi\mapsto\psi\rightarrow 0

is an isomorphism.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
It suffices to show that
(r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0\leq r
for all
r\in[0,1]
. Consider the
\mathbb{P}
-algebra
{\sf V}^{\rm op}=([0,1],\alpha_{R})
. By assumption, there is a real-enriched category
X
and a fully faithful left adjoint
f\colon{\sf V}^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
. Since
f
is a left adjoint and
1
is the least element of
{\sf V}^{\rm op}_{0}
,
f(1)
must be the least element of
(\mathcal{P}X)_{0}
, namely the weight
0_{X}
of
X
with constant value
0
. Since
f
preserves tensor and the tensor of
r
with
x
in
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is
r\rightarrow x
, it follows that for each
r\in[0,1]
,

r\otimes f(r)=f(r\rightarrow r)=f(1)=0_{X},

where
\otimes
denotes tensor in
\mathcal{P}X
. Thus,
f(r)\leq r\rightarrow 0_{X}
and consequently,

\displaystyle f(((r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow r)

\displaystyle=((r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0)\otimes f(r)

\displaystyle\leq((r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0)\otimes(r\rightarrow 0_{X})

\displaystyle\leq 0_{X},

where the last inequality holds because
r\rightarrow 0_{X}
is the constant function with value
r\rightarrow 0
. Since
f
is injective, one has
((r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow r=1
, hence
(r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0\leq r
, as desired. ∎

A
class of weights
is defined to be a subfunctor
\mathcal{T}
of the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}
through which the natural transformation
{\sf y}\colon{\rm id}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
factors. In other words, a class of weights is a functor

\mathcal{T}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}

subject to the following conditions:

(i)

for each real-enriched category
X
,
\mathcal{T}X
is a subset of
\mathcal{P}X
together with category structure inherited from
\mathcal{P}X
;

(ii)

for each real-enriched category
X
,
\mathcal{T}X
contains all representable weights of
X
;

(iii)

for each functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
and each
\phi\in\mathcal{T}X
,
\mathcal{T}(f)(\phi)=f_{\exists}(\phi)
.

Condition (ii) implies that for each real-enriched category
X
, the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
factors through
\mathcal{T}X
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
and
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{T}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
is the inclusion natural transformation. If the natural transformation
{\sf m}\circ(\mathfrak{i}*\mathfrak{i})
factors (uniquely) through
\mathfrak{i}
, where
\mathfrak{i}*\mathfrak{i}
is the horizontal composite of
\mathfrak{i}
with itself, then we say that
\mathcal{T}
is
closed under multiplication
.

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{{\sf m}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{i}*\mathfrak{i}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{i}}

In this case, we also use the symbol
{\sf m}
to denote the unique natural transformation
\mathcal{T}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}
that makes the above square commutative.

If
\mathcal{T}
is closed under multiplication, then
(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is a submonad of
(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
, with the inclusion transformation
\mathfrak{i}
being a monad morphism, see e.g. Manes
[
61
]
. In this case, we also say simply that the functor
\mathcal{T}
is a submonad of
(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
.

Proposition 12.8
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{T}
is closed under multiplication, hence a submonad
(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
.

(2)

For each real-enriched category
X
,
\mathcal{T}X
is closed in
\mathcal{P}X
under formation of
\mathcal{T}
-colimits; that is, for each
\Phi\in\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X
, the colimit of the inclusion functor
\mathfrak{i}_{X}\colon\mathcal{T}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
\Phi
belongs to
\mathcal{T}X
.

(3)

For each real-enriched category
X
and each
\Phi\in\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X
, it holds that
\Phi\circ{\sf y}_{*}\in\mathcal{T}X
, where
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}X
is the Yoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{T}X
.

Proof.

This follows directly from that for each
\Phi\in\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X
,

(\mathfrak{i}*\mathfrak{i})_{X}(\Phi)=\Phi\circ\mathfrak{i}_{X}^{*}

and that

\displaystyle{\colim}_{\Phi}\mathfrak{i}_{X}

\displaystyle={\colim}(\Phi\circ\mathfrak{i}_{X}^{*})=\Phi\circ\mathfrak{i}_{X}^{*}\circ(\mathfrak{i}_{X})_{*}\circ{\sf y}_{*}=\Phi\circ{\sf y}_{*}.\qed

Definition 12.9
.

A class of weights
\mathcal{T}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is saturated
if it satisfies one, hence all, of the equivalent conditions in Proposition
12.8
.

In other words, a saturated class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is a submonad of the presheaf monad
\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
.
For a saturated class of weights
\mathcal{T}
, we write
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
for the corresponding monad, which is a submonad of the preheaf monad. The monad
\mathbb{T}
is of Kock-Zöberlein type since so is
\mathbb{P}
. Therefore, for the Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
:

(i)

an object is a separated real-enriched category
A
such that every
\phi\in\mathcal{T}A
has a colimit;

(ii)

a morphism is a functor that preserves
\mathcal{T}
-colimits.

Proposition 12.10
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a saturated class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. Then, every retract of a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra, where
\mathbb{T}
denotes the monad
(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
.

Proof.

Suppose
B
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra;
s\colon A\longrightarrow B
and
r\colon B\longrightarrow A
are functors with
r\circ s=1_{A}
. We wish to show that
A
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. Let
{\sf y}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
be the Yoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{T}A
; and let
\colim_{A}
be the composite

\mathcal{T}A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.45197pt\raise 5.39168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}s}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.1933pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.3866pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{T}B\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.48796pt\raise 6.975pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96945pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\colim}_{B}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.7375pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.475pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>B\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.48068pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.44481pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.88962pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>A.

Then

{\colim}_{A}\circ{\sf y}_{A}=r\circ{\colim}_{B}\circ\mathcal{T}s\circ{\sf y}_{A}=r\circ{\colim}_{B}\circ{\sf y}_{B}\circ s=r\circ s=1_{A},

hence
\colim_{A}
is a left inverse of
{\sf y}_{A}
and consequently,
A
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. ∎

For each real-enriched category
X
, since
\mathcal{T}X
is closed in
\mathcal{P}X
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
-colimits, it follows that
\mathcal{T}X
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra and the the assignment

f\colon X\longrightarrow Y\quad\mapsto\quad f_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{T}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}Y

defines a functor

\mathcal{T}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

Proposition 12.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a saturated class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. Then, the functor

\mathcal{T}\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}

is left adjoint to the forgetful functor

U\colon\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}.

Hence for each real-enriched category
X
,
\mathcal{T}X
is the free
\mathcal{T}
-cocompletion of
X
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a functor with
Y
a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. It is readily verified that

\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{T}X\longrightarrow Y,\quad\phi\mapsto{\colim}_{\phi}f

is the unique
\mathbb{T}
-homomorphism that makes the diagram

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf y}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}X}
\textstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}

commutative, the conclusion thus follows.
∎

The monad defined by the adjunction
\mathcal{T}\dashv U
is precisely
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
. Furthermore, the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is monadic.

Example 12.12
.

(i)

By Theorem
8.6
the class of Cauchy weights is saturated.
The resulting submonad
\mathbb{C}=(\mathcal{C},{\sf m},{\sf y})
of
\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is called the
Cauchy weight monad
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. A
\mathbb{C}
-algebra is a Cauchy complete real-enriched category; a
\mathbb{C}
-homomorphism is just a functor between Cauchy complete real-enriched categories (colimits of Cauchy weights are preserved by all functors).

(ii)

By Theorem
9.17
, ideals of real-enriched categories constitute a saturated class of weights. The monad
\mathbb{I}=(\mathcal{I},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is called the
ideal monad
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. An algebra of
\mathbb{I}
is a Yoneda complete real-enriched category; an
\mathbb{I}
-homomorphism is a Yoneda continuous functor between Yoneda complete real-enriched categories. Corollary
9.12
shows that restricting domains and codomains of the functors
\omega
and
(\text{-})_{0}
gives us an adjunction between the category of dcpos and that of
\mathbb{I}
-algebras.

(iii)

By Theorem
10.6
the class of flat weights is saturated. The resulting submonad
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is called the
flat weight monad
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. An
\mathbb{F}
-algebra is a flat complete real-enriched category; an
\mathbb{F}
-homomorphism is a functor between flat complete real-enriched categories that preserves colimits of flat weights. Likewise, by Theorem
10.7
the class of conically flat weights is saturated. The resulting submonad
\mathbb{F}_{\rm c}=(\mathcal{F}_{\rm c},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is called the
conically flat weight monad
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

(iv)

For each real-enriched category
X
, let
\mathcal{B}X=\{r\with{\sf y}(x)\mid x\in X,r\in[0,1]\}
. Then
\mathcal{B}
is a saturated class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. To see this, it suffices to observe that for all
x\in X
and all
r,s\in[0,1]
, the colimit of the inclusion functor
\mathcal{B}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
weighted by
s\with\mathcal{B}X(-,r\with{\sf y}(x))
is the weight
(s\with r)\with{\sf y}(x)
of
X
. Write
\mathbb{B}=(\mathcal{B},{\sf m},{\sf y})
for the resulting monad. A
\mathbb{B}
-algebra is precisely a separated and tensored real-enriched category; a
\mathbb{B}
-homomorphism is a functor that preserves tensors.

(v)
