(Stubbe
[
79
]
) The class of conical weights is saturated, algebras for the resulting monad are the separated and conically cocomplete real-enriched categories.

Theorem 12.13

(Morita equivalence)
.

(Kelly
[
39
]
)
For all real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is isomorphic to
Y
in
[0,1]
-
Dist
.

(2)

\mathcal{C}X
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}Y
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, hence in
\mathbb{C}
-
Alg
.

(3)

\mathcal{P}X
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}Y
in
\mathbb{P}
-
Alg
.

Suppose
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
is a distributor. Since the functor

\phi_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X,\quad\xi\mapsto\xi\circ\phi

is left adjoint to

\phi_{\forall}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y,\quad\lambda\mapsto\lambda\swarrow\phi,

it follows that the assignment

\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y~\mapsto~\phi_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X

defines a functor

\mathfrak{P}\colon([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Dist})^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

Lemma 12.14
.

The functor
\mathfrak{P}\colon([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Dist})^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
is full and faithful.

Proof.

Since for each distributor
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
, each
x\in X
and each
y\in Y
,

\phi_{\exists}({\sf y}_{Y}(y))(x)={\sf y}_{Y}(y)\circ\phi(x)=\phi(x,y),

it follows that
\mathfrak{P}
is faithful.

To see that
\mathfrak{P}
is full, assume that
f\colon\mathcal{P}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is a left adjoint. Define a distributor
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
by
\phi={\sf y}_{Y}^{*}\circ f^{*}\circ({\sf y}_{X})_{*}
; that is,

\phi(x,y)=f({\sf y}_{Y}(y))(x).

Then, for all
y\in Y
,

\phi_{\exists}({\sf y}_{Y}(y))={\sf y}_{Y}(y)\circ\phi=f({\sf y}_{Y}(y)).

Since the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{Y}\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y
is colimit-dense and both
\phi_{\exists}
and
f
preserve colimits, it follows that
f=\phi_{\exists}
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
12.13
.

Firsr we show that (1) is equivalent to (2). If
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
is an isomorphism in
[0,1]
-
Dist
, then

-\circ\phi\colon\mathcal{C}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}X

is an isomorphism in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. Conversely, if
f\colon\mathcal{C}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}Y
is an isomorphism in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, then the composite

X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.48717pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{({\sf y}_{X})_{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.54036pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.08072pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.46114pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.89346pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.78693pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}Y\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.47423pt\raise 6.35265pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\sf y}_{Y}^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.09335pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.1867pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Y

is an isomorphism in
[0,1]
-
Dist
, where
{\sf y}_{X}
is the Yoneda embedding for
X
with codomain resricted to
\mathcal{C}X
; likewise for
{\sf y}_{Y}
.

Next we show that (1) is equivalent to (3). If
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
is an isomorphism in
[0,1]
-
Dist
, then

\phi_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X

is an isomorphism in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Conversely, assume that
f\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y
is an isomorphism in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. Since the functor
\mathfrak{P}
is full, there exist distributors
\phi\colon Y{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
and
\psi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
such that
f=\phi_{\exists}
and
f^{-1}=\psi_{\exists}
. Since the functor
\mathfrak{P}
is faithful, it follows that
\psi\circ\phi=Y
and
\phi\circ\psi=X
. Therefore
X
and
Y
are isomorphic in
[0,1]
-
Dist
. ∎

The Morita equivalence can be strengthened to the following:

Theorem 12.15
.

(Kelly and Schmitt
[
40
]
)

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a saturated class of weights that contains all Cauchy weights. Then, for all real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is isomorphic to
Y
in
[0,1]
-
Dist
.

(2)

\mathcal{T}X
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}Y
in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)

Consider the isomorphism

\psi_{\exists}\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y,\quad\xi\mapsto\xi\circ\psi

in the proof of Theorem
12.13
. If we can prove that
\xi\circ\psi\in\mathcal{T}Y
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{T}X
, then restricting the domain and the codomain of
\psi_{\exists}
would yield an isomorphism
\mathcal{T}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}Y
.

For each
x\in X
, let
f(x)
be the weight
\psi(-,x)
of
Y
. Since
\psi
is an isomorphism in
[0,1]
-
Dist
,
f(x)
is a Cauchy weight. Hence we obtain a functor
f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}Y
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is saturated, the colimit of the composite functor
\mathfrak{i}_{Y}\circ f\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}Y
weighted by
\xi
belongs to
\mathcal{T}Y
,
then

\displaystyle\xi\circ\psi=\sup_{x\in X}\xi(x)\with\psi(-,x)=\sup_{x\in X}\xi(x)\with f(x)=\colim_{\xi}(\mathfrak{i}_{Y}\circ f)\in\mathcal{T}Y,

as desired.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
We say that a distributor
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
is of type
\mathcal{T}
if
\phi(-,y)\in\mathcal{T}X
for all
y\in Y
. In particular, a weight
\xi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}*
is of type
\mathcal{T}
if and only if
\xi\in\mathcal{T}X
. Since
\mathcal{T}
contains Cauchy weights, every isomorphism in
[0,1]
-
Dist
is of type
\mathcal{T}
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is saturated,
\mathcal{T}
-distributors of type
\mathcal{T}
are closed under composition. Therefore, they form a subcategory of
[0,1]
-
Dist
, denoted by
\mathcal{T}\text{-}{\bf Dist}
.
A slight improvement of the argument in Lemma
12.14
shows that the correspondence

\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y~\mapsto~-\mathrel{\circ}\phi\colon\mathcal{T}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}X

defines a full and faithful functor

(\mathcal{T}\text{-}{\bf Dist})^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg},

which implies that if
\mathcal{T}X
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}Y
in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, then
X
is isomorphic to
Y
in
[0,1]
-
Dist
.
∎

The characterization of Cauchy completion in Proposition
8.8
can be strengthened as follows.
Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a saturated class of weights that contains all Cauchy weights. For each real-enriched category
X
, let
{\sf t}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}X
be the Yoneda embedding with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{T}X
. Then, by commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}{\sf t}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf t}_{\mathcal{T}X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{i}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}X}
\scriptstyle{(\mathfrak{i}*\mathfrak{i})_{X}}

one sees that the Cauchy completion of
X
is the equalizer of
\mathcal{T}{\sf t}_{X}
and
{\sf t}_{\mathcal{T}X}
in the category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

13.
The fuzzy powerset monad

Proposition
12.4
says that the forgetful functor
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
is monadic. This section shows that the forgetful functor
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
is also monadic. We show that
\mathbb{P}
-algebras are precisely the Eilenberg-Moore algebras of the fuzzy powerset monad in the category of sets.

The (covariant) fuzzy powerset monad (see e.g. Höhle
[
29
]
and Manes
[
60
]
)

(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})

in the category of sets is defined as follows:

•

the (covariant) fuzzy powerset functor
\mathscr{P}
sends each set
X
to
[0,1]^{X}
and sends each map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
to
\mathscr{P}(f)\colon[0,1]^{X}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{Y}
, where for all
\gamma\in[0,1]^{X}
and
y\in Y
,

\mathscr{P}(f)(\gamma)(y)=f_{\exists}(\gamma)(y)=\sup\{\gamma(x)\mid f(x)=y\};

•

for each set
X
, the unit
{\sf e}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow[0,1]^{X}
sends each
x\in X
to
1_{x}
;

•

for each set
X
, the multiplication
{\sf m}_{X}\colon[0,1]^{[0,1]^{X}}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{X}
sends each
\Lambda\colon[0,1]^{X}\longrightarrow[0,1]
to

\sup_{\gamma\in[0,1]^{X}}\Lambda(\gamma)\with\gamma.

Instead of verifying directly that the triple
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
is a monad, we show that it is induced by an adjunction between the categories of sets and
[0,1]
-modules.

For each set
X
, consider the action

\otimes\colon[0,1]\times[0,1]^{X}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{X},\quad(r,\lambda)\mapsto r\with\lambda.

Then
([0,1]^{X},\otimes)
is a
[0,1]
-module and the assignment
X\mapsto([0,1]^{X},\otimes)
defines a functor

F\colon{\bf Set}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Mod}

that is left adjoint to the forgetful functor

U\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Mod}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}.

It is readily seen that
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
is the monad induced by the adjunction
F\dashv U
.

Suppose
(X,h)
is an algebra of the monad
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
. Since
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
contains the usual (covariant) powerset monad in
Set
as a submonad, then
X
together with the restriction of
h
to
2^{X}\subseteq[0,1]^{X}
is an algebra of the usual powerset monad, hence
X
is a complete lattice and
h
maps each subset of
X
to its join (see e.g. Mac Lane
[
59
, page 142]
). Define
\otimes\colon[0,1]\times X\longrightarrow X
by
r\otimes x=h(r_{x}).
Then
(X,\otimes)
is a
[0,1]
-module. Therefore, an algebra of the fuzzy powerset monad
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
is essentially a
[0,1]
-module, hence a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra. This proves:

Theorem 13.1
.

(Pedicchio and Tholen
[
66
]
)

The forgetful functor
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
is monadic.

In the following we consider the double contravriant fuzzy powerset monad in the category of sets. The
contravriant fuzzy powerset functor

\exp\colon{\bf Set}^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}

maps each
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
to

\exp f\colon[0,1]^{Y}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{X},\quad\mu\mapsto\mu\circ f.

It is readily verified that
\exp\colon{\bf Set}^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
is right adjoint to its opposite
\exp^{\rm op}\colon{\bf Set}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}^{\rm op}
. The unit and the counit of the adjunction
\exp^{\rm op}\dashv\exp
are given respectively by

\eta_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\exp\exp^{\rm op}X,\quad\eta_{X}(x)(\lambda)=\lambda(x)

and

\epsilon_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\exp^{\rm op}\exp X,\quad\epsilon_{X}(x)(\mu)=\mu(x).

The monad induced by the adjunction
\exp^{\rm op}\dashv\exp
is called
the double contravariant fuzzy powerset monad
in the category of sets. As we shall see, this monad contains the monad
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
as a submonad, so we write
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
for the monad defined by
\exp^{\rm op}\dashv\exp
. Explicitly, for each set
X
and each function
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
,

•

\mathscr{E}X
is the set of all functions
[0,1]^{X}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>[0,1]
;

•

\mathscr{E}(f)\colon\mathscr{E}X\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}Y
is given by
\mathscr{E}(f)(\Lambda)(\mu)=\Lambda(\mu\circ f)
for all
\Lambda\in\mathscr{E}X
and
\mu\in[0,1]^{Y}
;

•

the unit
{\sf e}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}(X)
is given by
{\sf e}_{X}(x)(\lambda)=\lambda(x)
for all
x\in X
and
\lambda\in[0,1]^{X}
;

•

the multiplication
{\sf m}_{X}\colon\mathscr{E}^{2}X\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}X
is given by
{\sf m}_{X}=\exp(\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}X})
; explicitly,
{\sf m}_{X}(\mathcal{H})(\lambda)=\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\lambda})
for all
\lambda\in[0,1]^{X}
and
\mathcal{H}\colon[0,1]^{\mathscr{E}X}\longrightarrow[0,1]
, where
\widehat{\lambda}\colon\mathscr{E}X\longrightarrow[0,1]
sends
\Lambda\in\mathscr{E}X
to
\Lambda(\lambda)
.

The (covariant) fuzzy powerset functor
\mathscr{P}\colon{\sf Set}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\sf Set}
is closely related to the contravariant fuzzy powerset functor
\exp\colon{\sf Set}^{\rm op}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\sf Set}
. Of particular interest is the following

Lemma 13.2
.

(i)

A map
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
between sets is injective if and only if
\exp(f)\circ\mathscr{P}(f)=1_{\exp X}
.

(ii)

For any pullback square in the category of sets, as displayed on the left,

\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{D}}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\exp g}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{C}}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{D}}
\scriptstyle{\mathscr{P}(j)}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{C}}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\exp f}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{A}}
\textstyle{[0,1]^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\mathscr{P}(h)}

the right square is commutative.

It is known that the covariant powerset monad in the category of sets is a submonad of the double contravariant powerset monad, see e.g. Manes
[
61
, page 79]
, the following proposition says this is also true for fuzzy powersets. For each set
X
consider the map

j_{X}\colon[0,1]^{X}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>[0,1]^{[0,1]^{X}},\quad j_{X}(\lambda)(\gamma)=\sub_{X}(\lambda,\gamma).

Then the assignment
X\mapsto j_{X}
is a natural transformation from the functor
\mathscr{P}
to the functor
\mathscr{E}
, since for each map
f\colon X\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>Y
we have

\displaystyle j_{Y}\circ\mathscr{P}(f)(\lambda)(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sub_{Y}(f_{\exists}(\lambda),\gamma))=\sub_{X}(\lambda,\gamma\circ f)=\mathscr{E}(f)(j_{X}(\lambda))(\gamma).

Proposition 13.3
.

(Lu and Zhang
[
58
]
)
The natural transformation
j
exhibits
(\mathscr{P},{\sf m},{\sf e})
as a submonad of
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
.

Proof.

It is clear that
j_{X}
is an injection for each set
X
, so it suffices to show that for each set
X
the following square is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathscr{P}X}
\textstyle{\mathscr{E}X}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}^{2}X}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf m}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}^{2}X}
\textstyle{\mathscr{E}^{2}X}
\scriptstyle{(j*j)_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{E}^{2}X}
\textstyle{\mathscr{E}X}
\scriptstyle{{\sf m}_{X}}

For this we compute: for all
\Lambda\in[0,1]^{[0,1]^{X}}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]^{X}
,

\displaystyle~\quad{\sf m}_{X}\circ(j*j)_{X}(\Lambda)(\lambda)

\displaystyle={\sf m}_{X}\circ j_{\mathscr{E}X}\circ\mathscr{P}(j_{X})(\Lambda)(\lambda)

\displaystyle(\text{Definition of $j*j$})

\displaystyle=j_{\mathscr{E}X}((j_{X})_{\exists}(\Lambda))(\widehat{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\Xi\in\mathscr{E}X}((j_{X})_{\exists}(\Lambda)(\Xi)\rightarrow\Xi(\lambda))

\displaystyle(\text{Definition of $j_{\mathscr{E}X}$})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in[0,1]^{X}}(\Lambda(\gamma)\rightarrow\sub_{X}(\gamma,\lambda))

\displaystyle((j_{X})_{\exists}(\Lambda)(\Xi)=\sup_{j_{X}(\gamma)=\Xi}\Lambda(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\sub_{X}\Big(\sup_{\gamma\in[0,1]^{X}}\Lambda(\gamma)\with\gamma,\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=j_{X}\circ{\sf m}_{X}(\Lambda)(\lambda).

The proof is completed. ∎

For each set
B
,
(\exp B,\exp(\epsilon_{B}))
is an algebra of the monad
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
. The following theorem says that all algebras of
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
are of this form.

Proposition 13.4
.

(Pu and Zhang
[
67
]
)

Every algebra of the double contravariant fuzzy powerset monad
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
is of the form
(\exp B,\exp(\epsilon_{B}))
for some set
B
.

Proof.

Suppose
(A,h)
is an algebra of
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
; that means,
A
is a set,
h\colon\mathscr{E}A\longrightarrow A
is a map such that
h\circ\eta_{A}=1_{A}
and that
h\circ{\sf m}_{A}=h\circ\mathscr{E}(h)
. We show that there is some set
B
such that
(A,h)
is isomorphic to
(\exp B,\exp(\epsilon_{B}))
.

Consider the pullback

\textstyle{\exp^{\rm op}A}
\textstyle{\exp^{\rm op}\exp\exp^{\rm op}A}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\exp^{\rm op}A}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\exp^{\rm op}A}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\exp^{\rm op}A}
\textstyle{\exp^{\rm op}\exp\exp^{\rm op}A}
\scriptstyle{\exp^{\rm op}(h)}

in the category of sets. We prove in three steps that
B
satisfies the requirement.

Step 1
.
i=i^{\prime}
. This follows immediately from the triangular identity

\exp^{\rm op}(\eta_{A})\circ\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}=1_{\exp^{\rm op}A}

and the equality

\exp^{\rm op}(\eta_{A})\circ\exp^{\rm op}(h)=\exp^{\rm op}(h\circ\eta_{A})=1_{\exp^{\rm op}A}.

So
i
is an equalizer of
\exp^{\rm op}(h)
and
\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}
.

Step 2
.
k_{A}\coloneqq\exp(i)\circ\eta_{A}\colon(A,h)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(\exp B,\exp(\epsilon_{B}))
is a homomorphism of algebras; that means,
k_{A}\circ h=\exp(\epsilon_{B})\circ\mathscr{E}(k_{A})
. To see this, we compute:

\displaystyle k_{A}\circ h

\displaystyle=\exp(i)\circ\eta_{A}\circ h

\displaystyle=\exp(i)\circ\mathscr{E}(h)\circ\eta_{\mathscr{E}A}

\displaystyle(\text{naturailty of $\eta$})

\displaystyle=\exp(\exp^{\rm op}(h))\circ i)\circ\eta_{\mathscr{E}A}

\displaystyle(\mathscr{E}=\exp\circ\exp^{\rm op})

\displaystyle=\exp(\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}\circ i)\circ\eta_{\mathscr{E}A}

\displaystyle(\text{$i$ equalizes $\exp^{\rm op}(h)$ and $\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}$})

\displaystyle=\exp(i)\circ\exp(\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A})\circ\eta_{\mathscr{E}A}

\displaystyle=\exp(i)\circ{\sf m}_{A}\circ\eta_{\mathscr{E}A}

\displaystyle({\sf m}_{A}=\exp(\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}))

\displaystyle=\exp(i)

\displaystyle({\sf m}_{A}\circ\eta_{\mathscr{E}A}=1_{\mathscr{E}A})

\displaystyle=\exp(i)\circ\mathscr{E}(h)\circ\mathscr{E}(\eta_{A})

\displaystyle(h\circ\eta_{A}=1_{A})

\displaystyle=\exp(\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}\circ i)\circ\mathscr{E}(\eta_{A})

\displaystyle(\text{$i$ equalizes $\exp^{\rm op}(h)$ and $\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}A}$})

\displaystyle=\exp(\exp^{\rm op}\exp(i)\circ\epsilon_{B})\circ\mathscr{E}(\eta_{A})

\displaystyle(\text{naturailty of $\epsilon$})

\displaystyle=\exp(\epsilon_{B})\circ\mathscr{E}(\exp(i))\circ\mathscr{E}(\eta_{A})

\displaystyle=\exp(\epsilon_{B})\circ\mathscr{E}(k_{A}).

Step 3
.
k_{A}\colon(A,h)\longrightarrow(\exp B,\exp(\epsilon_{B}))
is an isomorphism of algebras. It suffices to check that
k_{A}\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\exp B
is a bijection.

Let
l_{A}=h\circ\mathscr{P}(i)
. On the one hand, by the computation in Step 2 we have
k_{A}\circ h=\exp(i)
, then

\displaystyle k_{A}\circ l_{A}=k_{A}\circ h\circ\mathscr{P}(i)=\exp(i)\circ\mathscr{P}(i)=1_{\exp B},

where the last equality holds because
i\colon B\longrightarrow\exp^{\rm op}A
is injective.

On the other hand, by virtue of Step 1 and Lemma
13.2
(ii) one has that

\mathscr{P}(i)\circ\exp(i)=\exp(\epsilon_{\exp^{\rm op}}A)\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h))={\sf m}_{A}\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h)).

Since
h\circ\eta_{A}=1_{A}
, it follows that
\exp^{\rm op}(h)
is an injection,
hence

\mathscr{E}(h)\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h))=\exp(\exp^{\rm op}(h))\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h))=1_{\mathscr{E}A}

by Lemma
13.2
(i). Consequently,

\displaystyle l_{A}\circ k_{A}

\displaystyle=h\circ\mathscr{P}(i)\circ\exp(i)\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=h\circ{\sf m}_{A}\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h))\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle=h\circ\mathscr{E}(h)\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h))\circ\eta_{A}

(
(A,h)
is an algebra)

\displaystyle=h\circ\eta_{A}

\displaystyle(\mathscr{E}(h)\circ\mathscr{P}(\exp^{\rm op}(h))=1_{\mathscr{E}A})

\displaystyle=1_{A}.

Therefore,
k_{A}\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\exp B
is a bijection, as desired.
∎

14.
Categories of
\mathbb{T}
-algebras

In this section, unless otherwise specified,
\mathcal{T}
always denotes a saturated class of weights,
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
denotes the corresponding submonad of the presheaf monad
\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
.
This section concerns function spaces in the category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of
\mathbb{T}
-algebras.

For any real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
, let
[X,Y]
be the set of all functors from
X
to
Y
. For
f,g\in[X,Y]
, let

[X,Y](f,g)=\inf_{x\in X}Y(f(x),g(x)).

Then
[X,Y]
becomes a real-enriched category, called the functor category.

For any functors
f\colon X_{2}\longrightarrow X_{1}
and
g\colon Y_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{2}
, the assignment
h\mapsto g\circ h\circ f
defines a functor
[X_{1},Y_{1}]\longrightarrow[X_{2},Y_{2}]
. In this way we obtain a bifunctor

[-,-]\colon([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat})^{\rm op}\times[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}.

For any real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
, it is readily verified that
(X\times Y,\alpha)
is a real-enriched category, where

\alpha\colon X\times Y{\longrightarrow\hskip-12.48604pt{\mapstochar}\hskip 13.3472pt}X\times Y,\quad\alpha((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))=X(x_{1},x_{2})\with Y(y_{1},y_{2}).

The category
(X\times Y,\alpha)
is called the tensor product of
X
and
Y
and is denoted by
X\otimes Y
.
2
2

2

The symbol
\otimes
is used to denote both the tensor in a real-enriched category (i.e.
r\otimes x
) and the tensor product of real-enriched categories. Fortunately, it is easy to detect from the context which one is meant.
The assignment
(X,Y)\mapsto X\otimes Y
defines a bifunctor

\otimes\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\times[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}.

It is easily seen that the tensor product is symmetric and has the terminal real-enriched category
\star
as unit, and that for each real-enriched category
X
, the functor

-\otimes X\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}

is left adjoint to

[X,-]\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}.

Therefore, the triple
([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},\otimes,\star)
is a symmetric monoidal closed category.

Given real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
,
\phi\in\mathcal{P}X
and
\psi\in\mathcal{P}Y
, the function

\phi\otimes\psi\colon X\times Y\longrightarrow[0,1],\quad(x,y)\mapsto\phi(x)\with\psi(y)

is a weight of
X\otimes Y
. By continuity of the t-norm
\&
one verifies that the assignment
(\phi,\psi)\mapsto\phi\otimes\psi
defines a functor
\mathcal{P}X\otimes\mathcal{P}Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(X\otimes Y).
So, the presheaf functor
\mathcal{P}
is a lax functor on the symmetric monoidal closed category
([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},\otimes,\star)
.

Theorem 14.1
.

(Lai and Zhang
[
48
]
)
Assigning to each pair
(X,Y)
of
\mathbb{T}
-algebras the subcategory
\hom(X,Y)
of
[X,Y]
composed of
\mathbb{T}
-homomorphisms gives rise to a bifunctor

\hom\colon(\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg})^{\rm op}\times\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

Lemma 14.2
.

Suppose
X,Y
are real-enriched categories and
Y
is
\mathcal{T}
-cocomplete.

(i)

[X,Y]
is
\mathcal{T}
-cocomplete and
\mathcal{T}
-colimits in
[X,Y]
are computed pointwise; that means, for each
\Phi\in\mathcal{T}[X,Y]
, the map

h\colon X\longrightarrow Y,\quad h(x)={\colim}_{\Phi}[x]

is a colimit of
\Phi
, where
[x]
is the projection
[X,Y]\longrightarrow Y,~f\mapsto f(x)
.

(ii)

The subcategory
\hom(X,Y)
of
[X,Y]
consisting of functors that preserve
\mathcal{T}
-colimits is closed under
\mathcal{T}
-colimits, hence
\mathcal{T}
-cocomplete.

Proof.

(i) We prove the conclusion in two steps.

Step 1
.
h
is a functor. Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
. For each
y\in Y
, since

\Phi\circ[x_{1}]^{*}(y)=\sup_{f\in[X,Y]}\Phi(f)\with\thinspace[x_{1}]^{*}(y,f)=\sup_{f\in[X,Y]}\Phi(f)\with Y(y,f(x_{1})),

it follows that

\displaystyle\quad\quad\Phi\circ[x_{1}]^{*}(y)\rightarrow\Phi\circ[x_{2}]^{*}(y)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{f\in[X,Y]}(\Phi(f)\with Y(y,f(x_{1}))\rightarrow\Phi(f)\with Y(y,f(x_{2})))

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{f\in[X,Y]}(Y(y,f(x_{1}))\rightarrow Y(y,f(x_{2})))

\displaystyle\geq X(x_{1},x_{2}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle Y(h(x_{1}),h(x_{2}))

\displaystyle=Y({\colim}_{\Phi}[x_{1}],{\colim}_{\Phi}[x_{2}])

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{P}Y(\Phi\circ[x_{1}]^{*},\Phi\circ[x_{2}]^{*})

\displaystyle=\inf_{y\in Y}(\Phi\circ[x_{1}]^{*}(y)\rightarrow\Phi\circ[x_{2}]^{*}(y))

\displaystyle\geq X(x_{1},x_{2}),

so
h
is a functor.

Step 2
.
h
is a colimit of
\Phi
. For each
g\in[X,Y]
,

\displaystyle[X,Y](h,g)

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}Y(h(x),g(x))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}Y({\colim}_{\Phi}[x],g(x))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}\mathcal{P}Y(\Phi\circ[x]^{*},Y(-,g(x)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}\mathcal{P}[X,Y](\Phi,Y(-,g(x))\circ[x]_{*})

\displaystyle(-\circ[x]^{*}\dashv-\circ[x]_{*})

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}\inf_{f\in[X,Y]}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow Y(f(x),g(x)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in[X,Y]}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow[X,Y](f,g))

\displaystyle=[X,Y](-,g)\swarrow\Phi,

so
h
is a colimit of
\Phi
.

(ii) We prove that for each
\Phi\in\mathcal{T}\hom(X,Y)
, the functor

h\colon X\longrightarrow Y,\quad h(x)={\colim}_{\Phi}[x]

preserves
\mathcal{T}
-colimits, where
[x]
denotes the projection

\hom(X,Y)\longrightarrow Y,\quad f\mapsto f(x).

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{T}X
and
a
be a colimit of
\phi
. We show that
h(a)
is a colimit of
h_{\exists}(\phi)
. On the one hand,

\displaystyle Y(h(a),y)

\displaystyle=Y({\colim}_{\Phi}[a],y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}Y(\Phi\circ[a]^{*},Y(-,y))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(\hom(X,Y))(\Phi,Y(-,y)\circ[a]_{*})

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow Y(f(a),y))

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow Y({\colim}_{\phi}f,y))

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}Y(\phi\circ f^{*},Y(-,y)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}X(\phi,Y(-,y)\circ f_{*}))

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}\inf_{x\in X}(\Phi(f)\rightarrow(\phi(x)\rightarrow Y(f(x),y)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}\inf_{x\in X}(\Phi(f)\with\phi(x)\rightarrow Y(f(x),y)).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}Y(h_{\exists}(\phi),Y(-,y))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}X(\phi,h^{-1}(Y(-,y)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\rightarrow Y(h(x),y))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}Y(\Phi\circ[x]^{*},Y(-,y)))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}(\phi(x)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\hom(X,Y))(\Phi,Y(-,y)\circ[x]_{*}))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in X}\inf_{f\in\hom(X,Y)}(\Phi(f)\with\phi(x)\rightarrow Y(f(x),y)).

Therefore
Y(h(a),y)=\mathcal{P}Y(h_{\exists}(\phi),Y(-,y))
, so
h(a)
is a colimit of
h_{\exists}(\phi)
.∎

Proof of Theorem
14.1
.

By lemma
14.2
(ii), it suffices to show that

(i)

for any
\mathbb{T}
-algebras
X,Y,Z
and any
\mathbb{T}
-homomorphism
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
, the functor

\hom(X,f)\colon\hom(X,Y)\longrightarrow\hom(X,Z),\quad h\mapsto f\circ h

preserves
\mathcal{T}
-colimits;

(ii)

for any
\mathbb{T}
-algebras
X,Y,Z
and any
\mathbb{T}
-homomorphism
g\colon X\longrightarrow Y
, the functor

\hom(g,Z)\colon\hom(Y,Z)\longrightarrow\hom(X,Z),\quad h\mapsto h\circ g

preserves
\mathcal{T}
-colimits.

For (i), assume that
\Phi\in\mathcal{T}\hom(X,Y)
. For each
x\in X
and each
h\in\hom(X,Y)
, since

[x]\circ\hom(X,f)(h)=f\circ h(x)=f\circ[x](h)

and

\displaystyle\hom(X,f)(\colim\Phi)(x)

\displaystyle=f\circ\colim\Phi(x)

\displaystyle=f({\colim}_{\Phi}[x])

(definition of
\colim\Phi
)

\displaystyle={\colim}_{\Phi}(f\circ[x]),

(
f
preserves
\mathcal{T}
-colimits)

it follows that

\displaystyle({\colim}_{\Phi}\hom(X,f))(x)

\displaystyle={\colim}_{\Phi}([x]\circ\hom(X,f))

\displaystyle={\colim}_{\Phi}(f\circ[x])

\displaystyle=\hom(X,f)(\colim\Phi)(x),

hence
{\colim}_{\Phi}\hom(X,f)=\hom(X,f)(\colim\Phi)
,
as desired.

The assertion (ii) can be verified in a similar way. ∎

Theorem
14.1
leads to an interesting question in the theory of real-enriched categories:

Question 14.3
.

Does there exist a bifunctor

\Box\colon\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\times\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}

and a bijection

\hom(Z\Box X,Y)\cong\hom(Z,\hom(X,Y))

natural in all variables
X,Y
and
Z
?

If the answer is positive and
\Box
has a unit, say
I
, then the triple
(\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg},\Box,I)
is a monoidal closed category.
A general problem is to find subcategories
\mathscr{A}
of
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
that satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

Restricting the domain of the bifunctor

\hom\colon(\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg})^{\rm op}\times\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}

yields a bifunctor

\hom\colon\mathscr{A}^{\rm op}\times\mathscr{A}\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}.

That means, for any objects
X,Y,Z
and any morphisms
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
,
g\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
\mathscr{A}
,

•

the real-enriched category
\hom(X,Y)
composed of
\mathscr{A}
-morphisms belongs to
\mathscr{A}
;

•

both

\hom(X,f)\colon\hom(X,Y)\longrightarrow\hom(X,Z),\quad h\mapsto f\circ h

and

\hom(g,Z)\colon\hom(Y,Z)\longrightarrow\hom(X,Z),\quad h\mapsto h\circ g

are morphisms of
\mathscr{A}
.

(ii)

There exists a bifunctor

\Box\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{A}\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}

and a bijection

\hom(Z\Box X,Y)\cong\hom(Z,\hom(X,Y))

natural in all variables
X,Y
and
Z
.

By a result in category theory, see e.g. Mac Lane
[
59
, page 102, Theorem 3]
, in the presence of (i), condition (ii) is equivalent to that for each object
X
of
\mathscr{A}
, the functor

\hom(X,-)\colon\mathscr{A}\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}

has a left adjoint.

For the class of Cauchy weights, the class of ideals, and the largest class of weights, the answer to Question
14.3
is positive, as we see below.

Proposition 14.4
.

The triple
(\mathbb{C}\text{-}{\bf Alg},\otimes,\star)
is a symmetric monoidal closed category.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{C}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
is a full subcategory of the closed category
([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},\otimes,\star)
and the terminal real-enriched category
\star
is Cauchy complete, we only need to check that for all Cauchy complete real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
, both the tensor product
X\otimes Y
and the functor category
[X,Y]
are Cauchy complete. Cauchy completeness of
[X,Y]
follows from Theorem
14.1
. In the following we use the characterizations in Proposition
8.13
to verify that
X\otimes Y
is Cauchy complete. Suppose
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence of
X\otimes Y
. It is clear that
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence of
X
and
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence of
Y
. Suppose
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
a
and
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
b
. It is readily verified that
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
(a,b)
, so
X\otimes Y
is Cauchy complete. ∎

Theorem 14.5
.

(Lai and Zhang
[
50
]
)
The triple
(\mathbb{I}\text{-}{\bf Alg},\otimes,\star)
is a symmetric monoidal closed category.

Proof.

Since
([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},\otimes,\star)
is a monoidal closed category and
\star
is Yoneda complete, it suffices to show that for all Yoneda complete real-enriched categories
X,Y,Z
, it holds that

(i)

X\otimes Y
is Yoneda complete;

(ii)

the subset
\hom(X,Y)
of
[X,Y]
consisting of Yoneda continuous maps is Yoneda complete;

(iii)

f\colon X\otimes Y\longrightarrow Z
is Yoneda continuous if and only if
f\colon X\times Y\longrightarrow Z
is Yoneda continuous separately.

Assertion (ii) is contained in Theorem
14.1
. Assertions (i) and (iii) are contained, respectively, in Lemma
14.6
and Lemma
14.7
below.
∎

The following fact will be used in Lemma
14.6
and Lemma
14.7
. Suppose
X,Y
are real-enriched categories and
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a net of
X\otimes Y
. Then
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X\otimes Y
if and only if
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
Y
, in which case,

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j,k\leq l}X(x_{j},x_{l})\with Y(y_{k},y_{l})

\displaystyle=1,

where “
i\leq j,k\leq l
” means “
i\leq j\leq l
and
i\leq k\leq l
”

Lemma 14.6
.

If both of the real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
are Yoneda complete, then so is the tensor product
X\otimes Y
.

Proof.

We prove that for any real-enriched categories
X
and
Y
and any forward Cauchy net
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
of
X\otimes Y
, if
a
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
and
b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, then
(a,b)
is a Yoneda limit of
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. For this we check that for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,

X\otimes Y((a,b),(x,y))=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\with Y(y_{j},y).

On the one hand,

\displaystyle X\otimes Y((a,b),(x,y))

\displaystyle=X(a,x)\with Y(b,y)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}Y(y_{j},y)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\Big[\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\with\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}Y(y_{j},y)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\with Y(y_{j},y).

On the other hand,
since both
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in D}
are forward Cauchy, then

\displaystyle\quad\quad~\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\with Y(y_{j},y)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\with Y(y_{j},y)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j,k\leq l}X(x_{j},x_{l})\with Y(y_{k},y_{l})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j,k\leq l}X(x_{l},x)\with Y(y_{l},y)\with X(x_{j},x_{l})\with Y(y_{k},y_{l})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{k\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\with Y(y_{k},y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\Big[\Big(\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\with\Big(\inf_{k\geq i}Y(y_{k},y)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{k\geq i}Y(y_{k},y)\Big)

\displaystyle=X(a,x)\with Y(b,y)

\displaystyle=X\otimes Y((a,b),(x,y)).\qed

Lemma 14.7
.

Suppose
X,Y,Z
are Yoneda complete real-enriched categories. Then, a functor
f\colon X\otimes Y\longrightarrow Z
is Yoneda continuous if and only if
f\colon X\times Y\longrightarrow Z
is Yoneda continuous separately.

Proof.

For necessity we check that
f
is Yoneda continuous in the first variable for example. Let
b\in Y
and let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
be a forward Cauchy net of
X
with a Yoneda limit
a
. We show that
f(a,b)
is a Yoneda limit of
\{f(x_{i},b)\}_{i\in D}
. Because
f\colon X\otimes Y\longrightarrow Z
is Yoneda continuous, it suffices to check that
(a,b)
is a Yoneda limit of the forward Cauchy net
\{(x_{i},b)\}_{i\in D}
of
X\otimes Y
. This follows from that for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,

\displaystyle X\otimes Y((a,b),(x,y))

\displaystyle=X(a,x)\with Y(b,y)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X(x_{j},x)\Big)\with Y(b,y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}(X(x_{j},x)\with Y(b,y))

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X\otimes Y((x_{j},b),(x,y)).

For sufficiency suppose
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X\otimes Y
and
(a,b)
is a Yoneda limit of
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. We show that
f(a,b)
is a Yoneda limit of
\{f(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
. Since
Z
is Yoneda complete,
\{f(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
has a Yoneda limit, say
c
. We wish to show that
c
is isomorphic to
f(a,b)
.

Since

\displaystyle Z(c,f(a,b))

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}Z(f(x_{j},y_{j}),f(a,b))

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}X\otimes Y((x_{j},y_{j}),(a,b))

\displaystyle=X\otimes Y((a,b),(a,b))

\displaystyle=1,

it follows that
c\sqsubseteq f(a,b)
.
It remains to check that
f(a,b)\sqsubseteq c
. On the one hand, since
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
Y
, by Yoneda completeness of
X,Y
and Lemma
14.6
one sees that
a
is a Yoneda limit of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in D}
,
b
is a Yoneda limit of
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in D}
, then

\displaystyle Z(f(a,b),c)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}Z(f(x_{j},b),c)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\sup_{h\in D}\inf_{k\geq h}Z(f(x_{j},y_{k}),c)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{k\geq i}Z(f(x_{j},y_{k}),c).

On the other hand, since
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
is a forward Cauchy net of
X\otimes Y
and
c
is a Yoneda limit of the net
\{f(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in D}
, then

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=Z(c,c)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{l\geq i}Z(f(x_{l},y_{l}),c)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{l\geq i}Z(f(x_{l},y_{l}),c)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j,k\leq l}X\otimes Y((x_{j},y_{k}),(x_{l},y_{l}))\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{l\geq i}Z(f(x_{l},y_{l}),c)\Big)\with\Big(\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j,k\leq l}Z(f(x_{j},y_{k}),f(x_{l},y_{l}))\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{i\leq j,k\leq l}Z(f(x_{l},y_{l}),c)\with Z(f(x_{j},y_{k}),f(x_{l},y_{l}))

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in D}\inf_{j\geq i}\inf_{k\geq i}Z(f(x_{j},y_{k}),c).

Therefore,
Z(f(a,b),c)=1
and consequently,
f(a,b)\sqsubseteq c
.
∎

Proposition
14.4
and Theorem
14.5
show that the category
\mathbb{C}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras and the category
\mathbb{I}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of
\mathbb{I}
-algebras are closed in the monoidal closed category
([0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat},\otimes,\star)
with respect to various operations, so they are themselves monoidal closed categories. But, the situation for the largest class of weights, i.e. the class
\mathcal{P}
of all weights, is quite different.

Example 14.8
.

Let
\&
be the product t-norm on
[0,1]
. Then the tensor product
{\sf V}\otimes{\sf V}
of the complete real enriched category
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
with itself is not tensored. Otherwise, there is some
(a,b)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
, the tensor of
1/2
with
(1/2,1/2)
, such that

{\sf V}\otimes{\sf V}((a,b),(x,y))=1/2\rightarrow{\sf V}(1/2,x)\with{\sf V}(1/2,y)

for all
(x,y)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
.
Putting
x=1/4
and
y=1/2
gives that
a\leq 1/4
; putting
x=1/2
and
y=1/4
gives that
b\leq 1/4
. Then

{\sf V}\otimes{\sf V}((a,b),(1/4,1/4))=(a\rightarrow 1/4)\with(b\rightarrow 1/4)=1,

but

1/2\rightarrow{\sf V}(1/2,1/4)\with{\sf V}(1/2,1/4)=1/2,

a contradiction.

Theorem 14.9
.

(Joyal and Tierney
[
38
, page 9]
)

For each
\mathbb{P}
-algebra
X
, the functor
\hom(X,-)\colon\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
has a left adjoint.

Lemma 14.10
.

For all
\mathbb{P}
-algebras
X
and
Y
, the real-enriched categories
\hom(X,Y)
and
\hom(Y^{\rm op},X^{\rm op})
are isomorphic.

Proof.

For each
f\in\hom(X,Y)
, let
G(f)\colon Y^{\rm op}\longrightarrow X^{\rm op}
be the opposite of the right adjoint of
f
. We claim that
G\colon\hom(X,Y)\longrightarrow\hom(Y^{\rm op},X^{\rm op})
is an isomorphism. For this we check that
G
preserves tensors and joins (w.r.t. the underlying order).

We check that
G
preserves tensors for example. Let
f\in\hom(X,Y)
and
r\in[0,1]
. It is readily verified that if
g\colon Y\longrightarrow X
is a right adjoint of
f
, the correspondence
y\mapsto r\multimap g(y)
defines a right adjoint of
r\otimes f
, which shows that
G
preserves tensors. ∎

Proof of Theorem
14.9
.

First of all, we note that

\hom(Z,\hom(X,Y))\cong\hom(X,\hom(Z,Y))

for all
\mathbb{P}
-algebras
X,Y
and
Z
.

For each
\mathbb{P}
-algebra
Y
, let

Y\Box X=\hom(Y,X^{\rm op})^{\rm op}.

For each morphism
f\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, let

f\Box{\rm id}_{X}\colon Y\Box X\longrightarrow Z\Box X

be the opposite of the right adjoint of

\hom(f,X^{\rm op})\colon\hom(Z,X^{\rm op})\longrightarrow\hom(Y,X^{\rm op}).

The correspondence
f\mapsto f\Box{\rm id}_{X}
gives rise to a functor

-\Box X\colon\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

We claim that this functor is left adjoint to

\hom(X,-)\colon\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}.

For this, we show that for any
Y,Z
of
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, there is a bijection

\hom(Z\Box X,Y)\cong\hom(Z,\hom(X,Y))

natural in both
Y
and
Z
. The correspondence
f\mapsto\overline{f}
displayed below exhibits one such:

\displaystyle f\colon Z\Box X\longrightarrow Y

\displaystyle f\colon\hom(Z,X^{\rm op})^{\rm op}\longrightarrow Y

definition of
Z\Box X

\displaystyle g\colon Y\longrightarrow\hom(Z,X^{\rm op})^{\rm op}

the right adjoint of
f

\displaystyle g^{\rm op}\colon Y^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\hom(Z,X^{\rm op})

the opposite of
g

\displaystyle h\colon Z\longrightarrow\hom(Y^{\rm op},X^{\rm op})

switch
Y^{\rm op}
and
Z

\displaystyle\overline{f}\colon Z\longrightarrow\hom(X,Y)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\hom(Y^{\rm op},X^{\rm op})\cong\hom(X,Y)\qed

The bifunctor
\Box
is symmetric, associative, and has
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
as unit. All told,

Proposition 14.11
.

The triple
(\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg},\Box,{\sf V})
is a symmetric monoidal closed category.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be the monad
(\mathcal{F},{\sf m},{\sf y})
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. As a special case of
Question
14.3
we ask the following:

Question 14.12
.

Is the category
\mathbb{F}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of
\mathbb{F}
-algebras monoidal closed?

15.
Continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebras

As in the previous section, unless otherwise specified, in this section
\mathcal{T}
always denotes a saturated class of weights on
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, and
\mathbb{T}
denotes the corresponding submonad of the presheaf monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

Definition 15.1
.

Suppose
A
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. We say that
A
is continuous if the left adjoint
\colim\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
of
{\sf y}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
has a left adjoint. In other words,
A
is continuous if there exists a string of adjunctions

\wayb\dashv{\colim}\dashv{\sf y}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A.

Suppose
A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. We claim that
\colim\wayb x=x
for each
x\in A
. On the one hand, since

A(x,\colim\wayb x)=\mathcal{T}A(\wayb x,\wayb x)=1,

then
x\sqsubseteq\colim\wayb x
, where
\sqsubseteq
is the underlying order of
A
. On the other hand, since

A(\colim\wayb x,x)=\mathcal{T}A(\wayb x,{\sf y}(x))=A(x,\colim{\sf y}(x))=A(x,x)=1,

then
\colim\wayb x\sqsubseteq x
. Therefore,
\colim\wayb x=x
since
A
is separated.

Example 15.2
.

Consider the saturated class
\mathcal{C}
of Cauchy weights and the monad
\mathbb{C}=(\mathcal{C},{\sf m},{\sf y})
. Then every
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A
is continuous, because every Cauchy weight of
A
is representable, so
{\sf y}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}A
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 15.3
.

For every real-enriched category
A
, the
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}A
is continuous.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is a KZ-monad, for every real-enriched category
A
, there is a string of adjunctions

\mathcal{T}{\sf y}_{A}\dashv{\sf m}_{A}\dashv{\sf y}_{\mathcal{T}A}\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}A,

which shows that
\mathcal{T}A
is continuous. ∎

For a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
A
, the distributor

\mathfrak{t}\coloneqq{\colim}^{*}\searrow{\sf y}_{*}\colon A{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}A

is called the
\mathcal{T}
-below distributor
on
A
.

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}A}
\scriptstyle{{\sf y}_{*}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{t}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}A}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{{\colim}^{*}}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\circ}

Explicitly, for all
x,y\in A
,

\mathfrak{t}(y,x)=\inf_{\phi\in\mathcal{T}A}(A(x,\colim\phi)\rightarrow\phi(y)).

Putting
\phi={\sf y}(x)
gives that
\mathfrak{t}(y,x)\leq A(y,x)
.

Suppose
X,Y
are real-enriched categories. For each functor
f\colon Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
, the distributor

X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y,\quad(x,y)\mapsto f(y)(x)

is called the
transpose
of
f
. In particular, the transpose of the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is the identity distributor
X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}X
. Conversely, for each distributor
\phi\colon X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
, the assignment
y\mapsto\phi(-,y)
defines a functor
Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
, called the
transpose
of
\phi
. Taking transpose defines a natural bijection between functors
Y\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
and distributors
X{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}Y
.

Proposition 15.4
.

Suppose
A
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. Then
A
is continuous if and only if for all
x\in A
, the weight
\mathfrak{t}(-,x)
belongs to
\mathcal{T}A
and has
x
as colimit. In this case, the left adjoint
\wayb\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
and the
\mathcal{T}
-below distributor
\mathfrak{t}\colon A{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}A
are transpose of each other.

Proof.

We prove the necessity first. Given a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
A
, we show that for each
x\in A
,
\mathfrak{t}(-,x)=\wayb x
, so
\mathfrak{t}(-,x)
belongs to
\mathcal{T}A
and has
x
as colimit. On the one hand, since
\colim\wayb x=x
, then

\mathfrak{t}(-,x)\leq A(x,\colim\wayb x)\rightarrow\wayb x=\wayb x.

On the other hand, since for all
\phi\in\mathcal{T}A
we have

A(x,\colim\phi)\with\wayb x=\mathcal{T}A(\wayb x,\phi)\with\wayb x\leq\phi,

it follows that
\wayb x\leq A(x,\colim\phi)\rightarrow\phi
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{T}A
, hence
\wayb x\leq\mathfrak{t}(-,x)
.

As for the sufficiency, we show that the assignment
x\mapsto\wayb x\coloneqq\mathfrak{t}(-,x)
defines a left adjoint of
\colim\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
. Let
x\in A
and
\phi\in\mathcal{T}A
. By definition of
\mathfrak{t}(-,x)
, we have
\wayb x\leq A(x,\colim\phi)\rightarrow\phi
, so
A(x,\colim\phi)\leq\mathcal{T}A(\wayb x,\phi)
. Since
\colim
is a functor and
\colim\wayb x=x
, then
\mathcal{T}A(\wayb x,\phi)\leq A(\colim\wayb x,\colim\phi)=A(x,\colim\phi)
. Therefore,
\wayb
is a left adjoint of
\colim
and
A
is continuous. ∎

Proposition 15.5
.

Suppose
A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. Then the
\mathcal{T}
-below distributor
\mathfrak{t}\colon A{\longrightarrow\hskip-13.3472pt{\circ}\hskip 7.3194pt}A
interpolates in the sense that
\mathfrak{t}\circ\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}
.

Proof.

Before proving the conclusion, we would like to note that the conclusion (or for some specific class of weights) has been proved in several places, e.g. Hofmann and Waskiewicz
[
35
,
36
]
, Stubbe
[
78
]
, and Waskiewicz
[
84
]
.

Since
\mathfrak{t}
is the transpose of the left adjoint
\wayb\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
,
it suffices to show that for all
x\in A
,

\wayb x=\sup_{z\in A}\wayb x(z)\with\wayb z.

Since
A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra,
\colim\wayb x=x
for all
x\in A
. Since
\wayb\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
is a left adjoint, it preserves colimits, so
\wayb x
is the colimit of the functor
\wayb\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
weighted by
\wayb x
, i.e.

\wayb x={\colim}_{\wayb x}\wayb.

Since
\mathcal{T}A
is closed under
\mathcal{T}
-colimits in
\mathcal{P}A
, then
{\colim}_{\wayb x}\wayb={\colim}_{\wayb x}(\mathfrak{i}\circ\wayb)
, where
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathcal{T}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is the inclusion functor. Thus, by Corollary
7.11
,

\displaystyle\wayb x

\displaystyle={\colim}_{\wayb x}(\mathfrak{i}\circ\wayb)=\sup_{z\in A}\wayb x(z)\with\wayb z.\qed

Proposition 15.6
.

Every retract of a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. Furthermore, a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
A
is continuous if and only if it is a retract of
\mathcal{T}A
in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Proof.

Suppose
B
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra;
s\colon A\longrightarrow B
and
r\colon B\longrightarrow A
are
\mathbb{T}
-homomorphisms with
r\circ s=1_{A}
. We show that

\wayb_{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}r\circ\wayb_{B}\circ\thinspace s\colon A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}B\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A

is left adjoint to
\colim_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
, so
A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra.

On the one hand,

\displaystyle{\colim}_{A}\circ\wayb_{A}

\displaystyle={\colim}_{A}\circ\mathcal{T}r\circ\wayb_{B}\circ s

\displaystyle=r\circ{\colim}_{B}\circ\wayb_{B}\circ\thinspace s

\displaystyle(\mbox{$r$ is a $\mathbb{T}$-homomorphism})

\displaystyle=r\circ\thinspace s

\displaystyle({\colim}_{B}\circ\wayb_{B}={\rm id}_{B})

\displaystyle=1_{A}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\wayb_{A}\circ\thinspace{\colim}_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}r\circ\wayb_{B}\circ\thinspace s\circ{\colim}_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}r\circ\wayb_{B}\circ\thinspace{\colim}_{B}\circ\mathcal{T}s

\displaystyle(\mbox{$s$ is a $\mathbb{T}$-homomorphism})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{T}r\circ\mathcal{T}s

\displaystyle(\wayb_{B}\dashv{\colim}_{B})

\displaystyle=1_{\mathcal{T}A}.

Thus,
\wayb_{A}
is left adjoint to
\colim_{A}
, as desired.

The second conclusion follows from that
\colim_{A}\circ\wayb_{A}={\rm id}_{A}
for every continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
A
. ∎

Corollary 15.7
.

Suppose
A,B
are separated real-enriched categories,
g\colon A\longrightarrow B
is a right adjoint preserving
\mathcal{T}
-colimits.

(i)

If
g
is surjective and
A
is a
continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra, then so is
B
.

(ii)

If
g
is injective and
B
is
continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra, then so is
A
.

Proof.

(i) Let
f\colon B\longrightarrow A
be the left adjoint of
g
. Then
g\circ f=1_{B}
, so
B
is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra by Proposition
12.10
, hence a retract of
A
in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, and consequently, a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra by the above proposition.

(ii) Let
f\colon B\longrightarrow A
be the left adjoint of
g
. Then
f\circ g=1_{A}
, so
A
is a retract of
B
in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, hence a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. ∎

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a subcategory (not necessarily full) of
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. If

•

for each morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
of
\mathscr{A}
,
\mathcal{T}f\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}B
is a morphism of
\mathscr{A}
; and

•

for each object
A
of
\mathscr{A}
, the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{A}
with codomain restricted to
\mathcal{T}A
is a morphism of
\mathscr{A}
,

then we say that (the restriction of)
\mathcal{T}
is
a class of weights on
\mathscr{A}
.

Let

\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf CCAlg}

be the category of complete and continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebras and functors preserving
\mathcal{T}
-colimits and arbitrary enriched limits.

Proposition 15.8
.

The following are equivalent:

(1)

The restriction of
\mathcal{T}
is a class of weights on the category of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebras.

(2)

For each
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
,
\mathcal{T}A
is a
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra.

(3)

For each
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
,
the inclusion
\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
is a right adjoint.

In this case,
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is a monad in the category
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebras and the forgetful functor
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf CCAlg}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
is monadic.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Obvious.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
By the dual of Corollary
7.9
, it suffices to check that
\mathcal{T}A
is closed in
\mathcal{P}A
with respect to cotensors and meets.
For
r\in[0,1]
and
\phi\in\mathcal{T}A
, let
r\multimap\phi
be the cotensor of
r
and
\phi
in
\mathcal{T}A
. Then for all
x\in A
,

(r\multimap\phi)(x)=\mathcal{T}A({\sf y}(x),r\multimap\phi)=r\rightarrow\mathcal{T}A({\sf y}(x),\phi)=r\rightarrow\phi(x),

thus,
\mathcal{T}A
is closed in
\mathcal{P}A
with respect to cotensors.
Let
\phi
be the meet of a subset
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{T}A
. Since
\mathcal{T}A
is complete, by Proposition
7.4
for all
x\in A
,

\phi(x)=\mathcal{T}A({\sf y}_{A}(x),\phi)=\inf_{i\in I}\mathcal{T}A({\sf y}_{A}(x),\phi_{i})=\inf_{i\in I}\phi_{i}(x),

so
\mathcal{T}A
is closed in
\mathcal{P}A
with respect to meets.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
First, we show that if
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
is a morphism of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, then so is
\mathcal{T}f\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}B
. Since the inclusion
\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
is a right adjoint,
\mathcal{T}A
is a retract of
\mathcal{P}A
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, it is a
\mathbb{P}
-algebra by Proposition
12.10
, then a
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra. Since
\mathcal{T}
, being a subfunctor of
\mathcal{P}
, preserves adjunctions, it follows that
\mathcal{T}f\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}B
is a right adjoint, hence a morphism of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.
Second, we show that for each
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
, the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
is a morphism in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. This follows from that it is right adjoint to the functor
\colim\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
. Therefore, the restriction of
\mathcal{T}
is a class of weights on
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Finally, we show that
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
is a monad in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. It suffices to check that for each object
A
of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, the component
{\sf m}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
of the multiplication
{\sf m}\colon\mathcal{T}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}
at
A
is a morphism of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.
Since
\mathbb{T}
is a KZ-monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, for each real-enriched category
X
,

{\sf m}_{X}={\colim}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}X\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}X

is both a left and a right adjoint.
In particular, for each
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
, the functor
{\sf m}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
is a morphism of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.
∎

By a
lifting
of the monad
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
along the forgetful functor

U\colon\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}

we mean a monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}},\widetilde{{\sf m}},\widetilde{{\sf y}})
in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
such that

\textstyle{[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}}
\textstyle{[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\textstyle{[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\scriptstyle{(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}},\widetilde{{\sf m}},\widetilde{{\sf y}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\textstyle{[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}}
\scriptstyle{U}

U\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}=\mathcal{T}\circ U,~~U\circ\widetilde{{\sf m}}={\sf m}\circ U,~~U\circ\widetilde{{\sf y}}={\sf y}\circ U.

It is clear that such a lifting exists if and only if

•

for each morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
,
\mathcal{T}f\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}B
is a morphism of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
;

•

for each object
A
of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
,
{\sf y}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
is a morphism of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Therefore, such a lifting exists if and only if the restriction of
\mathcal{T}
is a class of weights on
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. In this case the lifting is exactly the restriction of
\mathbb{T}
to the subcategory
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. This implies that the lifting of
\mathbb{T}
along the forgetful functor, when exists, is unique.

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}},\widetilde{{\sf m}},\widetilde{{\sf y}})
is a lifting of
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
along
U\colon\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, the monad in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
defined by the composite adjunction

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg})^{\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg})^{\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\scriptstyle{U}

\dashv

\textstyle{[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}}
\textstyle{[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}}
\scriptstyle{U}

\dashv

is called the composite of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
and
\mathbb{T}
, denoted by
\mathbb{T}\circ\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
, where
(\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg})^{\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}}
denotes the Eilenberg-Moore category of the monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
.

It is known in category theory, see e.g.
[
33
, II.3.8]
, that a lifting of the monad
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
along the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
determines, and is determined by, a unique distributive law of the monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger},{\sf m}^{\dagger},{\sf y}^{\dagger})
over the monad
\mathbb{T}=(\mathcal{T},{\sf m},{\sf y})
. A
distributive law
of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
over
\mathbb{T}
is a natural transformation
\delta\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}
satisfying certain conditions, details are omitted here. Since the lifting of
\mathbb{T}
along the forgetful functor, when exists, is unique, there is at most one distributive law of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
over
\mathbb{T}
, it is safe for us to say that “
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over
\mathbb{T}
”.

From Proposition
15.8
the following conclusion follows immediately.

Proposition 15.9
.

The following are equivalent:

(1)

The copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over
\mathbb{T}
.

(2)

The monad
\mathbb{T}
in
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
has a lifting to
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

(3)

For every
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
,
\mathcal{T}A
is a complete real-enriched category.

(4)

For every
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
, the inclusion
\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
is a right adjoint.

(5)

The restriction of
\mathcal{T}
is a class of weights on
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Example 15.10
.

(Stubbe
[
80
]
) The copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger},{\sf m}^{\dagger},{\sf y}^{\dagger})
distributes over the presheaf monad
\mathbb{P}=(\mathcal{P},{\sf m},{\sf y})
. This follows directly from the above proposition and the fact that for each real-enriched category
X
, the real-enriched category
\mathcal{P}X
is complete.

Proposition 15.11
.

If the copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over
\mathbb{T}
, then for each separated real-enriched category
A
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

A
is a complete and continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra.

(2)

A
is an algebra of
\mathbb{T}
when
\mathbb{T}
is viewed as a monad in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

(3)

A
is an algebra of the composite monad
\mathbb{T}\circ\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
; that means, there is map
h\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}A\longrightarrow A
such that
(A,h)
is an algebra of the monad
\mathbb{T}\circ\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We check
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
first. Let
A
be a complete and continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. Then
\mathcal{T}A
is an object of
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
by Proposition
15.9
. So, the string of adjunctions

\wayb\dashv\colim\dashv{\sf y}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A

ensures that
\colim\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
is a morphism in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
and consequently,
A
is an algebra for
\mathbb{T}
, viewed as a monad in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. Conversely, suppose
A
is an algebra for
\mathbb{T}
, viewed as a monad in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. Then
{\sf y}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
has a left adjoint
\colim\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
which is also a morphism in
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
. That means,
\colim
has a left adjoint. Hence
A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra.

The equivalence
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
is a special case of a general result in category theory, see e.g.
[
33
, II.3.8.4]
, which says that the Eilenberg-Moore category of the lifting
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
is isomorphic to that of the composite monad
\mathbb{T}\circ\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
.
∎

Corollary 15.12
.

If the copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over
\mathbb{T}
, then the category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf CCAlg}
is monadic over
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
.

Question 15.13
.

Is the full subcategory of
\mathbb{T}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
composed of complete and continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebras monoidal closed?

16.
Completely distributive real-enriched categories

Definition 16.1
.

A real-enriched category is said to be completely distributive if it is a continuous
\mathbb{P}
-algebra.

Completely distributive real-enriched categories are clearly analog of completely distributive lattices in the enriched context. In Stubbe
[
78
]
they are said to be
totally continuous
, and they are a special case of completely (totally) distributive categories in the sense of Marmolejo, Rosebrugh and Wood
[
62
]
.
As a special case of Proposition
15.3
, we obtain that for every real-enriched category
X
, the real-enriched category
\mathcal{P}X
is completely distributive. In particular,
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is completely distributive.

Proposition 16.2
.

A
\mathbb{P}
-algebra is completely distributive if and only if it is a retract of some power of
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
.

Proof.

For each set
X
, the power
{\sf V}^{X}
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}X
when
X
is viewed as a discrete real-enriched category. Then sufficiency follows from propositions
15.3
and
15.6
. Necessity follows from that each completely distributive real-enriched
A
is a retract of
\mathcal{P}A
and that
\mathcal{P}A
is a retract of
\mathcal{P}|A|
, where
|A|
is the discrete real-enriched category with the same objects as those of
A
. ∎

Proposition
2.29
says that for complete lattices, the notion of complete distributivity is self-dual. But, this is in general not true in the enriched context.

Theorem 16.3
.

(Lai and Shen
[
47
]
)
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The continuous t-norm
\&
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm.

(2)

If a real-enriched category
X
is completely distributive, then so is
X^{\rm op}
.

(3)

The real-enriched category
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is completely distributive.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
First, since
\with
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm, it is readily verified that for each real-enriched category
X
, the map

\neg\colon(\mathcal{P}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(X^{\rm op}),\quad\phi\mapsto\phi\rightarrow 0

is an isomorphism of real-enriched categories. Next, let
X
be a completely distributive real-enriched category. Then the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}\colon X\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}X
is a right adjoint and its left adjoint
\colim\colon\mathcal{P}X\longrightarrow X
is also a right adjoint. The left adjoints

\colim\circ\neg\colon\mathcal{P}(X^{\rm op})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{P}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow X^{\rm op}

and

\neg\circ{\sf y}\colon X^{\rm op}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{P}X)^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(X^{\rm op})

exhibit
X^{\rm op}
as a retract of
\mathcal{P}(X^{\rm op})
in
\mathbb{P}\text{-}{\bf Alg}
, so by Proposition
15.3
and Proposition
15.6
,
X^{\rm op}
is completely distributive.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
Trivial, since
{\sf V}=([0,1],\alpha_{L})
is completely distributive.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
Since
{\sf V}^{\rm op}
is completely distributive, the functor

{\colim}_{{\sf V}^{\rm op}}\colon\mathcal{P}({\sf V}^{\rm op})\longrightarrow{\sf V}^{\rm op}

is a right adjoint. Since
\mathcal{P}({\sf V}^{\rm op})=(\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf V})^{\rm op}
, it follows that

{\lim}_{\sf V}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf V}\longrightarrow{\sf V}

is a left adjoint. So the functor
{\lim}_{\sf V}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf V}\longrightarrow{\sf V}
preserves tensors. Since the tensor of
r
with (the constant coweight)
0_{\sf V}
in
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}{\sf V}
is given by

(r\multimap 0_{\sf V})(x)=r\rightarrow 0

for all
x\in[0,1]
, then for all
r\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=r\with{\lim}_{\sf V}\thinspace 0_{\sf V}

\displaystyle({\lim}_{\sf V}\thinspace 0_{\sf V}=1)

\displaystyle={\lim}_{\sf V}(r\multimap 0_{\sf V})

\displaystyle(\text{${\lim}_{\sf V}$ preserves tensor})

\displaystyle=\inf_{x\in[0,1]}((r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow x)

\displaystyle=(r\rightarrow 0)\rightarrow 0

which shows that
\with
is isomorphic to the Łukasiewicz t-norm. ∎

Since every
\mathbb{P}
-algebra is a
\mathbb{T}
-algebra, it is natural to ask whether every completely distributive real-enriched category (i.e. every continuous
\mathbb{P}
-algebra) is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. The answer depends on whether the copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over the monad
\mathbb{T}
.

Theorem 16.4
.

(Lai and Zhang
[
51
]
)
The following are equivalent:

(1)

Every completely distributive real-enriched category is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra.

(2)

For every real-enriched category
A
,
\mathcal{P}A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra.

(3)

The copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Trivial, since
\mathcal{P}A
is completely distributive.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
By Proposition
15.9
, it suffices to show that for each
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
-algebra
A
, the inclusion functor
\mathfrak{i}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
is a right adjoint.

Since
\mathcal{P}A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra, there is a string of adjunctions

\wayb\dashv{\colim}\dashv{\sf y}_{\mathcal{P}A}\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A.

Since the Yoneda embedding
{\sf y}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
has a left adjoint
{\colim}_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow A
and any 2-functor preserves adjunctions,
\mathcal{T}{\colim}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
is left adjoint to
\mathcal{T}{\sf y}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A
. Thus,

\mathcal{T}{\colim}_{A}\circ\wayb\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A

is left adjoint to

\colim\circ\mathcal{T}{\sf y}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A.

Since
{\sf y}_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
is fully faithful, by Proposition
5.10
and Example
6.5
we have
1_{\mathcal{P}A}={\sf y}_{A}^{-1}\circ({\sf y}_{A})_{\exists}=\colim_{\mathcal{P}A}\circ\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{A}
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is a subfunctor of
\mathcal{P}
, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}A}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}{\sf y}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}A}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{i}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}{\sf y}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}A}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{i}_{\mathcal{P}A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}A}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle{\colim}_{\mathcal{P}A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}A}
\scriptstyle{1_{\mathcal{P}A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}A}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}A}
\scriptstyle{\colim}

is commutative.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{i}_{A}\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
, being equal to
\colim\circ\mathcal{T}{\sf y}_{A}
, is a right adjoint of
\mathcal{T}{\colim}_{A}\circ\wayb\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}A
.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose
A
is a completely distributive real-enriched category. Since
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over
\mathcal{T}
, by Proposition
15.9
the inclusion
\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
has a left adjoint. The composite of the left adjoint of
{\colim}_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A\longrightarrow A
with the left adjoint of the inclusion
\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}A
is then a left adjoint of
\colim\colon\mathcal{T}A\longrightarrow A
, so
A
is a continuous
\mathbb{T}
-algebra. ∎

We write

[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}

for the category of completely distributive real-enriched categories and functors preserving arbitrary enriched limits and enriched colimits. The category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}
is clearly an analog in the enriched context of that of completely distributive lattices and maps preserving arbitrary meets and joins.

Proposition 16.5
.

(Stubbe
[
80
]
)
The category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}
is monadic over the category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
of real-enriched categories.

Proof.

Since the copresheaf monad
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
distributes over the presheaf monad
\mathbb{P}
(Example
15.10
), we have a composite monad
\mathbb{P}\circ\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
. Since the Eilenberg-Moore category of
\mathbb{P}\circ\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
is the Eilenberg-Moore category of the lifting of
\mathbb{P}
along the forgetful functor
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
, which is
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}
, it follows that
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}
is monadic over
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf Cat}
. ∎

Let
U\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
be the forgetful funcor, which is equal to the composite of forgetful funcors
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}\text{-}{\bf Alg}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
. Then
U
has a left adjoint, say
F\colon{\bf Set}\longrightarrow[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}
.
With a slight improvement the argument of Theorem
17.30
in next section can be applied to show that the forgetful functor
U\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
is monadic.

Proposition 16.6
.

(Pu and Zhang
[
67
]
)
The category
[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}
is monadic over the category of sets.

The monad induced by the adjunction
F\dashv U\colon[0,1]\text{-}{\bf CDL}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}
turns out to be a submonad of the double contravariant fuzzy powerset monad
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
in the category of sets (see Section
13
).
To see this, we present a lemma first.

Lemma 16.7
.

Let

\mathscr{G}\colon{\bf Set}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}

be a subfunctor of the double contravariant fuzzy powerset functor
\mathscr{E}
. Then
\mathscr{G}
is a submonad of of the double contravariant fuzzy powerset monad
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
if, and only if, it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

For each set
X
and each
x\in X
, the function

[0,1]^{X}\longrightarrow[0,1],\quad\lambda\mapsto\lambda(x)

belongs to
\mathscr{G}X
.

(ii)

For each
\mathcal{H}\colon[0,1]^{\mathscr{G}X}\longrightarrow[0,1]
of
\mathscr{G}^{2}X
, the function

[0,1]^{X}\longrightarrow[0,1],\quad\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\lambda}|\mathscr{G}X)

belongs to
\mathscr{G}X
, where
\widehat{\lambda}\colon\mathscr{E}X\longrightarrow[0,1]
sends each
\Lambda\in\mathscr{E}X
to
\Lambda(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{i}\colon\mathscr{G}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}
be the inclusion natural transformation. Then, condition (i) is equivalent to that the unit
{\sf e}
of the monad
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
factors through
\mathfrak{i}
; condition (ii) is equivalent to that
\mathscr{G}
is closed under the multiplication, since for each
\mathcal{H}\colon[0,1]^{\mathscr{G}X}\longrightarrow[0,1]
of
\mathscr{G}^{2}X
and each
\lambda\in[0,1]^{X}
,

{\sf m}_{X}\circ(\mathfrak{i}*\mathfrak{i})_{X}(\mathcal{H})(\lambda)=\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\lambda}|\mathscr{G}X).

This proves the conclusion.
∎

For each set
X
let

\mathscr{C}X

be the set of functions
\Lambda\colon[0,1]^{X}\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that
\Lambda(\mu)\with\sub_{X}(\lambda,\mu)\leq\Lambda(\lambda)
for all
\lambda,\mu\in[0,1]^{X}
. In other words,
\mathscr{C}X=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}X
with
X
viewed as a discrete real-enriched category. The assignment
X\mapsto\mathscr{C}X
defines a subfunctor

\mathscr{C}\colon{\bf Set}\longrightarrow{\bf Set}

of the double contravariant fuzzy powerset functor which satisfies the conditions in Lemma
16.7
, so
\mathscr{C}X
is a submonad of
(\mathscr{E},{\sf m},{\sf e})
. It is readily verified that
(\mathscr{C},{\sf m},{\sf e})
is the monad defined by the adjunction
